# Spiritual Ethos

## Introduction to the Book

This is a work of scholarship, and at its close a work of translation. Reza Shah-Kazemi published it in 2006, reading the Shii and Sufi traditions of Islam together and drawing on sources that reach back to the seventh century. Its subject is the spirituality of Ali ibn Abi Talib — cousin and son-in-law of the Prophet Muhammad, fourth caliph, and first of the Shii Imams — and its argument is that in this one figure the inward substance of the Islamic tradition finds an integral expression.

The book moves inward. It opens with Ali's nearness to the Prophet and the household around him, then turns to what he understood by intellect as distinct from mere reason, and to the certainty that intellect yields. From there it follows his conception of justice — sacred rather than merely legal — through the ordering of society, the claims of the poor, and the sincerity that must underlie every act of rule. Its final movement is devotional: prayer, the remembrance of God, and the extinction of the self in the divine Name.

The closing chapters set commentary aside and let Ali speak for himself. The first sermon of the Nahj al-Balagha and his letter to the governor Malik al-Ashtar are given in full translation, so that the reader may weigh the man against his own words.

## 1. In the Prophet's Shadow

In relation to the Prophet, Ali (عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ) was both a living presence and a promise. The epigraph names it: "I am the city of knowledge and Ali is its gate; so whoever desires knowledge, let him enter the gate" (أَنَا مَدِينَةُ الْعِلْمِ وَعَلِيٌّ بَابُهَا، فَمَنْ أَرَادَ الْعِلْمَ فَلْيَأْتِ الْبَابَ).

To speak of Ali ibn Abi Talib — cousin and son-in-law of the Prophet Muhammad, fourth caliph of Islam, and first in the line of Shii Imams — is to speak of the very heart of Islamic spiritual life. In this central figure of Islam's earliest days, both of its two great sources come together: the revelation of the Quran and the inspired Sunna of the Prophet. Sunna here does not mean simply copying the Prophet's outward actions — a narrow view that is all too common today — but rather the inner spiritual substance of prophetic perfection, the perfection to which the Quran itself points:

> وَإِنَّكَ لَعَلَىٰ خُلُقٍ عَظِيمٍۢ
>
> "Verily, thou art of a tremendous nature" (Al-Qalam: 4).

The Prophet's words and deeds give voice to that spiritual substance, yet they do not exhaust it. To take this substance inwardly into oneself, rather than merely copy its outward forms, is the aim of every Muslim drawn to the spiritual life. Between the believer's soul and the prophetic nature there is a deep kinship, a mysterious closeness that reaches across time and space, as the following verse expresses:

> ٱلنَّبِىُّ أَوْلَىٰ بِٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ مِنْ أَنفُسِهِمْ ۖ وَأَزْوَجُهُۥٓ أُمَّهَتُهُمْ ۗ وَأُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْأَرْحَامِ بَعْضُهُمْ أَوْلَىٰ بِبَعْضٍۢ فِى كِتَبِ ٱللَّهِ مِنَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُهَجِرِينَ إِلَّآ أَن تَفْعَلُوٓا۟ إِلَىٰٓ أَوْلِيَآئِكُم مَّعْرُوفًۭا ۚ كَانَ ذَلِكَ فِى ٱلْكِتَبِ مَسْطُورًۭا
>
> "The Prophet is closer to the believers than they are to themselves, and his wives are their mothers; and by the ordinance of God, blood relatives are more entitled to one another than other believers and emigrants, unless you would show kindness to your close associates. This is decreed in the Record" (Al-Ahzab: 6).

The Prophet thus shows outwardly the very perfection that the spiritually sensitive believer senses, in some measure, at the core of his or her own soul.

It is no surprise, then, to find the Prophet saying that Ali "is as my own soul":

> عليٌّ مِنِّي بِمَنْزِلَةِ نَفْسِي

This confirms not only the extraordinarily close bond between Ali and the Prophet, but also the kinship — and in the end the oneness — between every sanctified soul and the prophetic nature. More mysterious still, the Prophet said to Ali, "You are from me, and I am from you":

> أنْتَ مِنِّی وَ أَنَا مِنْکَ

As for how fully Ali took into himself the substance (مَوَادّ) of the Quran, there is this saying of the Prophet:

> إِنَّ عَلِيًّا مَعَ الْقُرْآنِ وَالْقُرْآنُ مَعَ عَلِيٍّ، لَنْ يَفْتَرِقَا حَتَّى يَرِدَا عَلَيَّ الْحَوْضَ
>
> "Ali is with the Quran and the Quran is with Ali. They will not separate from each other until they return to me at the [paradisal] pool (al-hawd)."

Ali thus took fully within himself both the spirit of the Quran and the soul of the Prophet; and it is exactly this taking-within of the two sources of the Islamic revelation that forms the spiritual path of Islam.

The chief way to study and reflect upon the spirituality of Ali is to engage with the body of teachings handed down under his name. Not every one of the thousands of sayings attributed to him can be assigned to him with confidence; yet the sheer size of this body of work shows that he must have given voice to a great many profound teachings. No other companion of the Prophet left anything close to it. The same holds for the sciences. His founding role in a whole range of them may be open to debate — among them jurisprudence (فِقْهٌ), theology (كَلَامٌ), Quranic exegesis (تَفْسِيرٌ), rhetoric (بَلَاغَةٌ), grammar (نَحْوٌ), and calligraphy (خَطٌّ), the mystical knowledge of Sufism, and such hidden sciences as numerology (جَفْرٌ) and alchemy (اَلْكِيمِيَاءُ) — but the later masters in every one of these fields look back to him as the first impulse behind their discipline, which itself testifies to how far and how deep the influence of both his formal teaching and his personal radiance reached.

Because the Islamic intellectual sciences as a whole are so closely bound to Ali, the Prophet's description of him as the "gate" to prophetic wisdom, quoted in the epigraph, reads in two ways at once: as an account of what Ali was to the Prophet in their own lifetime, and as a prophecy of the part he would play as those sciences later unfolded. Each of these sciences is understood as one more outward, formal expression of the inward, essential spirit of the Islamic revelation — a spirit that Ali drew together within himself and faithfully passed on. The main historical outlines of his life come first.

Ali was a "follower" of the Prophet in the deepest sense of that word. He lived at the Prophet's side, in his very shadow, and took in spiritually all that shone out from him. The closeness between them is caught in the words of one of the Imam's own sermons:

> لَمَّا كُنْتُ وَلِيدًا ضَمَّنِي إِلَى صَدْرِهِ، فَكُنْتُ أتَّبِعُهُ اتّبَاعَ الفَصِيلِ أُمَّهُ
>
> "When I was but a child he took me under his wing. I would follow him as a baby camel follows its mother."

> يَرْفَعُ لِي فِي كُلِّ يَوْمٍ عَلَمًا مِنْ أَخْلاقِهِ الْكَرِيمَةِ وَيَأمُرُنِي بِالاِقْتِدَاءِ بِهِ
>
> "Every day he would hold up for me a sign of his noble character and command me to follow it."

> وَكَانَ يَتَعَاهَدُ غَارَ حِرَاءٍ فِي كُلِّ سَنَةٍ، فَأَرَاهُ وَلَا يَرَاهُ غَيْرِي
>
> "Each year he would go into seclusion at the mountain of Hira. I saw him there, and no one else did."

> وَلَمْ يَجْمَعْ بَيْتٌ وَاحِدٌ يَوْمَئِذٍ فِي الإِسْلامِ غَيْرَ رَسُولِ اللهِ صَلَّى اللهُ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلَّمَ وَخَدِيجَةَ وَأَنَا ثَالِثُهُمَا
>
> "At that time no single household was gathered together in the religion of Islam, save that of the Messenger of God, Khadija, and myself as the third among them."

> أَرَى نُورَ الْوَحْيِ وَالرِّسَالَةِ، وَأَشُمُّ رِيحَ النُّبُوَّةِ
>
> "I saw the light of the revelation and the message, and I smelt the fragrance of prophecy..."

This sketch concerns Ali as the faithful follower who carried on the prophetic pattern. Its focus is the early period of his life, from his birth (c. 599 CE) to the death of the Prophet (11/632). Two later periods fall outside it: the second, from the Prophet's death to Ali's own accession to the caliphate (35/656); and the third, his brief caliphate, torn by tragic civil wars and ended by his assassination in 40/661. These periods raise questions tangled in historical and source-critical difficulty, and to enter those disputes would pull away from the main aim of this book — to present the spiritual ethos of this extraordinary figure in a way that shows how widely it speaks, both within Islam and beyond it. That ethos rises above the boundaries of formal religion, just as it rises above the political quarrels underlying the outward Shii-Sunni divergence — quarrels that only draw attention away from the essential spiritual message of Ali.

What this book brings forward instead is the uniting power of his spiritual teachings — teachings whose great importance and depth Muslims of every school affirm alike. What follows is a brief survey of that earlier period of Ali's life spent with the Prophet, along with the ways the Prophet described him in well-attested sayings; this is the necessary background for understanding his role as the gate to prophetic wisdom. The second part of this opening chapter then takes up some key principles of his spiritual ethos, with special attention to the place of the intellect within it.

By the account of all Shii authorities and of several Sunni sources, Ali held the singular distinction of being born inside the Kaba in Mecca. His mother was Fatima bt. Asad; his father, Abu Talib, son of 'Abd al-Muttalib, was a leading member of the Hashimite clan. Abu Talib had raised the young orphan Muhammad, son of his brother Abd Allah, and would later become his chief protector once Islam was preached openly in Mecca. Some years before the mission began, in a time of drought, Muhammad lightened Abu Talib's burden by taking his young son Ali — then about five years old — into his own household. From that time on, Ali was like a son to him, and the two were seldom apart.

Ali is held to be the first male to embrace Islam, though he was only nine or ten at the time. Abu Bakr was the first grown man to enter the new religion, and Khadija, the Prophet's wife, was the first woman. That the young boy answered the prophetic message at once, and held to it without wavering ever after, carries great weight. About three years later, when revelation instructed the Prophet to "warn thy nearest kin":

> وَأَنذِرْ عَشِيرَتَكَ ٱلْأَقْرَبِينَ

(Ash-Shuara: 214), he invited the leading members of his clan to a feast and then addressed them: "O sons of 'Abd al-Muttalib, I know of no Arab who hath come to his people with a nobler message than mine. I bring you the best of this world and the next. God hath commanded me to call you unto Him. Which of you, then, will help me in this, and be my brother, mine executor and my successor among you?" All remained silent, except for the youthful Ali, who spoke up: "O Prophet of God, I will be thy helper in this." The Prophet then placed his hand on Alis neck and said, "This is my brother, mine executor and my successor among you. Hearken unto him and obey him." Though the clan's leaders met this with mockery at the time, the declaration was taken far more seriously as the years passed and the extraordinary character, nobility, and intelligence of the Prophet's young cousin grew ever more dazzlingly plain.

This early period also holds the part Ali played in the migration (هِجْرَة) from Mecca to Medina (622 CE), the event that marks the start of the Muslim calendar. The Prophet's enemies had plotted to kill him; knowing this, Ali risked his own life by taking the Prophet's place, sleeping in his bed on the night he left. Once in Medina, the Prophet took Ali as his "brother" in the pact of brotherhood he made between the "emigrants" (الْمُهَاجِرُونَ) — the Muslims who had come with him on the hijra from Mecca — and the "helpers" (الْأَنْصَارُ), the Medinan tribes who had embraced the new faith.

During the Medinan years, Ali made his mark on the battlefield, as a scribe of the Quranic revelation as it came down, and as a leading companion of the Prophet. In the battles against the overwhelming forces of the Meccan Quraysh and their allies, his courage and heroism were second to none. He stood in the front rank of nearly every major engagement fought under the Prophet's banner and was often made standard-bearer. He was never beaten in the single-combat duel with which battles customarily opened; indeed, he was never beaten in any battle he fought at all. Both his unbreakable courage and his matchless skill as a warrior became legendary. It is said that at the battle of Uhud a heavenly voice was heard to proclaim, "There is no chivalric knight but Ali, no sword but Dhul-Faqar (لَا فَتَى إِلَّا عَلِيّ، وَلَا سَيْفَ إِلَّا ذُو الْفَقَارِ)," the last being the name of the sword the Prophet had given him. The battle that did most to fix Alis name as the foremost — indeed the unconquerable — warrior of Islam was Khaybar, in 7/629. The Muslims had been able to make no headway against the heavily fortified defenses of their foes, which seemed impossible to breach. The Prophet then declared that he would hand the army's banner to one who "loves God and His Messenger and is loved by God and His Messenger," and that through him victory would be granted. He sent for Ali, who was away on account of an ailment of the eyes. The Prophet healed him by placing saliva on his eyes, and Ali went on to lead the Muslims to victory. In the thick of the fighting, having lost his own shield, he is said to have torn a gate from its hinges and used it as a shield — a gate that, after the battle, eight men could barely lift.

## 2. The People of the House and the Prophet's Witness

Ali (ع)'s standing as a warrior is only one of many things that explain his unique place in the Islamic tradition. Far more important is how close he was, in every respect, to the Prophet, and the wisdom and sanctity that flowed from that closeness. Ali received the immense honor of marrying the Prophet's daughter Fatima. She and her mother Khadija, the Prophet's first wife, are regarded as the most saintly women in Islam. One of the epithets the tradition bestows on Fatima is Majma' al-Nurayn, 'Confluence of the Two Lights', the two lights of nubuwwa (prophecy) and وَلَايَة (sanctity). After the Prophet himself, Ali was thus the head of what became known as the Ahl al-Bayt, 'the People of the House', that is, the Prophet's family.

The Ahl al-Bayt are addressed in an important Quranic verse as having been purified of all defilement:

> وَقَرْنَ فِى بُيُوتِكُنَّ وَلَا تَبَرَّجْنَ تَبَرُّجَ ٱلْجَهِلِيَّةِ ٱلْأُولَىٰۖ وَأَقِمْنَ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ وَآتِينَ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ وَأَطِعْنَ ٱللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُۚۥٓ إِنَّمَا يُرِيدُ ٱللَّهُ لِيُذْهِبَ عَنكُمُ ٱلرِّجْسَ أَهْلَ ٱلْبَيْتِ وَيُطَهِّرَكُمْ تَطْهِيرًۭا
>
> 'God only wisheth to remove from you all impurity, O People of the House, and to purify you with a complete purification' (Al-Ahzab: 33).

According to several of the most authoritative early sources, the Prophet called for Fatima, Ali, and their two sons Hasan and Husayn, and declared, 'This is my Ahl al-Bayt.' On one well-attested occasion the Prophet gathered these members of his family beneath his cloak and recited the words of this verse. This happened when a Christian delegation from Najran was engaged in religious debate with the Prophet, and a revelation came calling for a challenge of mutual imprecation (مُبَاهَلَة) against the Christians. The Prophet brought forward the four persons named above for this مُبَاهَلَة, but the Christians declined the challenge.

The importance of the Ahl al-Bayt is further expressed in a verse in which the Prophet is instructed:

> ذَلِكَ ٱلَّذِى يُبَشِّرُ ٱللَّهُ عِبَادَهُ ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ ۗ قُل لَّآ أَسْـَٔلُكُمْ عَلَيْهِ أَجْرًا إِلَّا ٱلْمَوَدَّةَ فِى ٱلْقُرْبَىٰ ۗ وَمَن يَقْتَرِفْ حَسَنَةًۭ نَّزِدْ لَهُۥ فِيهَا حُسْنًا ۚ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ غَفُورٌۭ شَكُورٌ
>
> 'Say: I ask you for no reward, save love of the near of kin' (Ash-Shura: 23).

Several of the most significant Quranic commentators identify 'the near of kin' in this verse as the Prophet's Ahl al-Bayt. The revelation thus explicitly demands love of the Prophet's family. This does not exclude the verse's wider meaning, which takes in love for all those bound to a person by close kinship. How deeply all Muslims are to revere the Prophet's family is shown further by another practice: blessings are to be called down upon his al, or progeny, as part of the blessings called down upon him. The Quran instructs the believers:

> إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ وَمَلَٓئِكَتَهُۥ يُصَلُّونَ عَلَى ٱلنَّبِىِّۚ يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ صَلُّوا۟ عَلَيْهِ وَسَلِّمُوا۟ تَسْلِيمًا
>
> 'Truly, God and His angels bless the Prophet. O ye who believe, bless him and greet him with peace' (Al-Ahzab: 56).

When this verse was revealed, the Prophet was asked how one should perform this blessing, and he replied that blessings were to be invoked upon him and his al as follows: 'O God, bless Muhammad and the progeny of Muhammad, as Thou hast blessed Abraham and the progeny of Abraham. Truly, Thou art the Praised, the Glorious ...' This blessing is recited during the canonical prayer. Its importance, together with the obligation to love the Prophet's family, is beautifully expressed in the following lines by Imam al-Shafii, founder of one of the four schools of Sunni jurisprudence:

Loving you, O family of the household of the Prophet of God, is an obligation (فَرْضٌ) from God in the Quran which He revealed; Sufficient to show the grandeur of your dignity is that one who blesses you not, no prayer has he.

There is also a prophetic saying that underscores the spiritual significance of the Ahl al-Bayt:

Truly, I am leaving behind among you the two weighty things (اَلثَّقَلَيْنِ): the Book of God and my Ahl al-Bayt. They will not be parted from each other until they return to me at the [paradisal pool] الْحَوْضَ.

Beyond these sayings, which concern Ali as the most important member of the Prophet's Ahl al-Bayt, there are sayings of the Prophet that concern Ali specifically, and these deserve to be cited in full. Only on the basis of these sayings can one begin to appreciate the immensely important role of Ali in the spiritual dimension of the Islamic faith. These traditions (ahadith, sing. hadith) are presented here as a list with minimal commentary, following the manner in which they appear in most standard hadith collections. Anyone who takes prophetic guidance seriously cannot but take Ali seriously, and it is largely for this reason that countless Muslims across the centuries have meditated on these sayings, drawing from them diverse interpretations and incalculable inspiration. In a certain sense, these sayings form the prism through which the tradition views Ali, and they deepen one's spiritual receptivity to his teachings. The crucial importance of Ali in the spiritual firmament of nascent Islam emerges more clearly in the light of these prophetic descriptions. They should therefore be regarded as a significant part of the 'prophetic background' to Ali 's seminal contribution to Islamic spiritual and intellectual life, whether in a Shii, Sunni, or Sufi context.

The traditions given below can be found in any standard Shii hadith collection. To show that they — together with their implications and the devotion they have inspired — are not confined to Shii Islam, they are cited here from two of the most authoritative Sunni traditionists. The first is Ahmad al-Nasai, author of the Sunan, one of the six collections deemed 'canonical' in the Sunni corpus (اَلْكُتُبُ السِّتَّةُ). The second is al-Hakim al-Nisaburi, author of al-Mustadrak 'alal-sahihayn (Supplement to the Two Sound Collections). This work gathers those prophetic traditions that meet the exacting criteria of authenticity established by Bukhari and Muslim, authors of the sahihayn, but were not included in their own collections. These sayings speak of Ali s distinctions, virtues, and merits, all conveyed by the Arabic word fadail (sing. fadila). They explain why Ahmad b. Hanbal, another leading Sunni traditionist and founder of one of the four Sunni schools of law, made the following statement: No companion of the Prophet has had such fadail ascribed to him as those which have been ascribed to Ali ibn Abi Talib.'

The sayings are these:

'Truly, Ali is from me and I am from him (إِنَّ عَلِيًّا مِنِّي وَأَنَا مِنْهُ), and he is the wali (patron/spiritual master) of every believer after me.'

'Ali is with the Quran and the Quran is with Ali, إِنَّ عَلِيًّا مَعَ الْقُرْآنِ وَالْقُرْآنُ مَعَ عَلِيٍّ، لَنْ يَفْتَرِقَا حَتَّى يَرِدَا عَلَيَّ الْحَوْضَ. They will not separate from each other until they return to me at the [paradisal pool] (الْحَوْضَ).'

'Three things were revealed to me regarding Ali: he is the leader of the Muslims, the guide of the pious, and chief of the radiantly devout (سَيِّدُ الْمُسْلِمِينَ، إِمَامُ الْمُتَّقِينَ، وَقَائِدُ الْغُرِّ الْمُحَجَّلِينَ).'

'Gazing upon Ali is an act of worship (النَّظَرُ إِلَى عَلِيٍّ عِبَادَةٌ).'

'May God have mercy on Ali. O God, make the truth revolve around Ali wherever he turns (أَدِرِ الْحَقَّ مَعَهُ حَيْثُ دَارَ).'

'I am the city of knowledge and Ali is its gate; so whoever desires knowledge, let him enter the gate.'

'O Ali, you are a leader (سَيِّدٌ) in this world and the Hereafter. Your beloved is my beloved, and my beloved is the beloved of God; your enemy is my enemy, and my enemy is the enemy of God. Woe to those who hate you after me [after I have passed away].'

'Whoever desires to live my life and to die my death and to take his rest in the eternal Garden my Lord has promised me, let him orient himself toward Ali ibn Abi Talib, for truly he will never lead you away from right guidance, nor lead you into error.'

The Prophet said that Ali was 'as my own soul' (عليٌّ مِنِّي بِمَنْزِلَةِ نَفْسِي).

He said to Ali, 'You are from me and I am from you, أنْتَ مِنِّی وَ أِنَا مِنْکَ.'

... whoever obeys Ali obeys me, and whoever disobeys him disobeys me.

'You will clarify for my community that over which they will differ after me (أَنْتَ تُبَيِّنُ لِأُمَّتِي مَا اخْتَلَفُوا فِيهِ مِنْ بَعْدِي).'

'There is one among you who will fight for the تَأْوِيل [spiritual interpretation] of the Quran as I have fought for its tanzil [literal revelation], عَلِیٌّ یُقَاتِلُ عَلیٰ تَاوِیلِہِ کَما قَاتَلْتُ عَلیٰ تَنزِیلِہِ.' Abu Bakr asked, 'Is it I?' The Prophet said, 'No.' 'Umar asked, Is it I?' The Prophet said, 'No, it is the one who is mending the sandal.' The Prophet had given Ali his sandal to mend.

'O Ali, there is in you something akin to Jesus, on whom be peace and blessings. The Jews hated him so intensely that they slandered his mother; and the Christians loved him so intensely that they ascribed to him a rank he did not possess.' 'O Ali, whoever separates himself from me separates himself from God, and whoever separates himself from you, O Ali, separates himself from me.'

'Whoever curses Ali curses me, and whoever curses me curses God.'

The Prophet said to his wife 'Aisha, Call unto me the leader (سَيِّدٌ) of the Arabs.' She asked, 'O Prophet of God, are you not the leader of the Arabs?' He said, 'I am the leader of the children of Adam, and Ali is the leader of the Arabs.'

When Fatima was asked by some women why the Prophet had given her in marriage to a man as poor as Ali, she related this to the Prophet. He said: 'Are you not pleased that God, exalted and glorified be He, surveyed the people of the world and chose two men from among them: one is your father, and the other your husband?'

'The first of you to reach the [paradisal] pool (الْحَوْضَ) is the first of you who entered Islam, Ali ibn Abi Talib.'

'Ali is from me and I am from him (عَلِيٌّ مِنِّي وَأَنَا مِنْهُ), and nobody can fulfill my duty but myself and Ali.'

Ali himself relates that the Prophet told him that none but a believer will love him, and none but a hypocrite will hate him.

When the Prophet was about to depart on an expedition to Tabuk, he left Ali as his deputy in Medina. Ali was saddened not to be going with him. The Prophet said, 'Are you not content to stand in relation to me as Aaron stood in relation to Moses, except that there is no prophet after me?'

The Prophet prayed to God to bring 'the most beloved of Thy creatures' to share with him a meal of fowl. Only when Ali arrived did the Prophet invite him to join him.

Among the several Quranic verses on which the Prophet commented with reference to Ali is 13:7: 'Verily thou art a warner, and for every people there is a guide.' The Prophet said, 'I am the warner... you are the guide, O Ali. After me, the rightly-guided shall be guided by you.'

Verse 55 of Sura 5 (al-Maida (اَلْمَائِدَةُ)) reads, 'Verily your wali is only God and His Messenger and those who believe, establish the prayer and give alms while bowing in prayer.' The commentators tie its revelation to the occasion when Ali, while bowing in prayer, extended his ring to a beggar who had asked for alms. On being told of the incident, the Prophet recited this verse, and then added what are perhaps the most celebrated words ever spoken about Ali: 'For whomever I am the mawla [guardian, master, close friend], Ali is his mawla (مَنْ كُنْتُ مَوْلَاهُ فَعَلِيٌّ مَوْلَاهُ).'

This last saying was expressed on a number of occasions, the most famous of which came after the Prophet's final pilgrimage to Mecca, in the year 10/632, at a pool midway between Mecca and Medina known as Ghadir Khumm. The Prophet assembled all the pilgrims, had a pulpit erected, and delivered an address to the thousands gathered there. The address culminated in the declaration: 'For whomever I am the mawla, Ali is his mawla.' For Shiis, this is an unambiguous designation (نَصّ) of Ali as the Prophet's successor; for Sunnis it marks the special closeness of Ali to the Prophet, but not his nomination as successor in the political domain. That the reference to Ali as mawla — in some versions as wali — carries the highest spiritual significance is, however, not seriously disputed. 'Umar is reported to have approached Ali and said, Bakh bakh', an expression of congratulation, 'you have become my mawla and the mawla of every Muslim.' The heated debate that has occupied countless minds throughout history, and continues to this day, turns on the political implications of Ali 's spiritual authority, his وَلَايَة.

Although the place of Ali within Shii Islam is not the subject here, his charisma was such that, even during the Prophet's own lifetime, a group came to be known as the shia, or 'partisans,' of Ali. Al-Tabari, author of the first great commentary on the Quran, reports that the Prophet interpreted the verse:

> إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ أُو۟لَٓئِكَ هُمْ خَيْرُ ٱلْبَرِيَّةِ

'Those who have faith and do righteous deeds, they are the best of created beings' (Al-Bayyinah: 7), as referring to Ali and his 'shia.'

Four individuals in particular were renowned for their devotion to Ali during the Prophet's lifetime and are regarded as the prototypes of early Shiism: Salman al-Farisi, Abu Dharr al-Ghifari, 'Ammar b. Yasir, and Miqdad b. Amr. This early love for Ali should not be equated with the theological doctrines later elaborated in the Shii branch of Islam in the generations that followed. It is nonetheless worth emphasizing that, at the core of Shiism in all its different expressions, lies the notion of fidelity to Ali. This fidelity, loyalty, or love is one aspect of وَلَايَة; the other, complementary aspect is the spiritual authority or sanctity of Ali. It is equally important to stress that neither devotion to Ali nor the spiritual orientation toward the وَلَايَة he embodies belongs to Shiism alone. Rather, it extends across the whole of the Islamic faith, for devotion to Ali as head of the Prophet's Ahl al-Bayt is found throughout the Muslim world and is especially deep wherever Sufism flourishes. Ali is regarded as the first spiritual pole' (قُطْبٌ) of Sufism after the Prophet himself, and he stands at the summit of all the spiritual chains (salasil, sing. silsila) through which the Sufi orders trace their lineage back to the Prophet. To speak of Ali is to speak not of exclusivist sectarianism but of universal spirituality.

## 3. Intellect, Not Reason

The spiritual ethos of Ali (ع) has an intellectual heart. The intellect (الْعَقْل) plays a central part in how his spiritual teachings are worked out and taken in. Sketching the key features of that ethos, with special attention to its intellectual side, gives firmer ground to the explorations that follow. One aim is to bring out something of the 'spirit of the intellect', الرُّوحُ الْعَقْلُ — the phrase coined for the bond between the intellect and the spirit as it appears in the Imam's worldview. This spirit surpasses the workings of the rational mind even as it contains them, and it reaches into areas not usually tied to the intellect today, such as moral conduct and a sense of beauty. This spiritual understanding of the intellect is directly connected to the Quranic idea of the spirit (الرُّوح), and much of what follows examines what comes of engaging deeply with this unifying spirit that belongs to the intellect.

It helps at the outset to explain why the English word 'intellect' is preferred over 'reason' to render 'aql. The word is meant to call up the original sense of intellectus in Latin Christendom, a sense almost identical to that of nous in the Greek of the Church Fathers. Intellectus means understanding', and it holds within it the idea of standing under a thing: to understand something is to stand beneath a perception of reality and let that perception overshadow and govern one's actions, for a person always acts in the shadow of how things are perceived. Intellectus, or nous, is therefore the faculty able to see transcendent realities directly, in contemplation — the very realities of which the Quran speaks but which are hidden from the senses. Reason, which renders the Latin ratio and the Greek dianoia, is by contrast indirect and step by step; it works through logic and reaches only mental concepts of those realities. The root of ratio means a relation or comparison between two numbers, and this catches how reason proceeds: by comparison, by a binary logic of affirming and denying, weighing one thing against another until it has defined what it examines. Reason so understood is the instrument the intellect uses, but it can carry a person only as far as the outward form of a thing, only as far as the mental concept or image of a reality. With the intellect one can contemplate, or 'see', the Absolute; with reason one can only think about it. To know about a reality is one thing; to contemplate and experience it directly is quite another, and it is the intellect, not reason, that is capable of that experience — even though it is through reason that the intellect is brought to it.

These opening reflections on the spirit of the intellect in the Imam's ethos turn chiefly on a short discourse of the greatest importance in the Nahj al-balagha: the one the Imam gave to Kumayl b. Ziyad, among his closest disciples. The method here is that of an interpretive commentary on the Imam's words, drawing not only on his other sayings on the same theme but also on Quranic verses that agree with them and, at times, on traditions of the Prophet that either help clarify the Imam's saying at hand or are themselves clarified by it. Three factors belonging to the spirit of this intellectual outlook must be stressed first: the centrality of revelation; the harmony between revelation and the intellect; and the distinction between the intellect, understood as the principle by which consciousness is articulated, and reason, understood as one expression of that principle.

To begin with the first: everything in the Imam's life and teachings is centered on the Quranic revelation and on its embodiment in the Prophet's conduct. The Quran is the center around which the intellect must turn, for the intellect needs a center, principles, and definitions to hold fast to if it is to be rightly guided. Both the thought and the life of the Imam can be read as a kind of spiritual commentary, a تَأْوِيل, on the sacred text. On the doctrinal plane, it can be said with confidence that Ali had an inborn gift for the work of تَأْوِيل. On the plane of his own life as well, his path — from his birth in the most holy shrine of the Kaba to his martyrdom in the mosque of Kufa — reads as a dramatic unfolding of the inner meaning of the revealed text. Ali 's refusal to stop at the surface of things, whether religious, ethical, or political, showed an unflinching fidelity to the sacred substance of the religion, a fidelity that called for inward intellectual penetration and outward armed resistance alike. The saying of the Prophet cited earlier bears closely on this: Ali would fight for the تَأْوِيل of the Quran just as he, the Prophet, had fought for its tanzil, عَلِیُّ یُقَاتِلُ عَلیٰ تَاوِیلِہ کَما قَتَلُ عَلیَ تَنزِیلِہِ. Ali s short caliphate was dominated by bloody civil wars, and a strong case can be made that all three — the wars of the Camel (اَلْجَمَلُ), of Nahrawan, and of Siffin — were fought for the sake of interpreting and applying the Quran rightly, for the clarification of its central message of تَوْحِيد, and for the realization of that oneness on every level, spiritual and social alike. These were not political battles or mere struggles for power; they were waged to clarify and uphold the oneness of God according to the principles of the Quran — in thought, in speech, and in action. The Imam's response to a bedouin brings this vividly home. Just as the battle of the Camel (اَلْجَمَلُ) was about to begin, the man approached him and said, O Commander of the Faithful (یَا أَمِیرَ الْمُوْمِنِینَ)! Do you say that God is one?' Those present rebuked the bedouin, telling him this was neither the time nor the place for such questions. The Imam, however, intervened: 'Leave him, for surely what the bedouin wishes is what we wish for the people.'

This response bears directly on the principle that right intention comes before outward action, whatever that action may be. Had the Imam been guided by reason alone, he would have sided with those who rebuked the bedouin and told him to find a fitter time and place; but he saw with the intellect that stands above reason. For the Imam, the deepest intention behind every action, whether religious in the ordinary sense or military as here, is knowledge of the true nature of ultimate reality. What the Imam 'wishes for the people', then, is precisely this knowledge, and it must lie at the very heart of all concerns, motives, and actions, whether on the battlefield or the prayer-rug. There is therefore no time or place where the question 'What do you mean by saying God is one?' is beside the point. It was for the sake of true knowledge of the divine, and of all that flows from it in society, that the Imam was fighting, in fulfillment of the prophetic saying just mentioned. On the very field of battle, then, the Imam gives a discourse on the meaning of divine oneness. The first part of his reply holds a key to his whole spiritual ethos. He rejects any notion that God's oneness is numerical, a matter of counting. He tells the bedouin that one may not say 'one' while holding any numerical idea in mind, for 'that which has no second does not enter into the category of numbers'. The moment a thing can be counted as 'one', it at once admits the possibility of 'two', 'three', 'four'; a oneness understood in this numerical sense is thus undone by the very number that would affirm it.

'That which has no second' is not merely a divinity to which no partner can be ascribed, as a theologian might put it, but that reality which has no 'otherness' — the Real, اَلْحَقّ, at once transcendent and immanent, beyond all 'things' and yet made manifest by all 'things', at once out of reach and inseparable from all that is. To say that God has no otherness is not to say that things exist which might have been His partners but which believers simply choose not to associate with Him; it is to affirm that nothing in all of existence bears His likeness, nothing that could be set beside Him. In these few words the Imam works, for those who have 'ears to hear', a shift of awareness: from the doctrinal notion of one God against many gods to the mystical grasp of the one and only Reality, before which everything else is mere appearance. All things come to be seen as outward appearances waiting to be read, as signs (آیات) pointing beyond themselves to That of which they are the manifestations, for an aya is precisely a meaningful token that points beyond itself to another. Tracing these appearances back to their source and origin is the literal meaning of تَأْوِيل: a return to the awwal, 'the First' (عَوْدَۃٌ اِلَی الْاَوَّلِ). On the one hand, the Real is described as the beginning and end of all things, the apparent and the hidden; there is nothing in being but this one Reality: 'He is the First and the Last, the Outward and the Inward' (Al-Hadid: 3).

> هُوَ ٱلْأَوَّلُ وَٱلْآخِرُ وَٱلظَّهِرُ وَٱلْبَاطِنُ ۖ وَهُوَ بِكُلِّ شَىْءٍ عَلِيمٌ

On the other hand, apparent multiplicity is to be 'brought back' to real oneness: 'We shall show them Our signs (سَنُرِيهِمْ آيَتِنَا) on the horizons and in their own souls, so that it be clear to them that He is the Real' (Fussilat: 53).

> سَنُرِيهِمْ آيَتِنَا فِى ٱلأٓفَاقِ وَفِىٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ حَتَّىٰ يَتَبَيَّنَ لَهُمْ أَنَّهُ ٱلْحَقُّ

In one saying attributed to Ali, the Prophet is described as the first whom God taught the science of تَأْوِيل, and who in turn taught it to Ali. According to the Prophet, 'Truly, Ali is with the Quran, and the Quran is with Ali, أَنَّ عَلِیٌّ مَعَ الْقُرْآنِ، وَ قُرْآنٌ مَعَ الْعَلِیِّ. In another famous saying, often quoted by Sufi commentators, Ali claimed that he could load seventy camels with the pages of commentary he could give on the Fatiha alone, the opening chapter of the Quran. Ali s cousin Ibn Abbas, often called the first commentator', likewise claimed to have learned the science of interpretation from Ali.

Closely related is a saying of the Imam of the first importance, which helps explain why he is held in the highest esteem within the discipline of Quranic exegesis, tafsir: 'The Quran consists of a book inscribed, between two covers; it speaks not with a tongue, it cannot do without an interpreter (لَا بُدَّ لَهُ مِنْ تُرْجُمَانٍ).' The mutual dependence of revelation and the intellect is underlined again in the saying, 'The prophet of a man is the interpreter of his intellect (رَسُولُ الرَّجُلِ تَرْجُمَانُ عَقْلِهِ).' Elsewhere the intellect is set in plain correspondence with the 'inner' messenger: the intellect is 'the messenger of the Real'.

The Imam's teachings, which give voice to his spiritual ethos, should therefore be read above all as creative interpretations of the Quranic revelation. They set in motion a movement from the letter of the revelation to its spirit, and from the spirit of the revelation to the spirit of the Revealer — a work of 'retracing', of returning to the source, the origin, the beginning, al-awwal, achieved to the degree that the spirit of the intellect is brought to life within the interpreter. Yet the Revealer stays of necessity hidden even as He is revealed through the revelation, hidden because the essence of what is revealed, the divine Reality in itself, cannot be uttered or put into words; only the forms of its manifestations can be revealed. This play between form and the formless, the evident and the hidden, the revealed and the concealed — such pairs of opposites lie at the root of the paradoxical manner of expression for which the Imam is renowned, and which recurs throughout this book. The relationship that especially concerns the present discussion is the second of the three factors named above: that between revelation and 'intellection', by which is meant the creative use of the intellect. This relationship matters all the more in a setting so largely defined by fidelity to revelation. Where strict rationalists hold intellect and revelation to be mutually exclusive, here they are seen as fully harmonious, standing in creative reciprocity, each needing the other for its completeness, its wholeness, for the 'making one' that is the very essence of تَوْحِيد.

Indeed, one of the most striking features of the Imam's outlook is the way it lays bare the emptiness of many false or overdrawn oppositions that later came to mark, or were forced upon, Islamic thought. This owes much to the deeply synthetic quality of the Imam's discourses, seen as 'pre-philosophical' by some scholars and as 'supra-philosophical' — inspired and evocative — by those within the Islamic spiritual tradition. The seemingly unbridgeable split between intellect and revelation shows up in several opposed forms: reason versus faith, convention (سنۃ) versus nature (طَبِيعَة), imitation (تَقْلِيد) versus verification (تَحْقِيق), piety versus inquiry, and so on. In Ali 's ethos, however, intellect and revelation are not mutually exclusive but complementary, and at the highest level inseparable, since truth is one. The human intellect plainly cannot be equated with divine revelation, yet the spirit that animates the intellect is not, in the end, bound by human limits. It is this spirit that perceives and grasps the divine character of revelation.

The divine spirit that shapes the form of revelation and fills its content must be fathomable, to some degree, by human awareness; without this, revelation would amount to no more than a set of rules and regulations for the will, rather than a source of creative inspiration for the intelligence. It cannot be stressed too strongly that divine revelation, far from curbing the workings of reason and imposing some unintelligible command, on the contrary asks for a creative engagement with all the resources of the intelligence — above all those acts of reflection and meditation for which the Imam himself is renowned, and which the Quran calls for again and again. It is the very opposite of a decree imposed from outside: the Quran constantly demands fikr, deep and sustained reflection, and such reflection does not shut the meaning of the text within its literal surface but opens it out, unfolding the inner horizons of meaning folded within the letter.

For the revealed text is itself silent; it speaks only through its interpreter, the intellect. This understanding of the bond between intellect and revelation shuts out all literalism and shallowness, every attempt to pen meaning within a flat, one-sided reading. How deeply the text's meaning is sounded depends on the depth and penetration of the interpreter's own intellect, for the text carries many levels of meaning at once. At its most profound, this 'prophetic' intellect rejoins the very source of revelation itself; for it is this 'inner prophet', the innermost degree of consciousness, that is the ultimate ground on which a person can confirm the verses of the outer revelation as true — to validate them through lived experience, and not merely to affirm them as dogma. It is in this sense that the Imam says, in the first sermon of the Nahj, that one of the purposes for which God sent forth prophets to mankind was 'to unearth for them the buried treasures of the intellects' (دَفَائِنُ الْعُقُولِ).

This saying is one of the keys to the spiritual ethos under consideration, and it returns again and again throughout these essays. The intellect is not stifled by revelation; it is brought to life by it. Revelation is a clarion call to the spiritual depths of the intellect, cultivating the seeds of consciousness — the seeds of توحید — that God has planted there and that lie buried within us, and bringing them to fruition. For the spirit of one's intellect already carries the initial imprint of its Creator: in the world of the spirit it witnessed its Lord and testified to His lordship, and revelation, the ذکر, does no more than remind it of that primordial testimony. A reminder, after all, is never a new piece of information; it is knowledge one already possesses but has forgotten, for one can only be reminded of what one already knows. We were created in this world in a kind of amnesia. Just as a physician tells the family of a man who has lost his memory to surround him with familiar things, with signs (آیات) out of his past, to jog his memory, so God has set His reminders in the horizons and the souls (آفَاق وَ أَنْفُس). The spirit of one's intellect, then, cannot be alien to what descends as revelation from on high; on the contrary, it must be fundamentally akin to it, and it is precisely for this reason that one can be absolutely certain that the revelation truly comes from the Absolute. The point is not to set up a dichotomy between intellect and revelation, but to see the intellect as revelation, or at least as one mode of revelation: the God-given means by which the individual comes to discern, affirm, and plumb the divine 'revelations' outside himself, both those of scripture and those constituted by the 'signs' (آیات) of the natural world. If the human intellect can perceive and recognize revelation, it is because both are rooted in one and the same ultimate source; we came from a spiritual realm, and so did revelation, sent to remind us of what we once witnessed and have since forgotten. Prophetic revelation awakens and quickens the depths of the human intellect; it does not replace or displace it. The messengers do for these buried seeds what the farmer does when he waters what lies hidden beneath the soil, what the miner does when he digs into the mountain for its gold and silver, and what the diver does when he descends to the sea-bed to bring up its buried treasures. From this perspective the revealed text is not some extraneous source set against the intelligence, but the objective, outward expression of the very principles that the intellect itself yields up to the 'inner prophet' — the intellect serving as the inner messenger whom the outer messenger awakens and nourishes. By 'intellect' is meant not simply the faculty of reasoning but the source of consciousness that articulates the human spirit.

The intellect holds unsuspected depths, buried treasures, and is far from being exhausted by its surface operations — the cognitions and reasonings proper to its purely mental dimension. A third matter now follows: the relationship between intellect and reason. Just as there can be no contradiction whatsoever between revelation and intellection, so too a false dichotomy between reason and intellect must be avoided. Reason should rather be seen as one of the modes of the intellect; reason no more contradicts intellect than intellect contradicts revelation. Reason is a mode of the intellect, but it is not to be equated with it wholesale, just as one cannot reduce the universal objectivity of revelation to the subjective specificities of any one individual's intellection, to his or her own 'private' revelation. To equate reason with intellect would amount to denying the very existence of the 'buried treasures' of the intellect — those supra-rational dimensions that are, at first, buried beneath, and thus hidden from, the rational surface of the intellect. The revelation constituted by the intellect is embedded within the creation of the human being, the intellect being that which most critically defines the human being as such. Far from pitting the intellect against revelation, each is to be seen in the light of the other: the substance of revelation cannot be engaged except through the intellect, on the one hand, and the 'buried treasures' of the intellect cannot be disclosed except through revelation, on the other — a genuine co-dependency of the two.

This understanding of the intellect stands close to what the Imam refers to as the 'heart' (قَلْب), that innermost mode of perception capable of 'seeing' God. It is the 'heart' that can 'see' God — not with eyesight, not with بصارۃ, but with insight, with بصیرۃ, that spiritual insight generated by the 'verities of faith' (حَقَائِقُ الْإِيمَان / حَقَائِقُ الْاِیمَانِ). As the Imam puts it, in response to the questioner asking whether he had 'seen' his Lord: 'Eyes see Him not through sight's observation, but hearts see Him through the verities of faith.'

Between the spiritual depths of the intellect and its rational surface there is both continuity and discontinuity. The continuity shows in an evident fact: all thinking begins with concepts that convey intelligible meaning. Thought sets out from ideas that are rationally discernible, and these must bear some relation to the realities they express, or they collapse into ideas that lead nowhere. But there is discontinuity too between the spiritual and rational aspects of the intellect, in that reason stops short at the ideas it has 'de-fined'. The Arabic word for definition, hadd (حَدّ), expresses — like its English equivalent — both senses at once: that of a conceptual definition and that of a limitation, a boundary, the very boundary that reason draws around an idea by weighing its pros and cons until the idea is fixed and 'defined'. To go beyond the limits imposed by this rational act of definition, something more than reason is needed; and it is this 'something more' that makes the ethos under consideration a spiritual one, and not a rationalistic one. It is certainly 'rational', in the sense that the form of the ethos is indeed open to rational scrutiny; but it is not 'rationalistic', inasmuch as its essence is not predicated on reason alone.

Now Ali frequently hints, in highly allusive terms, at the mysteries and marvels — the unearthed treasures of the intellect — that he has directly witnessed by the eye of his purified heart. He also alludes to this supra-rational mode of awareness indirectly, through a bewildering use of apophatic discourse and paradoxical utterance, of which the corpus attributed to him is full. In this ethos it seems that the higher the truth, the deeper the paradox: the sheer lack of any common measure between reality as it is in itself and the human conceptions formed of it is effectively evoked by this creative use of paradox. In modern parlance, each human conception of reality needs to be 'deconstructed'. But then, unlike a modern deconstructionism so radically destructive that it has nothing left to 'reconstruct', the Imam's apophasis feeds directly into modes of spiritual intuition, contemplation, and realization of that which reason, on its own, cannot attain. It is this intuition and contemplation which 're-members' — or 'reconstructs', if one likes — the spiritual realities that transcend the categories of reason. The value of the paradoxes, the antinomies, the apophasis, and the negation is entirely predicated on the eminently positive realities which the intellect can plumb, thereby becoming aware of its own hidden depths. The resulting consciousness does not so much negate reason as situate it, revealing the limits of reason's proper sphere of operation: it reveals that reason alone cannot 'comprehend' the higher realities, nor express them fully, however indispensable reason may be in initiating the cognitive movement toward those realities.

Thus, when the Imam negates, as he does so often, the possibility of human conceptions encompassing the divine Reality, what is being negated is the limitation that is the inescapable consequence of all such conceptions — not the positive content, the mana or spiritual meaning, of the conception itself. For this conception is a necessary starting point for a process of assimilation that engages a deeper level of awareness than that 'de-fined' by the mind, and which therefore extends far beyond the domain of speculative thought. In this light, the negation of the limitations of human conception should be seen as the prelude to an absolute affirmation: the affirmation of the Absolute, which has no limitation. The spiritual negation of conceptual limitation is of an upward, 'constructive' order, not a nihilistic, deconstructive one. Nowhere is this style of discourse more apparent than in the famous opening lines of the first sermon of the Nahj al-balagha:

Praise be to God, whose laudation those who speak cannot deliver, whose graces those who count cannot number, whose rightful due those who strive cannot render; He who cannot be grasped by far-reaching aspirations, nor fathomed by profound intuitions; He whose attribute has no binding limitation, no existing description, no time appointed, no term extended.

The divine attribute (الصِّفَة) has no limit in terms of which it can be defined — laysa li-sifatih hadd mahdud — and yet it is affirmed as real. It has no 'existing description', and yet in some sense it is described (مَوْصُوف), by the very words of the Quranic revelation; for in the Quran God is described as having such attributes as mercy, compassion, and power. He is the Merciful, the Compassionate, the Almighty, and yet the Imam affirms that these very attributes cannot be defined, bounded, or described. The paradox sharpens still further when the Imam speaks of the perfection of إِخْلَاص — the 'rendering sincere', the purification of one's devotion to God and of one's conception of Him: this perfection, he says, is to divest (نفی) God of all attributes. On the one hand the attributes are affirmed; on the other they are to be negated. The rational mind can only think of God in terms of the attributes given verbal expression in the Quran; and yet here the Imam appears to negate these same attributes, thereby depriving the mind of any convenient conceptual handle by which to grasp the nature of the divine Reality. The simultaneous affirmation and negation of the attributes raises an apparently irresolvable contradiction, and in doing so enhances receptivity to a deeper mode of knowing. This style of discourse appears designed to awaken in the listener some latent awareness inscribed in the heart or the depths of the intellect, and to invite the seeker to dive into those depths, into the knowledge 'buried' in the intellect. The very shock of the apparent contradiction in this apophasis seems intended to precipitate a leap of consciousness, from the formal activity of reason to the supra-rational contemplation of the spirit.

The words spoken by the Imam immediately after affirming that his heart had indeed witnessed God — not by means of outward vision but through 'the verities of faith' — run:

My Lord is not described in terms of dimension (بُعْدٌ), nor motion (حَرَكَة), nor rest (سُكُونٌ) ... nor through coming or going: He is subtle in subtlety but not described by subtlety, magnificent in magnificence but not described by magnificence, great in greatness but not described by greatness ...

The attributes are again affirmed and denied. God possesses these attributes but is not reducible to them. The mind can begin to think of God in terms of these categories, but He is not reducible to such conceptions. The attributes afford the mind something by which the divine nature can be understood, but this understanding is fragmentary, never exhaustive, and it needs to be negated as the prelude to a fuller apprehension. The initial conceptual orientation to the divine Reality needs to be both negated and sublimated: negated as a mental conception, while the positive content of the conception needs to be sublimated through spiritual intuition. Only then can the two-dimensional 'portrait' defined by reason be given a third dimension — that of depth. But this third dimension emerges only after the insufficiency of the rational depiction is understood; whence the need for that cognitive shock which paradox produces, a shock intended to reveal the pretension of reason in its effort to disclose the nature of the Absolute, the transcendent source of reason's own activities.

The Imam's discourses show a repeated combination of affirmation and denial, which mirrors the negation and affirmation found in the first testimony of Islam: la ilaha, 'no god', being the negation (نفی); then illaLlah, 'except God', coming as the affirmation (اِثْبَات). This helps to show that the negation of the attributes which the Imam names as the perfection of إِخْلَاص is in fact an affirmation of the infinitude of the divine Reality. Each of the attributes is to be 'negated', but only insofar as it is conceived by the mind. Such negation is necessary because, at the mental level, each attribute excludes other attributes, and also excludes the object of attribution itself — that is, the divine Essence — such exclusion following inescapably from the very possibility of conceptually distinguishing between the attribute and the object of attribution, between the divine quality and the divine Essence. For any conception to take place in the mind, defined ideas are necessary; and where there are definitions, there are limits and thus exclusions. The reality of the attribute, however, is unlimited, by virtue of its absolute identity with the Essence. The attribute that has been 'negated' is paradoxically affirmed — not in itself, but inasmuch as it is nothing but God: illaLlah. Thus one can place each of the negated attributes in the position of the nafy of the first testimony and arrive at such formulations as la rahmana illaLlah, 'There is no All-Compassionate except God'; la rahima illaLlah, 'No All-Merciful except God', and so on. The apparent negation of the attributes becomes, in fact, an affirmation of their true identity, shorn of any restrictive association with the poverty of mental conceptions. The attributes are not superadded to the Essence but are absolutely one with it, differentiable from it only conceptually, not ontologically. The Essence does indeed comprise all the attributes mentioned — greatness, subtlety, and so on — but it is also infinitely more than these attributes; and, for their part, the attributes are infinite only within and as the Essence. The Imam's apophasis here can be seen as a commentary on the Quranic refrain, 'Glorified be God above what they describe' (Al-Muminun: 91). The infinitude of God strictly requires, on the human plane, the negation of all that the mind can conceive through reason — but not of all that the heart can 'see' through spiritual vision. This very negation on the plane of reason calls out to something deeper, and this in turn can only be understood in the light of spiritual praxis, which is always assumed as the reflective, devotional, or contemplative context for the hearers of the Imam's sermons and discourses. The heart, symbolic seat of the contemplative intelligence, calls out to be 'polished', and this is precisely what the 'remembrance of God' does — this dhikr (ذِکْر) Allah being, according to the Imam, the 'most excellent form of worship'. Even at this level of praxis there is a further paradox: the 'reality' of the remembrance is not attained until 'you forget yourself in your remembrance'.

It is not just the rational mind and the determinate conceptions proportioned to it that are to be negated or 'deconstructed', but the individual soul itself, as the locus of all cognition, rational or supra-rational. This final denouement, or فَنَاء, is the ultimate form of apophasis.

These considerations show the extent to which the spirit of the intellect, in Ali 's perspective, transcends the rational faculty. The intellect must be seen, first and foremost, as a spiritual faculty; and the spirit, in turn, must be appreciated as the source of the intellect, as well as the source of life. Without the intellect the spirit is reduced to unconscious existence; conversely, without the spirit, the intellect tends toward lifeless thought. These aspects of the spirit are clearly expressed in the first sermon of the Nahj, which can be read as a commentary upon the Quranic verses describing the creation of man in terms of God's breathing' of His spirit into man: 'He began the creation of man with clay... He fashioned him and breathed into him of His spirit' (ثُمَّ سَوَّىٰهُ وَنَفَخَ فِيهِ مِن رُّوحِهِ).

The following lines, from the first sermon, form one of the many passages in which this infusion of the divine spirit into man is commented upon by Ali:

> ثُمَّ جَمَعَ سُبْحَانَهُ مِنْ حَزْنِ الاْرْضِ وَسَهْلِهَا، وَعَذْبِهَا وَسَبَخِهَا۔ تُرْبَةً سَنَّهَا بالمَاءِ حَتَّى خَلَصَتْ۔ وَلاَطَهَا بِالبَلَّةِ حَتَّى لَزَبَتْ۔ فَجَبَلَ مِنْها صُورَةً ذَاتَ أَحْنَاءَ وَوُصُولَ، وَأَعْضَاءَ وَفُصُول۔ أَجْمَدَهَا حَتَّى اسْتَمْسَكَتْ، وَأَصْلَدَهَا حَتَّى صَلْصَلَتْ۔ لِوَقْت مَعْدُود، وَأجَل مَعْلُوم۔ ثُمَّ نَفَخَ فِيها مِنْ رُوحِهِ فَمَثُلَتْ إِنْساناً ذَا أَذْهَانُ يُجيلُهَا، وَفِكَر يَتَصَرَّفُ بِهَا، وَجَوَارِحَ يَخْتَدِمُهَا، وَأَدَوَات يُقَلِّبُهَا، وَمَعْرِفَة يَفْرُقُ بِهَا بَيْنَ الحَقِّ وَالبَاطِلِ۔

Then He, glorified be He, gathered up a quantity of dust, from some hard earth and some soft, some sweet earth and some salty, and poured water onto it until it was pure, then kneaded into it some moisture until it stuck together; then He fashioned from this a form with curves and joints, limbs and parts. He compacted it until it held tightly together, solidified it until it became as sounding clay, assigning to it a measured duration and a known consummation. Then He breathed into it of His spirit, and it stood up as a human being, endowed with intellectual powers with which to reflect, thoughts by which to conduct himself, limbs to put to service, instruments at his disposal, and knowledge with which to discern between the true and the false ...

The body had been fashioned entire — its eyes, its ears, the organ of thought all in place — and yet it could neither hear nor see nor reflect until God breathed His spirit into it; only then did it stand up as a human being. It is this 'in-spiration' — literally, the in-breathing and the insertion of the spirit, of God's breath — that produces consciousness and life. Death, by contrast, is the 'ex-piration', the departure of the spirit. Life in the absence of 'aspiration' — the intention to be true to one's spirit — is a kind of death. It is through the spirit that inanimate, unconscious matter is brought to life and to awareness of itself: it generates not only knowledge but self-knowledge as well. This intimate intertwining between the gift of life and the capacity to know — existence that is conscious, and knowledge that is existential — is one of the foundations of any truly spiritual perspective. The question is not one of setting the spirit against the body, but of recognizing that the infusion of the spirit into the body is precisely what brings the body to life; and it is for this reason that knowledge vivifies, in the very measure that it is spiritual: 'O ye who believe, respond to God and His Messenger when they call you to that which vivifies you' (Al-Anfal: 24). According to the Imam, one of the most important aspects of this call from God to human beings is to 'remind them of the buried treasures of the intellect.'

Existentiation is the bestowal of the spirit, which is consciousness. The 'aql, or intellect, is the principle of articulation for the spirit, and so it is not merely a mental faculty but is itself constitutive of our true life. The intellect is that of which the mental faculty is one formal expression, one mode of its operation — but by no means the only one. The aql is many-faceted, comprising aspects that in our times are rarely associated with intelligence. To be true to one's intellect — to the treasures buried deep within it, and not merely to the rational functions operative on its surface — amounts to being spiritual.

For Ali, the true intellectual — al-aqil — is one who not only thinks correctly but also acts ethically, and, at the deepest level, one who seeks to realize the ultimate Reality. The intellectual is defined as one who 'puts all things in their proper place' (العاقل من وضع شئی حق محلہ), one who aligns his acts with the intentions of his Creator. This same alignment is the very definition of justice (عَدْل); only the true intellectual can therefore be fully just (عَادِل), for only one who sees things as they truly are is able to put them in their right place.

Thought, action, and realization are all the concern of the true intellectual, the الْعَاقِل. Correct thinking, impeccable virtue, and authentic being are inextricably bound up with the intellect in the spiritual ethos considered here. These aspects of the ethos come into sharper focus in the light of the Imam's discourse to Kumayl.

## 4. The Discourse to Kumayl

The discourse the Imam delivered to Kumayl brings these aspects of the ethos into sharper focus. Its importance lies above all in how deeply esoteric it is. The Imam took his disciple Kumayl ibn Ziyad by the hand and led him out of Kufa to a graveyard; once the city lay behind them, he drew a deep breath and gave him the following private counsel.

> يَا كُمَيْل بْن زِيَادٍ، إِنَّ هذهِ الْقُلُوبَ أَوْعِيَةٌ فَخَيْرُهَا أَوْعَاهَا فَاحْفَظْ عَنِّي مَا أَقُولُ لَكَ۔ النَّاسُ ثَلاَثَةٌ- فَعَالِمٌ رَبَّانِيٌّ وَمُتَعَلِّمٌ عَلَى سَبِيلِ نَجَاةٍ، وَهَمَجٌ رَعَاعٌ أَتْبَاعُ كُلِّ نَاعِقٍ يَمِيلُونَ مَعَ كُلِّ رِيحٍ، لَمْ يَسْتَضِيئُوا بِنُورِ الْعِلْمِ، وَلَمْ يَلْجَؤُوا إِلَى رُكْنٍ وَثِيقٍ

O Kumayl ibn Ziyad, truly these hearts are vessels, containers for knowledge and wisdom, and the best of them are those which hold the most. So retain from me that which I say to you. People are divided into three types: a lordly knower (عَالِمٌ رَبَّانِيٌّ); one who seeks knowledge (مُتَعَلِّمٌ) for the sake of deliverance; and the common folk (هَمَجٌ رَعَاعٌ), the word naming the moth that hurls itself at any flame, following just anyone, swaying with every current, neither desiring to be illumined by the light of knowledge nor seeking refuge in any strong support.

O Kumayl, knowledge is better than wealth, for knowledge guards you, while you must guard your wealth; and wealth diminishes as it is spent, while knowledge only increases as it is disbursed, replenishing itself the more it is given away; the fruits of wealth vanish when the wealth itself is gone, the friends bought with money and the comforts it purchased all falling away with it, while no one can take a man's knowledge from him. O Kumayl ibn Ziyad, the wise apprehension of knowledge (مَعْرِفَةُ الْعِلْمِ) is a religion by which God is worshipped, for din, religion, is kin to dayn, a debt, and it is by acquiring knowledge that a man repays the debt his own intellect has incurred to its Maker. Through it man acquires obedience in his life and a good name after his death. And knowledge is a judge, while wealth is judged: knowledge is active and wealth passive, for the one who knows takes command of what he owns, his knowledge directing him how to handle it, whereas wealth is forever something ruled upon by its possessor.

O Kumayl ibn Ziyad, those who hoard wealth perish even while they live, but the knowers endure for as long as time subsists; their material forms are absent, but their images in the hearts of men are present. Ah, what abundant knowledge is here (and he pointed to his breast), would that I could find one to bear it. Yes, I found one who was quick to understand, but he could not be trusted with it, exploiting the tools of religion for the sake of worldly gain, empowering himself with the graces of God against His slaves and with His proofs against His friends; for when the vessel of the heart is corrupt, this knowledge, pure and precious as it is, becomes a weapon turned against the very friends of God. Then there was another, obedient to the bearers of truth but lacking heartfelt insight, in whom, at the first appearance of obscurity, doubt was kindled in the heart. So alas, neither this one nor that! Nor one who is greedy for pleasure and submissive to passion, nor one obsessed with acquisition and accumulation, neither of them a guardian of religion in any respect. They resemble nothing so much as grazing cattle. Thus does knowledge die with the death of its bearers, when its trust falls to the worthless and the self-serving.

But indeed, O my God, the earth will never be empty of one who establishes the proof of God, whether overtly and in the open or fearfully and in obscurity, lest God's proofs and elucidations come to naught. Yet how many are such as these, and where are they? By God, they may be the smallest in number, but with God they are the greatest in rank, for here authority is not the possession of the many, and truth is not settled by the count of those who hold it; the guardians of the proof are few, and it is they who stand highest in the sight of God. Through them God preserves His proofs and elucidations, so that they may entrust them to their compeers and sow them in the hearts of those who resemble them. Through them, knowledge penetrates the reality of insight; they take hold of the very spirit of certainty; they make easy what the extravagant find harsh, and they befriend that by which the ignorant are estranged; with their bodies they keep company with the world, while their spirits are bound to the transcendent realm. They are the vicegerents of God upon His earth, the summoners to His religion. Ah, how I long to see them! Go now, Kumayl, as you will.

The four sentences of the penultimate paragraph describe those who bear the title of lordly knower (عَالِمٌ رَبَّانِيٌّ): the fewest in number, yet the greatest in the sight of God, and the ones through whom His religion is sustained. By His religion is meant the divine reality of religion — the pure and unchanging essence of the faith, perfect and constant — as against religion in the ordinary sense, whose outward institutions are subject to the changes of human history. From Adam to the Seal of the prophets those outward institutions have shifted with the conditions of men, but the essence, the message, and the purpose have never altered: the salvation of the soul through willing submission to God, as the Quran declares: إِنَّ ٱلدِّينَ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ ٱلْإِسْلَمُ, "Truly, religion in the sight of God is submission" (Ali 'Imran: 19). It is through the knowledge of the true knowers that religion stays true to itself, and only through such sages that religion truly lives in the world.

"Through them, knowledge penetrates the reality of insight." The Imam does not say that their knowledge penetrates it, but that through them knowledge penetrates the reality of insight. Knowledge can remain merely abstract, far removed from that reality. There is the outward, surface knowledge of a thing — its zahir — and the inner, hidden knowledge of the same thing — its batin. Yet knowledge holds a potential that calls out to be realized. It needs to be brought to life so that it may reach something deeper than the words and concepts that give shape to its own outward form, the meaning that lies ready on the surface. A man may have a surface knowledge of a verse of the Quran, of a saying, of why he prays and fasts and how many cycles each prayer requires; but when he presses past that surface and asks what the bowing and the prostration truly represent, a world of knowledge opens that is wholly unlike the bare information he began with. Degrees of knowledge are plainly implied here, and with them the rejection of all literalism and superficiality, of the easy mistake of taking factual information for real knowledge, let alone for wisdom. The shift of consciousness this penetration requires is shown vividly by the Imam's gesture toward his breast: it is not the chest but the heart hidden behind it that holds knowledge. The chest is the outward form, the zahir that conceals the heart within its batin, and to reach the heart one must first penetrate the chest.

The word chosen to translate hajama is "penetrate," but the primary sense of the verb is to assault, to take by storm, to charge. In the warfare of that age the battle lines were drawn as a right wing, a left wing, and a center — the heart of the army (قَلْبُ الْعَسْكَرِ), where the king or commander stood. The aim of the assault was to break through the wings and reach that heart, for once the king was taken the battle was won, and the victor took command of everything the defeated had held: the kingdom, its treasury, its army, its people. The chessboard preserves the same rule. So the word helps to show that conceptual knowledge holds a latent force — one might almost call it an explosive power — deriving from the reality of knowledge, its haqiqa or spiritual essence. It is this that calls out to be made real through tahaqquq, realization in depth rather than mere verification on the surface; and the striving by which one storms the outer wall of words to reach the heart of their meaning is تَحْقِيق, investigation. This haqiqa can be pointed to, but never captured, by the words and concepts that make up its formal, communicable aspect — just as the chest can only be opened in order to reach the heart. By means of this inner force, which its outer conceptual form at once hides and evokes, knowledge can effect a shift of consciousness from one degree of knowledge to another: a man begins to see the very realities of this world differently from those who rest in the surface play of cause and effect, so that the same act which is a burden to one is a delight to another. An indirect, mental conception of reality thus prepares the way for a penetrating, existential perception of reality. This is the difference between mere sight and true insight: sight reaches only the outward, superficial reality, whereas the reality of insight opens the intelligence to reality as such — ordinary insight, like abstract knowledge, remaining only at the surface level of understanding. The same idea appears in a saying of Ali (ع) concerning the true understanding that comes to plumb, and so truly to know, the buried treasures of knowledge: "He who understands knows the depth of knowledge (مَنْ فَهِمَ عَلِمَ غَوْرَ اَلْعِلْمِ)." And the effort that true knowledge demands, as against the mere piling up of facts, is likewise indicated: "Through learning and assimilating knowledge, until it becomes deeply rooted in one's consciousness, is knowledge attained (بِالتَّعَلُّمِ يُنَالُ الْعِلْمُ)."

The urgency of realizing this immanent power within knowledge — of moving from the abstract to the concrete, the potential to the actual, the static to the dynamic, the formal to the essential — is powerfully evoked by hajama, a word bound up with battle. It warns against taking the first understanding of an idea for its full comprehension, and full intellectual comprehension for the idea's living interiorization in the spirit. For when knowledge at last storms the surface of religion and takes the heart, a transformation begins from within: the heart, now illumined by the realities it has seized, takes command of the limbs and the actions, which are the soul's own resources, exactly as the victor takes command of the kingdom he has won. This interiorization is a process of endless unfolding within, not the mere registering of some fact from without. It requires the effective integration of knowledge within being: knowledge from without must be transformed into being from within, woven into the deepest texture of one's existence. Knowledge thus demands not merely the attention of the mind but the total orientation of the soul.

In this ethos, then, the effective integration (تَوْحِيد) of knowledge within being cannot be reached without the participation of the whole soul; and this in turn requires the elimination of all the vices that flow from self-centeredness. Thus the الْعَاقِل, the true intellectual, is described by the Imam as one constantly engaged in struggle against his own soul — against the lower inclinations, the passionate whims, and the distracting caprices of the ego. These vices are not confined to the plane of morality; they encroach upon the domain of knowledge as well, and this becomes clear in the very measure that knowledge is grasped as a concrete vision of things rather than a merely notional or conceptual grasp of them. The vices are then seen as so many clouds obscuring the vision proper to the reality of insight. According to the Quran, evil actions become a rust that covers over the heart: "Nay, their hearts have become rusted by that which they have done" (Al-Mutaffifin: 14). The aqil, then, is engaged in a constant inner struggle against the tendencies that not only sully the soul but blind the heart. As a virtuous man he opposes vice on the moral plane, because it runs contrary to virtue; but vice is opposed for a further reason, one that lies on the higher plane of spiritual reality: it contradicts the nature of the Real, it obscures the reality of insight, and it blinds the eye of the heart which alone can see God.

This does not deny the validity of the moral perspective; the stress falls rather on the deeper motive that drives the الْعَاقِل toward virtue, a motive that rises above the moral plane while including it. Morality appears here as the fruit or effect, and spirituality as the seed or cause. By drawing attention to the intellectual side of the aqil's motive for shedding the vices, the Imam shows more clearly how immorality is rooted in ignorance; and to uproot that ignorance, for the true intellectual, is to dissolve the very substance of the vices. This uprooting demands great effort, a relentless battle within — indeed the most formidable of all battles, al-jihad al-akbar, the greatest struggle, as the Prophet defined it.

The following sayings of Ali bear on the importance of this inner struggle:

"Struggling against the soul through knowledge, such is the mark of the intellect."

"The strongest of people are those who are strongest against their own souls."

"Truly, one who fights his own soul in obedience to God, and does not sin against Him, has the rank of the righteous martyr in the sight of God."

"The ultimate battle is that of a man against his own soul."

"He who knows his soul fights it."

"No jihad is more excellent than the jihad of the soul."

In another saying the Imam clarifies what the Prophet meant by naming the greatest jihad the jihad of the soul (النَّفْس). It is not so much a matter of fighting against the soul as of the battle for the soul itself. This emerges from the metaphor by which the Imam defines the struggle: الْعَقْل, the intellect, leads the forces of al-Rahman, the Compassionate; al-hawa — whim, caprice, desire — commands the forces of al-shaytan, the devil; and النَّفْس, the soul, wavers between them, drawn by both (مُتَجَاذِبَةٌ بَيْنَهُمَا), falling under the sway of whichever of the two prevails.

These sayings make clear that for the true intellectual the soul in its entirety must be "conquered" and enlisted on the side of the intellect. The aim is not to destroy the lower soul but to free it from the destructive influence of its caprice, its egoistic whims, its subjectivism, its individualism — all of which are gathered up in the word هَوَى. The Prophet, the Quran tells us, does not speak from هَوَى; rather: "It is naught but a revelation revealed" (An-Najm: 3-4). The divine objectivity of revelation is here set against the human subjectivity of passionate whim. In the setting of the holy war within, the Imam's sayings bring a parallel opposition into focus: the objectivity of the intellect must prevail over the subjectivity of individual caprice and egotistic desire. It is the intellect that represents — in the sense of "makes present" — the divine power of al-Rahman, and that combats the forces of al-shaytan as embodied in al-hawa.
