The word هَوَى is used further in the Quran to denote desires that spring from the ego, by which the ego becomes a kind of false god: "Hast thou seen him who maketh his desire his god?" (25: 43; almost identical at 45: 23). This is the subtle or hidden idolatry (الشِّرْكُ الْخَفِيُّ) that the Prophet described as more hidden than a black ant crawling on a dark stone in a moonless night. To root out this idolatry of the ego, a force higher than the ego itself must be brought to bear, since the ego can never be controlled by anything that belongs to it; and that higher force is the divine Reality, access to which is opened through the power of the intellect and the graces of faith. This complementarity between the effort to overcome the egoism of هَوَى and the inflow of divine grace is well expressed in the following Quranic verse: "As for he who fears the station of his Lord, and restraineth his soul from whim (هَوَى), verily, Paradise will be his abode" (An-Naziat: 40-41).

The soul's fundamental energy is not to be destroyed but converted and redirected — away from the fleeting objects of individualistic desire and the impulses of al-shaytan, and toward the one true object named by al-Rahman. This "conquest" of the soul is far more than the arrival at some mental conception. It is not only the mind, the will, and the character that must be transformed; the soul's very deepest disposition must be integrated into the truth perceived by the intellect, if a man is to perceive that truth in all its dimensions and ramifications across the different planes of being.

For the Imam the need to win the soul over in its totality follows naturally from his understanding of تَوْحِيد, the most fundamental principle of Islam. تَوْحِيد is a verbal noun, and to translate it as mere oneness or unity fails to capture its full force. Literally it means "affirming one" — the affirmation of and belief in God's oneness, which is the outward, theological meaning — or "realizing one," the realization that there is but one reality, which is the inner, spiritual meaning. In the Imam's view, then, the absolutely Real is perceived not only as being one but also as making one, as integrating. The Real is not only unique; it is also unifying. تَوْحِيد thus comes to mean not merely affirming the oneness of God, but being one oneself so as to reflect the One perfectly: to know God is to be "integrated." Here one may see a link with the etymology of 'aql, whose primary meaning is "to bind." Yet whereas the later mystical tradition tends to stress the limiting aspect of this word — the intellect as that which defines and encloses meaning, over against the heart, which intuits and discloses spiritual truth — here the intellect is that which "binds together" the scattered elements of the soul into an organic unity. This principle is expressed in the first of the following sayings of the Imam, each of which merits deep reflection:

"He who knows God integrates himself (مَنْ عَرَفَ اللهَ تَوَحَّدَ)."

"He who knows his soul disengages himself (مَنْ عَرَفَ نَفْسَهُ تَجَرَّدَ)."

"He who knows people isolates himself (مَنْ عَرَفَ النَّاسَ تَفَرَّدَ)."

"He who knows the world withholds himself (مَنْ عَرَفَ الدُّنْيَا تَزَهَّدَ)."

Elsewhere this total adherence to the Real is described by the Imam as "sincerity" — sidq or إِخْلَاص — terms that mean far more than simple truthfulness, as the root of إِخْلَاص already shows. The verb khalusa means to be utterly pure, true to one's essence, unmixed with any foreign element. This side of the meaning comes through clearly in the following saying of the Imam: "Make purely for God (أَخْلِصْ لِلَّهِ) your action and your knowledge, your love and your hatred, your taking and your leaving, your speech and your silence."

To be totally sincere, then, means to make oneself "purely" for God — to carry no "stain" of impurity, understood here as whatever is other than God, in one's devotion, action, intention, or even sentiment. One must love and hate only in relation to one's bond with God. Of all the tendencies that work their way into one's motives and intentions, the most destructive is egotism. إِخْلَاص, pure sincerity, is the active effort to be, think, and act purely for God — that is, with selflessness — which is why the Imam lays such stress on the holy war within the soul as the "ultimate battle." One who wins this battle attains the rank of the "righteous martyr," for the victor here is a martyr in the truest sense: he has sacrificed his lower life for the sake of God, with whom is the real life — the life witnessed by the intellect, the only element within human consciousness able to look upon the soul as "the other" with genuine objectivity.

As the saying holds, "struggling against the soul through knowledge, such is the mark of the intellect." The intellectual engaged in this battle is also one who bears "witness" (al-shahid, meaning both martyr and witness, as in the original Greek sense of the word). He bears witness to God through the intellect's sacrifice of whatever opposes the truth of God, and the chief opponent is his own egotism, his own "false life." To possess perfect إِخْلَاص, then, is to be rid of all hypocritical self-preoccupation and to put in its place a sincere theocentricity — a mode of being centered upon God rather than oneself. إِخْلَاص, therefore, means being for God, or belonging to God, in the sense pointed to by the Quranic verse: "Say: Truly my worship and my sacrifice, and my living and my dying, are for God, Lord of the universes" (Al-Anam: 162).

That worship is the first thing named in this verse is of particular significance for understanding Ali 's spiritual ethos, and the indispensable role of worship and devotion must be emphasized in setting that ethos out. The second chapter takes up this role in the context of the quest for the fundamental virtue of justice. The ultimate fruits of worship and devotion — of which the quintessence is the remembrance of God — are the subject of the third chapter. One of the defining features of the Imam's spiritual ethos is indeed the emphasis he places on prayer, taking that term in its widest sense to include all the means — from formal worship and supplication to meditation and reflection, remembrance and contemplation — by which the individual is oriented and attuned to the Real, اَلْحَقّ.

Worship is therefore an integral aspect of إِخْلَاص, precisely because it answers to and sustains a fundamental dimension of the human soul. That within the soul which yearns to worship, and is nourished by worship alone, must be given its due. In a spiritual context, if worship is pushed to the margins, one's sincerity is compromised: one is no longer being sincere with God, no longer being "just" to God, no longer rendering God His due. And the spiritual logic of the Imam holds that if one is not just with God, one cannot be just to His creatures. "Whoso establishes well-being between himself and God," he says, "God establishes well-being between him and mankind."

One might also cite here the Quranic verse: "Worship thy Lord until certainty cometh to thee" (Al-Hijr: 99). The quest for knowledge demands total dedication if it is to yield its inner riches, and that dedication includes the near-alchemical transformation of consciousness worked by total concentration on the divine Reality — prayer in one of its deepest modes. In this form of prayer, the intensity of dedication is such that nothing else can occupy one's consciousness alongside its object: the "remembrance" (ذِکْر) of God strictly implies the forgetting of everything else, and above all of oneself. The spiritual reality of the dhikr can only be reached when "you forget your soul in your remembrance." Concentration upon the divine Reality as the supreme object of knowledge, made present through the Name or Names of God, is thus a mode of prayer — one which, to be effective, requires both presence of mind and a genuine engagement of the will. To pray absent-mindedly or mechanically is far from enough. On this the Imam says, "He who prays without exertion is like one who shoots [arrows] without a bow." In the same way, there is nothing passive or easy about acquiring knowledge: "Knowledge calls out for action; if it is answered [it is of avail], otherwise it departs."

The same idea is expressed more forcefully in this saying of the Imam: "When you hear knowledge [being imparted] cleave to it, do not mix it up with jest, lest hearts spew it forth." Knowledge that is not taken seriously will be of no avail; it remains "undigested" and cannot be assimilated by the "heart" — that innermost faculty which has access to the "reality of insight" and which can thus "see" God. For it is the heart that sees Allah, not by eyesight, not by بصارۃ, but through the spiritual insight, the بصیرۃ, generated by the true principles of faith, the حَقَائِقُ الْاِیمَانِ. Indeed, it is not the individual who "assimilates" knowledge to himself; it is knowledge — or rather the reality of being expressed through true knowledge — that assimilates the individual to itself: "Acquire knowledge, and [true] life will acquire you." The relationship between life and consciousness, so central to the intrinsic function of the spirit, is again at the heart of this maxim. At first the individual seeks knowledge and cultivates consciousness; then life "seeks" the individual, as being makes itself more fully real within him. Put differently, the individual first makes an active effort to integrate knowledge within his being; but at a certain point this activity turns into passive receptivity: being — "true life" — integrates the individual within itself. To return to the Imam's guidance to Kumayl: "Through them knowledge penetrates the reality of insight." It is not the knowers who penetrate; it is knowledge that penetrates to the reality of insight, doing so through them, and this in the very measure of their effacement within knowledge — an effacement that is the shadow cast by their effective integration within the life that "acquires" them.

## 5. The Fruits of Certainty

The discourse to Kumayl continues: "They rejoice in their intimacy with the spirit of certainty." This sentence speaks directly to the sheer happiness that flows from the life of the spirit. In the Arabic phrase *wa basharu ruh al-yaqin*, the word *bashara* means first of all to have intimate experience of something, to come into direct physical contact with it. In this form the verb also carries a sense of joy, so that "to rejoice in intimate contact" conveys better what the single word holds. To touch the spirit of certainty is not to reach some cold logical fact at the end of a purely mental process. It means entering deeply into the very object of one's certainty — and that object, in the end, is nothing less than the divine itself. This reality is infinitely more blessed than any bliss of Paradise one could imagine, for the Quran affirms that the divine رِضْوَان, its beatitude and contentment, is "greater" than Paradise. It cannot, then, but bestow joy upon the soul that truly opens itself to it:

> وَعَدَ ٱللَّهُ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنَتِ جَنَّتٍۢ تَجْرِى مِن تَحْتِهَا ٱلْأَنْهَرُ خَلِدِينَ فِيهَا وَمَسَكِنَ طَيِّبَةًۭ فِى جَنَّتِ عَدْنٍۢ ۚ وَرِضْوَنٌۭ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ أَكْبَرُ ۚ ذَلِكَ هُوَ ٱلْفَوْزُ ٱلْعَظِيمُ
>
> "God has promised the believers, men and women, gardens beneath which rivers flow, wherein they abide, and goodly dwellings in gardens of Eden. And beatitude from God is greater. That is the supreme triumph" (At-Tawbah: 72).

In the words of Ali (ع), "Learning and reflecting upon knowledge is the delight of the knowers."

This might be called a way of knowing steeped in joy, for "the most joyous of mankind is the intellectual (الْعَاقِل)." Such a view stands in sharp contrast to the modern conception, modeled on science, that treats knowledge as dry, cold, and abstract. It deserves careful thought, for it opens up a crucial dimension of the spiritual knowledge proper to the Imam's perspective, and indeed to every spiritual tradition worthy of the name. To engage with truths ought to bring happiness, and it does so to the degree that those truths are fundamental to one's own being, or to Being as such. In the end these two are one and the same, given the uniqueness — the "one-and-onliness" — of Being. What has no second, no other, cannot be counted; Being is indivisible. So the being of the individual cannot finally differ from Being as such, however vast the difference in existence between the individual and God — a difference, indeed, past all common measure. True knowledge is knowledge of Being, and so it cannot help but be a form of self-discovery, even though knowledge of the Absolute is impossible without absolute self-abandonment. The highest discovery calls for the deepest self-effacement.

In the early stages, though, the true knowledge the intellectual gains is a self-discovery that brings the happiness of a homecoming — a return from the exile of ignorance. This is so because "the most intellectual of people" is "the one who is closest to God" and "the one who is most alive." To be close to God is to be fully alive, and this true life gives a deep joy, the *joie de vivre*, the sheer joy of living. It is a joy that belongs to existence itself, independent of any particular experience, pleasant or not.

Here the crucial bond between life and consciousness comes into view — between the fruits of the in-spiration of God's spirit on one side, and this life of spiritual awareness and joy on the other. Those who are true to the spirit of God within them truly possess joy. It is likewise from their intimate and unshakeable certainty of God's goodness and compassion that the Imam defines the true فَقِيهٌ — not so much "the jurist" as "one who truly understands" — as "he who does not make people despair of the mercy of God, and does not make them lose hope in the gracious spirit of God (*rawh Allah*)." So deeply is the sage immersed in the joyous certainty of God's mercy that no room is left for despair. To despair of this mercy is itself a mark of unbelief:

> قَالَ وَمَن يَقْنَطُ مِن رَّحْمَةِ رَبِّهِۦٓ إِلَّا ٱلضَّآلُّونَ
>
> "And who despaireth of the mercy of his Lord except those who stray in error?" (Al-Hijr: 56).

The Quran's assurances of mercy come fully alive in souls that possess a deep knowledge of the blessed nature of the divine Reality — a knowledge lived, not merely thought:

> وَٱكْتُبْ لَنَا فِى هَذِهِ ٱلدُّنْيَا حَسَنَةًۭ وَفِى ٱلْآخِرَةِ إِنَّا هُدْنَآ إِلَيْكَ ۚ قَالَ عَذَابِىٓ أُصِيبُ بِهِۦ مَنْ أَشَآءُ ۖ وَرَحْمَتِى وَسِعَتْ كُلَّ شَىْءٍۢ ۚ فَسَأَكْتُبُهَا لِلَّذِينَ يَتَّقُونَ وَيُؤْتُونَ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ وَٱلَّذِينَ هُم بِـَٔايَتِنَا يُؤْمِنُونَ

"My mercy encompasseth all things" (Al-Araf: 156), and

> وَإِذَا جَآءَكَ ٱلَّذِينَ يُؤْمِنُونَ بِـَٔايَتِنَا فَقُلْ سَلَمٌ عَلَيْكُمْ ۖ كَتَبَ رَبُّكُمْ عَلَىٰ نَفْسِهِ ٱلرَّحْمَةَ ۖ أَنَّهُۥ مَنْ عَمِلَ مِنكُمْ سُوٓءًۢا بِجَهَلَةٍۢ ثُمَّ تَابَ مِنۢ بَعْدِهِۦ وَأَصْلَحَ فَأَنَّهُۥ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۭ
>
> "thy Lord has prescribed for Himself mercy" (Al-Anam: 54).

These are the ones who truly understand, the true فُقَهَاءُ.

This opening to joy through intimate knowledge of God's mercy cannot be separated from beauty, and in the Imam's view the perception of beauty is another unexpected companion of the intellect. The love of beauty is not merely a matter of private taste; it belongs objectively to the intellect's own working. Beauty also leads straight to the heart of morality. The Arabic احسان is often translated simply as "virtue" or "excellence," yet one of its plain, literal meanings is "making beautiful," since حَسَنَ means "beauty." Virtue, احسان, can thus be understood as what makes the soul beautiful. Seen this way, the bond among intellect, morality, and beauty grows far clearer. "The excellence of the intellect," says Ali, "is in the beauty of things outward and inward (جَمَالُ الظَّوَاهِرِ وَالْبَوَاطِنِ)." The outward beauty of forms and actions must be matched by inward beauty — beauty of soul, or virtue in its fullest and deepest sense. All of this follows naturally from two sayings attributed to the Prophet: "Truly God is beautiful and He loves beauty," and "Assume the character traits of God."

To cultivate beautiful traits within oneself makes one lovable to God, since He loves beauty. And making oneself lovable to God cannot be separated from imitating the virtues brought to perfection in the soul of the Prophet:

> قُلْ إِن كُنتُمْ تُحِبُّونَ ٱللَّهَ فَٱتَّبِعُونِى يُحْبِبْكُمُ ٱللَّهُ وَيَغْفِرْ لَكُمْ ذُنُوبَكُمْ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۭ
>
> "Say: O ye who believe, if ye love God, follow me, the Prophet; God will love you" (Ali 'Imran: 31).

Another saying of the Imam points to this drawing-down of God's love through making oneself beautiful in the fullest sense: "The foundation of the intellect is the assimilation of love (أَوَّلُ الْعَقْلِ التَّوَدُّدُ)." Instead of speaking of love as such (*wudd/mawadda*), the Imam chooses the form تَوَدُّد to draw attention to the need to make oneself lovable to God. The word points to a twofold work: taking the principle of love into oneself and making it real there, and at the same time letting the love felt within shine outward — for love is at once an outward radiance and an inward attraction.

To follow the Prophet, and so make oneself open to the inflowing of divine love, means among other things cultivating the prophetic virtues. These virtues are called prophetic not because they belong to the Prophet alone, but because he is held to have realized virtue in all its fullness and is therefore the exemplar, par excellence, of virtue itself. Beauty and love are not the only qualities that give shape to the intellect's openness to the divine Reality. Kindness and contentment, courtesy, generosity and modesty, and wise forbearance (حِلْم) matter just as much. Not one of these moral qualities is missing from the soul of the true intellectual. There can be no wisdom or intellect in the fullest sense in a soul that lacks them. It is exactly the presence of these virtues that lets the intellect grow in depth and in breadth. Without them it stays trapped on the flat, horizontal plane of its own operations, held to the work of reason alone. Such reason, until faith touches it and grace deepens it, does not know what lies beneath the surface of its own field. It stays blind to the hidden treasures of the intellect, and blind to the need to gather the whole soul within the mold of that integral, or unifying, virtue which gives intelligence its integrity. This making-one, this integration, lies at the very root of تَوْحِيد, now understood as a living process of spiritual unification demanded by the very idea of God's oneness. Truth must plainly inspire the soul; but just as necessary, in the other direction, is that virtue should aerate the intelligence — the leaven that lets it rise to its full dimensions.

In this perspective, sinful and immoral qualities are seen not only as vices but as so many blind spots, so many failures of the intellect. Vice sickens the heart; it blinds the heart's eye and so leaves it unable to see the Real. As the Quran says:

> فِى قُلُوبِهِم مَّرَضٌۭ فَزَادَهُمُ ٱللَّهُ مَرَضًۭا ۖ وَلَهُمْ عَذَابٌ أَلِيمٌۢ بِمَا كَانُوا۟ يَكْذِبُونَ

"In their hearts is a disease and God increased them in disease" (Al-Baqarah: 10), and

> أَفَلَمْ يَسِيرُوا۟ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ فَتَكُونَ لَهُمْ قُلُوبٌۭ يَعْقِلُونَ بِهَآ أَوْ آذَانٌۭ يَسْمَعُونَ بِهَا ۖ فَإِنَّهَا لَا تَعْمَى ٱلْأَبْصَرُ وَلَكِن تَعْمَى ٱلْقُلُوبُ ٱلَّتِى فِى ٱلصُّدُورِ
>
> "It is not the sights that are blind, but blind are the hearts" (Al-Hajj: 46).

All the vices are contained within egotism, which in turn is often called simply caprice (هَوَى). In one saying the Imam treats the conquest of caprice as the same as salvation: "He attains deliverance whose intellect dominates his caprice." Deliverance, فَلَاحٌ, which carries the sense both of salvation and of blessedness, is thus within reach to some degree here and now; it is not a grace granted only after death. Such deliverance means tasting the joy that flows from certainty, from intimate contact with the spirit of that certainty. As for how to cultivate an openness to this spirit of certainty: beyond obedience to the rules and regulations of the revealed Law, what is required is that union of pure intention and sincere devotion that the word إِخْلَاص expresses. Without this sincerity, this purity, obedience to formal rules is nothing but hypocrisy (نِفَاقٌ) and pretension (رِيَاء) — which the Imam in turn calls the gravest of all sins, شِرْك, the setting-up of partners to God: "Know that the slightest pretension (رِيَاء) is polytheism." This saying can be read as a commentary on one of the Quran's weightiest condemnations of hypocrisy. The verses speak of those who deny the essence of faith while outwardly keeping to it:

"Hast thou seen the one who belieth religion? He is the one who repelleth the orphan and urgeth not the feeding of the poor. So woe be to those who pray, those who are heedless of their prayer, who want only to be seen, and withhold small kindnesses" (Al-Maun: 1-7).

This short sura makes plain that formal acts of devotion have no value at all unless they are woven into a life of virtue and performed with the right intention — that is, with إِخْلَاص. It also helps explain why the Imam's ethos is called here not merely religious but spiritual: it carries the ideals of religion to their deepest application. To be spiritual in this sense is precisely to be true to one's intellect, true to the treasures buried deep within it, and not merely to the reasoning that works on its surface. Without this depth, religion is empty. "There is no religion for one who has no intellect," says the Imam. And likewise: "Action without knowledge is error." What counts is the intention behind the action, for "action, all action, is dust, except what is purified within it." This turning away from the action in itself, toward an inner state of being from which good actions must flow, clearly reflects the Quranic teaching:

> لَن يَنَالَ ٱللَّهَ لُحُومُهَا وَلَا دِمَآؤُهَا وَلَكِن يَنَالُهُ ٱلتَّقْوَىٰ مِنكُمْ ۚ كَذَلِكَ سَخَّرَهَا لَكُمْ لِتُكَبِّرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ عَلَىٰ مَا هَدَىٰكُمْ ۗ وَبَشِّرِ ٱلْمُحْسِنِينَ
>
> "It is not their sacrificed flesh nor their blood that reacheth God, but it is piety from you that reacheth Him" (Al-Hajj: 37).

"There is no religion for one who has no intellect." Perhaps no group shows the truth of this saying better than the Kharijites. Among those who rebelled against the Imam were many readers (قُرَّاء, sing. *qari*) of the Quran — men who enjoyed considerable religious prestige for their Quranic knowledge and their intense worship. Their piety, though, was more apparent than real; it was in fact a zeal born of bitterness, a caricature of true piety. When his supporters spoke of the long night vigils kept by members of this group, the Imam answered sharply: "Sleeping with certainty is better than praying with doubt." Certainty brings peace and joy. So for one immersed in what the Imam calls the spirit of certainty, sleep is a rest of the body that only deepens the happiness flowing from a good conscience and from spiritual certitude — a certitude that is itself a kind of unbroken wakefulness, an awakeness to higher realities. Sleep of the body, then, does not contradict wakefulness of the spirit. As the hadith of the Prophet has it: "My eyes sleep, but my heart sleeps not."

When it comes to formal worship, the true intellectual, the الْعَاقِل — whom the Imam defines as the one who puts each thing in its proper place (العاقل من وضع شئی حق محلہ) — is one who obeys the divine command to pray. Yet he does not pray by rote, supposing that the act alone is enough. He knows that such obedience is necessary but not sufficient for opening himself to grace. There must be awareness of what one is doing and why; and it is this awareness, together with virtue, inward beauty, that draws divine acceptance. "The most worthy knowledge for you," says the Imam, "is that by virtue of whose presence action is accepted by God."

"They make easy what the extravagant find harsh; they befriend that by which the ignorant are estranged." This sentence bears, among other things, on how the remembrance of death — its certainty, and the renunciation of this world that certainty demands — works upon the souls of the true sages. To begin with, what the extravagant, the greedy, worldly sort find harsh is outward hardship, difficulty, and loss. The Quran views all such circumstances as the trials that must accompany life in this world:

"And We shall try you with something of fear and hunger and loss of property and life and fruits; but give glad tidings to the patient, those who, when struck by affliction say: 'Verily, we belong to God and verily unto Him we are ever-returning.' These are the ones upon whom there are blessings from their Lord, and mercy; and these are the ones who are rightly guided" (Al-Baqarah: 155-157).

The passage continues cleanly. Here is the articulated chapter prose.

The rightly-guided perceive the blessings poured out upon those who stay patient under affliction—a patience that grows from their knowledge of the truth: "By the Age, truly man is in a state of loss, except those who believe and do virtuous deeds and exhort one another to truth and exhort one another to patience" (Al-Asr: 1-3). These believers stand firm through trials, rooted in their awareness of where they came from and where they return, and in the knowledge that unto Him we are ever-returning. So they can make the trials and hardships of life easy to bear, while the extravagant meet those same trials only as harshness and bitterness. The ease folded into every difficulty stays hidden from the ignorant but is plain to the wise: "Verily, with hardship cometh ease; verily with hardship cometh ease" (Ash-Sharh: 5-6). The wise do more than await ease as the certain fruit of hardship; they taste that fruit already as the seed hidden inside hardship itself, for they befriend the very thing that the ignorant flee.

One layer of this meaning is lit by a saying of Ali: "Death is more intimate to me than the breast of the mother is to the suckling babe." In the verse cited above, the mention of trials and hardships follows a reminder that those slain in the path of God are not truly dead, "nay, they are alive but ye perceive not." This affirms the life hereafter, but it also points to the inner meaning of being slain for God: اَلْفَنَاءُ فِي اللَّهِ, extinction in God. The Imam's intimacy with death can be understood in relation to this death while still alive, which he touches on in another saying: "I am the death of the dead." Because life in the world is itself a kind of death—a limitation, and so a lack—set against the boundless fullness of the divine realms, dying to this death amounts to true life:

> وَمَا هَذِهِ ٱلْحَيَوٰةُ ٱلدُّنْيَآ إِلَّا لَهْوٌۭ وَلَعِبٌۭ ۚ وَإِنَّ ٱلدَّارَ ٱلْآخِرَةَ لَهِىَ ٱلْحَيَوَانُ ۚ لَوْ كَانُوا۟ يَعْلَمُونَ

"And the life of this world is nothing but sport and play," says the Quran, "and verily, the abode of the Hereafter, that is true life, did they but know" (Al-Ankabut: 64).

What must be negated is not the physical world as such, but worldliness—the self-centered attachment to the world. One key principle of the Imam's outlook, stressed again and again throughout the *Nahj al-balagha*, bears directly here: the principle of زُهْدٌ, which no single English word can render adequately. It gathers several meanings: asceticism, austerity, abstention, detachment, doing without, and making little of. The following passage from the *Nahj* conveys well the attitude of the *zahid* toward this world:

The world is an abode for which annihilation is ordained, and for its people departure from it is decreed. It is sweet and green, and it hastens to one who seeks it, and confounds the heart that gazes upon it. So set out from it with the best of the provision available to you, and ask not from it above what suffices and seek not from it more than what can be attained.

What is this best of provision, this زَادٌ? In many places the *Nahj* describes it as تَقْوَى, another word that resists simple translation, its meaning spanning piety, fear of God, awe, and God-consciousness. "I exhort you, O slaves of God, to تَقْوَى, which is the provision," says the Imam. This provision for the next world is won only in the measure of one's detachment from the perishable side of the life of this world, for "whoso desireth that which hasteneth away, in that We hasten for him what We will":

> مَّن كَانَ يُرِيدُ ٱلْعَاجِلَةَ عَجَّلْنَا لَهُۥ فِيهَا مَا نَشَآءُ لِمَن نُّرِيدُ ثُمَّ جَعَلْنَا لَهُۥ جَهَنَّمَ يَصْلَىٰهَا مَذْمُومًۭا مَّدْحُورًۭا
>
> "Whoever desires the fleeting life, We hasten for him therein what We will, for whom We intend; then We appoint for him Hell, wherein he burns, disgraced and rejected." (Al-Isra: 18)

The Imam describes the contemptible side of the world in vivid terms. "By God, this world of yours is more contemptible in my eyes than a chewed-up bone of a pig in the hand of a leper." "An image of the world is the snake, soft to touch, but virulent poison within." The world is "as worthless to me as that which drips from the nose of a goat." It is the grandeur and beauty of God that renders the world small and distasteful; only in the light of the Absolute do the world's pleasures and attractions show themselves as fleeting and illusory: "The greatness of the Creator for you belittles creatures in your view." In the same spirit, the Imam speaks of a brother in God: "What made him great in my eyes was the smallness of the world in his eyes (*وَكَانَ يُعَظِّمُهُ فِي عَيْنِي صِغَرُ الدُّنْيَا فِي عَيْنِهِ*)."

This, in the Quranic usage of *zahid*, is the real meaning of زُهْدٌ. In the sura named after Joseph (يُوسُفُ), the brothers of Yusuf held him of little value in selling him for a small price, and in relation to him they are described as زَاهِدِينَ:

> وَشَرَوْهُ بِثَمَنٍۭ بَخْسٍۢ دَرَهِمَ مَعْدُودَةٍۢ وَكَانُوا۟ فِيهِ مِنَ ٱلزَّهِدِينَ
>
> "And they sold him for a paltry price, a few dirhams counted out, and they were of those who deemed him of little worth." (Yusuf: 20)

In the Imam's outlook the world is likewise held of little value—not valueless outright, but rendered all but insignificant beside the Hereafter, beside eternity, beside ultimate reality. All created things shrink to tiny, fleeting shadows bound for annihilation: "Everything perishes except His Face":

> وَلَا تَدْعُ مَعَ ٱللَّهِ إِلَهًا آخَرَ ۘ لَآ إِلَهَ إِلَّا هُوَ ۚ كُلُّ شَىْءٍ هَالِكٌ إِلَّا وَجْهَهُۥ ۚ لَهُ ٱلْحُكْمُ وَإِلَيْهِ تُرْجَعُونَ
>
> "And do not invoke with God another deity. There is no deity except Him. Everything will perish except His Face. His is the judgment, and to Him you will be returned." (Al-Qasas: 88)

Yet the world is not stripped of all value; indeed, its significance lies precisely in what it cannot help but signify: "And there is nothing that does not hymn His glory with praise":

> تُسَبِّحُ لَهُ ٱلسَّمَوَتُ ٱلسَّبْعُ وَٱلْأَرْضُ وَمَن فِيهِنَّ ۚ وَإِن مِّن شَىْءٍ إِلَّا يُسَبِّحُ بِحَمْدِهِۦ وَلَكِن لَّا تَفْقَهُونَ تَسْبِيحَهُمْ ۗ إِنَّهُۥ كَانَ حَلِيمًا غَفُورًۭا
>
> "The seven heavens and the earth and whatever is in them exalt Him. And there is not a thing except that it exalts Him by His praise, but you do not understand their way of exalting. Indeed, He is ever Forbearing and Forgiving." (Al-Isra: 44)

Everything in the world, every creature in existence, proclaims the boundless creativity of the Creator. So that the imagination might grasp the mystery of this universal praise, the Quran says: "And the birds in flight, each one knoweth its prayer and glorification":

> أَلَمْ تَرَ أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُسَبِّحُ لَهُۥ مَن فِى ٱلسَّمَوَتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱلطَّيْرُ صَٓفَّتٍۢ ۖ كُلٌّۭ قَدْ عَلِمَ صَلَاتَهُۥ وَتَسْبِيحَهُۥ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ عَلِيمٌۢ بِمَا يَفْعَلُونَ
>
> "Do you not see that God is exalted by whoever is within the heavens and the earth and by the birds with wings spread in flight? Each one has known its prayer and its exalting, and God is Knowing of what they do." (An-Nur: 41)

There is thus no contradiction in the Imam's despising the world and yet growing lyrical over the beauties of untouched nature, including that unsurpassable marvel, the peacock. This comes in a sermon that also describes birds in general, along with ants, serpents, and elephants. The sermon ends with a stirring description of Paradise, setting the marvels of this world beside the delights of the next; in the light of the latter, all earthly things fade into insignificance. For once the eye of the heart turns toward Paradise, "your soul would become averse to the marvels of this world, its passions, its pleasures and its embellished scenery."

It might seem contradictory to hold together such apparently opposed qualities as joy in certainty, discussed above, and the befriending of hardship. Yet the latter is in fact the fruit of the former. Every difficulty without exception is borne through the spiritual resources that faith makes available when it is joined to intellectual assimilation—or rather, in the measure that faith is intellectually assimilated and so becomes *marifa* (معرفۃ) or gnosis, holding a certainty so deep and so complete that "even if the veil be lifted" it cannot increase, according to the famous saying noted earlier. It is possible to believe, at one level of the mind, that there is no god but God. The Imam, however, draws attention to what this apparently simple belief should imply when it is sincere or pure: "I bear witness that there is no god but God alone Who has no partner, a testimony whose sincerity has been tested and whose essence has been assimilated with conviction (*mutaqadan musaffaha*)."

The word مُصَفَّى names the choicest and purest part of any substance, its very essence. The root *massa* means "to suck," the idea being that the liquid or juice of a substance or fruit holds its essence, the part drawn out from it. Here the Imam is stressing the spiritual substance of the testimony, as distinct from mere spoken affirmation or mental grasp of the words. He affirms a heartfelt bond with the reality to which the testimony bears witness; the words that define it are an echo of the reality they carry, and are not to be confused with that reality itself. He continues: "We cling to it always, for as long as we endure, storing it up as protection against the terrors that will befall us. For truly, it renders faith resolute, opens up spiritual virtue, pleases *al-Rahman* (the All-Compassionate), repels *al-shaytan* (the devil)."

Returning to "befriending that by which the ignorant are estranged," this points not only to the trials of life—a source of strength for the wise and of repulsion for the ignorant—but also to the inescapability of death. For the wise, death means release, the meeting with God, blissful eternity—in short, a mode of تَوْحِيد, whereby the soul enjoys ever deeper degrees of integration within higher principles. For the ignorant, by contrast, death appears as a terrifying disintegration, the end of their world rather than the beginning of endless life. There is also the prefiguration of death in life already noted above: فَنَاء, "extinction," "effacement," the "perishing" of egoistic consciousness before the Absolute. The ignorant recoil in horror at the prospect of losing what they falsely take to be their life, not seeing that this sacrifice of egoistic and limited self-consciousness is the very ground on which infinite consciousness is glimpsed or tasted. The ocean of the Real can be tasted only by being drowned in it.

The means to this ultimate attainment is likewise embraced by the wise and feared by the ignorant: the act of pure prayer, total concentration on the Real. Such concentration shuts out every worldly or egotistic preoccupation; the sheer intensity of focus upon the Absolute burns away thoughts of the relative, and for those who ignorantly identify with relativities, inward and outward alike, this amounts to a kind of death. For the wise, by contrast, this concentration on the Absolute, the unsullied remembrance of God, is a joyous contemplation of reality before which the ego is displaced and all illusions dissolve. This leads straight to the next sentence of the passage: "With their bodies, they keep company with the world, while their spirits are tied to the transcendent realm."

This sentence grows more concrete when read alongside another well-known expression of the Imam. He describes himself as belonging to a group whose spiritual degree is such that "their hearts are in the Gardens of Paradise while their bodies are at work." To be tied or attached to that realm—which takes in the paradisal abodes and reaches up to the divine Presence itself—means that they already dwell within that transcendent realm more fully than within this earthly one. This helps explain the saying quoted earlier: God keeps man perpetually in that which his soul desires. Those whose hearts or spirits are in the transcendent domain are those whose consciousness is shaped, substantially and not merely conceptually, by transcendent realities. Those whose consciousness, by contrast, is shaped by worldly concerns are in this world in the sense of being enclosed by its formal limits; their orientation and imagination are trapped within a worldly frame. Their actions bear the mark of this worldliness, whereas the actions of the wise are the fruits of their immersion in a higher reality. Their outward actions in the world belie their inward transcendence of all action; they act, yet are not bound by the fruits of their action. As noted earlier, they know that action, all action, is dust, except what is purified, done sincerely, within it.

At this point the notion of the Hereafter deserves consideration within the Imam's ethical framework. The Hereafter is invoked again and again in the Imam's discourses. Is this simply a matter of holding out a heavenly reward and threatening a hellish punishment to enjoin the good and forbid the evil? Even if the answer is partly yes, there is a deeper dimension to this apparently straightforward use of divine warnings and promises as levers for moral action.

The ethical orientation here is defined not merely by belief in God but by the spiritual reality of the Hereafter as well, both objectively, in itself, and subjectively, insofar as the individual assimilates it. It thereby becomes a living dimension, a process, an orientation, a reflux, within the believer's own soul, rather than a bare belief or hope. Anticipation of the Hereafter marks every believer in principle; its realization marks the soul of the *wali Allah*, the friend of God, the saint and the sage. As noted earlier, the Imam speaks in a famous sentence that bears witness to his total immersion in the reality of which he is certain: "Were the veil to be removed, I would not increase in certitude (*law kushifal-ghita, ma azdadtu yaqinan*)." The anticipation—or better, the foretaste—of the Hereafter deepens rather than fades in proportion to one's virtue, piety, and sanctity; it does not dwindle into a mere heuristic device whose value is spent once its moral impact is achieved. The following moving passage from the *Nahj* deserves reflection in this light. What appears here is the description of a blissful vision of the reality of Paradise, not merely a rhetorical flourish in the service of moral exhortation:

If you were to cast the eye of your heart toward what is described for you of the Garden, your soul would become averse to the marvels of this world, its passions, its pleasures and its embellished scenery. And your soul would be rapt in contemplation of the swaying trees whose roots lie hidden in dunes of musk on the banks of the rivers of the Garden, and the clusters of dazzling pearls hanging down from the branches of its trees …

The highest degree of spirituality does, of course, transcend the desire for Paradise and the fear of Hell, along with the moral conduct proportioned to them. But this means that when such higher degrees of realization are attained, the soul's state can properly be called paradisal—that is, already so utterly content in the beatific presence of God that it desires nothing more. This state of soul is called in the Quran اَلنَّفْسُ الْمُطْمَئِنَّةُ, "the soul at peace in absolute certainty":

> يَٓأَيَّتُهَا ٱلنَّفْسُ ٱلْمُطْمَئِنَّةُ
>
> "O reassured soul." (Al-Fajr: 27)

The Imam frequently echoes, implicitly if not explicitly, the Quranic principle that "the Hereafter is better and more lasting":

> وَٱلْآخِرَةُ خَيْرٌۭ وَأَبْقَىٰٓ
>
> "But the Hereafter is better and more enduring." (Al-Ala: 17)

The Hereafter is not only the time at which divine warnings and promises reach their fulfillment; it is also the space in which all positive qualities—human, angelic, divine—are manifested in their fullness, those qualities that appear on earth as reflections of higher realities and that encompass and subtly penetrate the whole sphere of earthly existence. The Quran, perhaps more than any other scripture, furnishes the imagination with numerous and complex descriptions of paradisal bliss. One of the key functions of these descriptions is alluded to in the Quran itself: "Assuredly, We purify them with a pure quality, remembrance of the Abode of the Hereafter":

> إِنَّآ أَخْلَصْنَهُم بِخَالِصَةٍۢ ذِكْرَى ٱلدَّارِ
>
> "Indeed, We chose them for an exclusive quality: remembrance of the Home of the Hereafter." (Sad: 46)

The mere thought of Paradise is itself a purification of mind and heart, a way of guarding the soul against the ever-present temptation to seek its ultimate happiness and well-being in this world alone. Conversely, one's well-being here below, where action is concerned, lies in the doing of good deeds, and such well-being is proportioned to the depth of one's belief that "Whatever good ye send before for your souls, ye will find it with God, better and more tremendous as reward":

> إِنَّ رَبَّكَ يَعْلَمُ أَنَّكَ تَقُومُ أَدْنَىٰ مِن ثُلُثَىِ ٱلَّيْلِ وَنِصْفَهُۥ وَثُلُثَهُۥ وَطَآئِفَةٌۭ مِّنَ ٱلَّذِينَ مَعَكَ ۚ وَٱللَّهُ يُقَدِّرُ ٱلَّيْلَ وَٱلنَّهَارَ ۚ عَلِمَ أَن لَّن تُحْصُوهُ فَتَابَ عَلَيْكُمْ ۖ فَٱقْرَءُوا۟ مَا تَيَسَّرَ مِنَ ٱلْقُرْآنِ ۚ عَلِمَ أَن سَيَكُونُ مِنكُم مَّرْضَىٰ ۙ وَآخَرُونَ يَضْرِبُونَ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ يَبْتَغُونَ مِن فَضْلِ ٱللَّهِ ۙ وَآخَرُونَ يُقَتِلُونَ فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ ۖ فَٱقْرَءُوا۟ مَا تَيَسَّرَ مِنْهُ ۚ وَأَقِيمُوا۟ ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ وَآتُوا۟ ٱلزَّكَوٰةَ وَأَقْرِضُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ قَرْضًا حَسَنًۭا ۚ وَمَا تُقَدِّمُوا۟ لِأَنفُسِكُم مِّنْ خَيْرٍۢ تَجِدُوهُ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ هُوَ خَيْرًۭا وَأَعْظَمَ أَجْرًۭا ۚ وَٱسْتَغْفِرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ ۖ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۢ
>
> "And whatever good you put forward for yourselves, you will find it with God. It is better and greater in reward." (Al-Muzzammil: 20)

In other words, a sincere orientation toward the Hereafter is transformed into a perception of heavenly beatitude already here below, in the form of those very acts and attitudes which, being good, share the same nature as the felicity to which they lead. In this light, morally good actions and noble intentions are grasped by one who sees beyond the veil of this world as vivid prefigurations of heavenly realities, not merely as ethical prerequisites for salvation. Goodness, in other words, does not simply lead to a heavenly reward; it already participates in that reward: "Is the reward of goodness (احسان) aught but goodness?":

> هَلْ جَزَآءُ ٱلْإِحْسَنِ إِلَّا ٱلْإِحْسَنُ
>
> "Is the reward for good anything but good?" (Ar-Rahman: 60)

The Imam's saying, "The fruit of good action is like its root," can be read as an echo of the idea that the end is already present in the means that lead to it. Fitting to close is the following verse of the Quran, which also serves as an introduction to what follows:

> وَبَشِّرِ ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ أَنَّ لَهُمْ جَنَّتٍۢ تَجْرِى مِن تَحْتِهَا ٱلْأَنْهَرُ ۖ كُلَّمَا رُزِقُوا۟ مِنْهَا مِن ثَمَرَةٍۢ رِّزْقًۭا ۙ قَالُوا۟ هَذَا ٱلَّذِى رُزِقْنَا مِن قَبْلُ ۖ وَأُتُوا۟ بِهِۦ مُتَشَبِهًۭا ۖ وَلَهُمْ فِيهَآ أَزْوَجٌۭ مُّطَهَّرَةٌۭ ۖ وَهُمْ فِيهَا خَلِدُونَ
>
> "And give good tidings to those who believe and perform virtuous deeds, that for them are Gardens underneath which rivers flow. Every time they are given to eat from the fruits thereof, they say: This is what we were given to eat before. And they were given the like thereof." (Al-Baqarah: 25)

The fruits of the paradisal gardens are thus tasted already here below, in the form of all goodness, beauty, and truth, and the diverse modes of happiness that flow from them. All such experiences are so many foretastes of the ultimate beatitude in Paradise. On the ethical plane, the doing of good deeds, and, even more directly, the realization of intrinsic virtue, احسان, is therefore not simply a prerequisite for posthumous salvation, but is already a kind of deliverance, here and now: a deliverance from the imprisonment of sin on the outward plane, and from the bondage of egocentricity on the inward plane. This theme of salvation here and now, grounded in unshakeable certitude, is fundamental to the spirit of the Imam's teachings and will be explored further throughout the rest of this book. In the following chapter, the cardinal virtue of justice will be considered within Ali 's perspective, rooted as it is in احسان, a virtue defined by the Prophet as worshipping God as if one saw Him, and concretely affirmed by the Imam as real and not merely hypothetical, for God is indeed seen by the pure of heart through the verities of faith.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> A related teaching preserved in this tradition names *ihsan* as the highest form of a single
> root, ح-س-ن, in which goodness and beauty are one word — so that to do well is to make something
> beautiful. What that tradition adds is a definition this chapter's own argument reaches toward:
> *ihsan* is to worship and act as though seeing God, giving every deed its full inward beauty.
> This joins the two threads the chapter holds side by side — the intimate certainty that touches
> its divine object, and the virtue that "makes the soul beautiful." The one who acts as though
> seeing God is at once the person of certainty and the person of *ihsan*; the beauty of outward
> and inward that Ali praises is simply what such worship looks like from without and within.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 6. A Sacred Conception of Justice

A Sacred Conception of Justice

The reflections on justice that follow are not an exercise in theoretical speculation about what justice means in Ali (ع)'s letter to Malik al-Ashtar, written fourteen centuries ago. Nor are they the kind of definition found in a dictionary, where justice amounts to little more than fairness, equality, and fair play. The aim is, first of all, to bring the discussion of justice into alignment with the concept and the reality of the sacred — the reality of the sacred One, القدس. On one side, the principle of justice carries a sacred and spiritual dimension that contemporary discussion so often overlooks. On the other, a careful appreciation of what the sacred means opens a deeper understanding of the roots of justice within the divine nature itself, and a fuller awareness of why justice is necessary, both in the world and in the soul.

The central argument is this. In Ali 's worldview, the ethical orientation toward justice is immeasurably deepened once it is consciously tied to the spiritual precepts of the Islamic faith. The spirit that underlies moral rules and ethical values is strengthened by a commitment to transcendent principles — even though, or precisely because, those principles rise above the realm of action in which the rules themselves operate. The شریعۃ may be pictured as having a body and a spirit, much as a person does. Its body is made up of actions: prayer, fasting, the paying of زکوۃ. But the spirit that animates those actions rests on the nature of the divine, which is justice. To adhere to the شریعۃ is therefore to train in becoming like the divine through the principles of justice, and so action must be joined by contemplation of the divine. Here contemplation and action complete each other rather than conflict, for their union lies at the very heart of the Islamic message of تَوْحِيد, توحید — an integrating oneness that makes theory and practice one, so that a person becomes a true موحد. This unity shines with dazzling clarity in the life of the Imam, as it does in the life of the Prophet. The harmony between the two principles — contemplation and action, being and doing, theory and practice, ideal and reality — runs throughout the remarkable document that is the subject of these reflections.

These reflections stand against the background of contemporary ethical discourse and may be seen as a modest contribution, from an Islamic perspective, to the scholarly debates on virtue and ethics. In particular, they should be understood in light of one of the most serious and far-reaching critiques of modern and postmodern conceptions of virtue in general, and of justice in particular — that of Alasdair MacIntyre. Put simply, his critique holds that for several centuries ethical discourse in Western society has drifted far from its moorings in absolute values. He argues, compellingly, that debates about morality and ethics, together with their philosophical foundations, are no longer grounded in any conception of 'the good life' or of 'the Good' as such. As a result, MacIntyre holds, various shades of relativism, nihilism, utilitarianism, and opportunism have emerged in practice as the shifting bases on which ethical values are formed, if they are formed at all. Relativism holds that knowledge, truth, and morality are not absolute but relative, existing only in relation to a culture, a society, or a historical moment. Nihilism rejects all religious and moral principles and takes life to be essentially meaningless. Utilitarianism counts an action right only if it is useful or benefits a majority, so that good and bad rest on nothing more than a calculation of greater benefit or loss. And opportunism seizes whatever advantage circumstances offer, heedless of any plan or principle — the way of the manipulator, who flatters, performs, or deceives to get what he wants.

Whatever value these human-made theories occasionally yield, none of it is rooted in the divine; and so, even where they happen to mirror the values of faith, they remain baseless, without spirit — dead corpses. Virtue practiced on an ad hoc basis is not virtue properly speaking. It is a series of pragmatic adjustments to changing circumstances, made in the absence of any genuine principle of virtue. Truthfulness illustrates this. The underlying reason for always speaking the truth is twofold: that truth is the very nature of our Creator — الحق, the absolute truth — and that our purpose in this world is to take on the virtues of our Creator. If one resolves to speak the truth without grasping that reason, truthfulness will in the end become situational and self-serving, something to be compromised whenever circumstances seem to warrant it, spoken when it profits and abandoned when it costs. But once truth is seen as the nature of the divine, it becomes everlasting and eternal, indifferent to consequence, because one speaks it in emulation of the immortal nature of God. The virtue itself becomes immortal, and the need to practice it on an ad hoc basis falls away. 'We are looking,' writes MacIntyre, 'for a conception of the good which will enable us to order other goods, for a conception of the good which will enable us to extend our understanding of the purpose and content of the virtues, for a conception of the good which will enable us to understand the place of integrity and consistency in life ...'

It is at this level, in particular, that the insights of Ali and other sages of the Islamic tradition are especially relevant. MacIntyre's proposed solution to the crisis — a reformulation of an Aristotelian conception of ethics grounded in a Thomistic worldview — shares a great deal with the Islamic tradition of ethical discourse (أَخْلَاق). But one can argue further: a careful engagement with the Imam's legacy yields far more than a merely theoretical approach to the question of justice, and this in two respects. First, the Imam was himself a paragon of justice, and thus a living embodiment of this and other fundamental virtues. In his life one sees a perfect, indeed unflinching, adherence to this cardinal virtue — a life governed not by his نفس but entirely by the divine nature of God. The remarkable deeds recorded of him, at which one marvels, are the fruit of his irresistible attraction toward the nature of الحق, for his whole existence was oriented toward God. His attention rested never on the action itself but on the source of the principles that generated it; fixing upon the beauty of the divine nature and striving to take it on, he found good actions arising from him naturally and inevitably. Although this work does not enter into many details of his life and its lessons, certain incidents and events that help illustrate the point will be recalled. Secondly — and more important for the present purpose — the discourses, letters, and sayings attributed to Ali offer a rich source of reflection on the dynamic spirituality that lies at the root of a life of virtue. In them one is granted a glimpse of a way of life wholly governed by the sacred, one in which the desire for the 'good life' emerges as the fruit of an irresistible attraction toward the Good as such, which is identical to the absolutely Real, اَلْحَقّ.

In other words, one is ethically inspired by 'the Good' in direct proportion to one's existential orientation toward the Real. If moral rules rest on ethical perspectives, and those perspectives rest in turn on a conception of the 'good life', then that conception of the Good is itself drawn from the moral agent's fundamental understanding of what life means. Amyn B. Sajoo, in a recent thoughtful work on Muslim ethics, draws a careful distinction between ethics and morals. Ethical discourse, he argues, is concerned more with conceptions of 'the good', while morals pertain more specifically to rules about right and wrong. Ethics deals with the soul and the spirit of the good, the باطن, and out of ethics morals are born. 'Certainly,' he continues, 'the concepts overlap substantially in theory and practice; but it is well to bear in mind the more encompassing sense of reality that typifies ethics.' To set ethics within the wider framework of spiritual virtue — in the sense of احسان, احسان — widens the compass of both ethics and morality still further, lending each a dimension of existential depth and grounding both in a more organic and all-encompassing whole. For the spiritual life has three components: Islam, which supplies the outer aspects of morality; ایمان, which is the framework of ethics; and the union of the two, serving as their underlying principle, which issues in احسان — the good life, the beautiful life. Abstract questions of right and wrong thus come to rest on the more definitive principle that emerges in answer to the question: what is reality?

The previous chapter's discussion of the deeper meanings of divine unity bears on this. In the Imam's spiritual vision of تَوْحِيد, one perceives, according to one's capacity, an ultimate reality that not only 'has no second' but also has no moral 'opposite'. People tend to picture the world as good against evil, God against شیطان, forever thinking in opposites; yet the Imam teaches that God has no opposite at all. Satan does not stand against God, for nothing has the power to stand against God. He stands against God's creation, and his whole effort is to drive people away from their inherent divine nature by making its opposite beautiful — beautifying arrogance where God's nature is modest, lewdness where it is decent, boastfulness where it is humble. For the goodness proper to God is not the moral opposite of evil. It occupies an altogether different plane, that of اَلْحَقّ, الحق, the absolutely Real, whose only 'opposite' is unreality, or nothingness. Goodness is at one with the pure positivity of the Absolute, while evil arises solely from a negative capacity to negate the Real, the Good. The essence of all goodness and beauty, in other words, is identified with the Creator, whereas evil is a mode of the created — the corrupted state of a creation that has deviated from the divine nature. This is strongly implied in the Quranic verse of سُورَۃُ الْفَلَقِ, which calls the believer to seek refuge in God from 'the evil of that which He created' (Al-Falaq: 2):

> قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ ٱلْفَلَقِ ۱ مِنْ شَرِّ مَا خَلَقَ ۲
>
> 'Say: I seek refuge with the Lord of the daybreak, from the evil of what He has created.'

This verse is often mistranslated as 'from the evil which He created', which is wrong both theologically and lexically. The expression 'min sharri ma khalaqa', مِن شَرِّ مَا خَلَقَ, plainly refers to the evil that derives from creation, not to creation as such. In theological terms, God creates morally free agents, and it is they who are the authors of evil in creation; when creation, by its own free will, chooses to negate the divine nature of its Creator, evil is born. God may be said to 'create' all actions only in the metaphysical sense that He is the source of all being, and so of all modes within being, of which action is one; He cannot be said to have created evil as such, but only the conditions within which evil arises. Evil is thus the negation of the Good, which is at one with the Real, and as such it has no ultimate ontological principle. It is only a temporary state of deviation from that pure and divine nature — an illusion witnessed by the 'eye of the heart'.

The verse, then, may be understood as a call to seek refuge in the Real from the unreal, since between goodness and evil there is no common measure in being. Goodness can be opposed only by the appearance of evil — an appearance the 'eye of the heart' grasps as illusion, that eye which truly 'sees' God through 'the verities of faith'. This vision of the merely relative reality of evil, far from breeding indifference toward it, greatly strengthens the spiritual resolve to overcome it, for the moral effort to be good intensifies in proportion to one's conviction of accountability to that goodness. That conviction is in turn carried inward, from the mind to the heart, by the certainty that goodness is 'more real' than evil — a certainty itself nourished by the spiritual vision of the good as the Real. To perceive the radical incommensurability between good and evil, therefore, leads not to indifference in the face of evil but to a more existentially compelling orientation toward the good. In the Imam's perspective, the notions of 'right' and 'rightful due' gathered within the meaning of haqq, حق, are charged with an aura of absoluteness. To understand what is right leads to the commitment to fulfill one's obligations; what is rightfully due is the restoration of reality through the removal of illusory evil; and both are equally infused with reality, which is justice. The moral notions of rectitude and propriety are expressions of a principle that transcends morality, a principle belonging to ultimate reality. One's commitment to fulfilling one's obligations — to giving every being its rightful due — becomes charged, likewise, with a powerful sense of reality, and the resolve to be just is made more absolute by the very absoluteness proper to the Real.

This duty to give each and every being its due connects to the most explicit definition of justice the Imam gives: وَضَعَ الشَّئُی حَقَّ مَحَلِّہِ, 'Justice puts everything in its right place.' One is 'just' in so far as one gives everything its proper due, rendering the haqq owed to each person — indeed to each and every thing in existence; and the capacity to be 'just' in this demanding, universal sense grows in proportion to one's attunement to اَلْحَقّ, the Real. As the previous chapter makes clear, this spiritual conception of virtue not only takes in one's duty to all but engages all that one is. One must be good before one can do good, and the only way 'to be' is to be at one with the Real — that which alone truly 'is'.

The Imam's remark on justice calls to mind the more famous definition of Plato: ...we have laid down, as a universal principle, that everyone ought to perform the one function in the community for which his nature best suited him... that principle, or some form of it, is justice. There are indeed many remarkable parallels between Plato's *Republic* and the Imam's letter to Malik al-Ashtar. Some of the more obvious correspondences deserve attention, along with the way the Imam's explicit spirituality illuminates what stays implicit in Plato's philosophy: namely, how one may actually attain the vision of the Real, which Plato calls 'gazing upon the Good'. It is this contemplation of the Good as such that serves as the source of one's capacity to act ethically at every level, for it lets one take the Good 'as a pattern for the right ordering of the state and of the individual'.

Platonic and Neoplatonic ideas of justice have left a well-documented mark on the way ethics came to be discussed in Islam. In that discussion, justice is not one virtue standing beside others; it is the perfection, the sum, of all the rest — above all of the three chief Platonic virtues: temperance, courage, and wisdom. These belong, in turn, to the appetitive, the spirited, and the rational parts of the soul. When every part of the soul is filled out by its proper virtue and stands in its right place, the soul may be called "just."

What has not received enough attention is how closely Plato's chief means of reaching the vision of the Good — anamnesis — corresponds, at the spiritual level, to the central place of dhikr in Islam. Both words can be rendered in English as "remembrance." And how is this justice achieved? What releases its power is precisely this remembrance: the recollection of ultimate reality and of unchanging principles as they apply at their several levels. It is not a matter of learning something new, but of calling back to mind principles already present in the very substance of the intellect — principles by which all things can be understood, and which are rooted, finally, in the Sovereign Good itself. Such remembrance is the ground on which ethical thought rests, both as knowledge and as reasoning. That its importance has been largely overlooked is a symptom of the modern habit of cutting ethics off from spirituality, treating each as though it were sealed away from the other. Ethics is still taken seriously, as a branch of philosophy and a discipline in its own right; spirituality, by contrast, is too often waved aside as beside the point in serious scholarship and consigned to the realm of the imaginary, the vague, and the mythological. This pushing of spirituality to the margins of supposedly serious discourse is closely bound up with the shrunken view of knowledge that has increasingly marked Western thought since the Renaissance — a narrowing that goes hand in hand with the steady draining away of the sacred dimension of life.

In the Islamic tradition, ethics and spirituality are closely bound together. This shows nowhere more clearly than in the Sufi tradition, which is now often, and rightly, called the ihsani tradition. The word احسان is taken here in the sense the Prophet gave it in a saying of central importance about the three foundations of the Islamic way. That saying is known as "the hadith of Gabriel," because the man who put the questions — though the Companions present did not know it — was the angel Gabriel (جبرئیل) in human form. He came up to the Prophet as he sat among his Companions and said:

'O Muhammad, tell me about submission (الْإِسْلَام),' يَا Muhammad، أَخْبِرْنِي عَنِ الْإِسْلَامِ.

The Prophet replied: الإِسْلاَمُ أَنْ تَشْهَدَ أَنْ لاَ إِلهَ إِلاَ اللهُ وَأَنَّ Muhammadاً رَسُولُ اللهِ، وَتُقِيمَ الصَّلاَةَ، وَتُؤْتِيَ al-zakat، وَتَصُومَ رَمَضَانَ، وَتَحُجَّ الْبَيْتَ إِنِ اسْتَطَعْتَ إِلَيْهِ سَبِيلاً, 'الْإِسْلَام is to testify that there is no god but God and Muhammad is the messenger of God, to perform the prayers, to pay the poor-due (الزَّكَاةَ), to fast in Ramadan, and to make the pilgrimage to the House if you are able to do so.'

'You have spoken truly (صَدَقْتَ),' Gabriel said. The Companions, who saw only a man and did not know he was an angel, were astonished that he should question the Prophet and then declare that he had spoken truly.

'Then tell me about faith (الإِيْمَانِ),' he said, فَأَخْبِرْنِي عَنِ الإِيْمَانِ.

The Prophet said: أَنْ تُؤْمِنَ بِاللهِ، وَمَلاَئِكَتِهِ، وَكُتُبِهِ، وَرُسُلِهِ، وَالْيَوْمِ الآخِرِ، وَتُؤْمِنَ بِالْقَدَرِ خَيْرِهِ وَشَرِّهِ, 'It is to believe in God, His angels, His books, His messengers, and the Last Day, and to believe in divine destiny, both the good and the evil thereof.'

'You have spoken truly (صَدَقْتَ),' he said. 'Then tell me about virtue (الإِحْسَانِ),' he said, فَأَخْبِرْنِي عَنِ الإِحْسَانِ.

He said: أَنْ تَعْبُدَ اللهَ كَأَنَّكَ تَرَاهُ، فَإِنْ لَمْ تَكُنْ تَرَاهُ فَإِنَّهُ يَرَاكَ, 'It is to worship God as though you see Him, and if you see Him not, yet truly He sees you.'

The word احسان can also be translated literally as "doing what is beautiful," since its root (حَسَنَ) is bound up first of all with beauty, حَسَنَ. In Islamic ethical discourse, virtue and vice are often called حَسَنَ and qubh (قُبْح) — beauty and ugliness. In the end it is the vision of divine beauty that awakens virtue, which is beauty of soul, just as turning away from the divine qualities is what leaves the soul ugly (قُبْح). It is no surprise, then, that — broadly speaking — while the jurists, the فُقَهَاءُ, have been the specialists in outward action, and the theologians the specialists in formal belief, it fell to the mystics to be the guardians of spiritual virtue, of "making beautiful." And it is no accident that the great majority of artists in the Islamic world — calligraphers, painters, poets, musicians, and the rest — have been practicing Sufis.

The Prophet's definition of احسان as "worshipping God as if one could see Him" makes plain the crucial bond between worship and virtue — between spirituality and ethics, between devotion to the Creator and goodness toward His creatures. One cannot be had without the other. This bond lies at the very heart of the spiritual tradition in Islam. And it is the denial and betrayal of that tradition by some modern Muslims that, more than any other single cause, lies behind the atrocities now being committed by Muslims in the name of Islam.

Both the theory and the practice of ethics have always been part of the very fabric of Sufism. Even some who wrote in more philosophical and less openly spiritual terms — al-Farabi among them — were known to be practicing Sufis as well. The figure in whom spirituality and ethics meet most powerfully is, of course, al-Ghazali, whose great Revival of the Sciences of Religion stands as a monument to what Islam's deepest spirit does to the ethical temper of the believing soul. He has rightly been called the greatest thinker on ethics in Islam; and since his thought is shaped through and through by Sufism, this by itself testifies to how closely Sufism and ethics are joined. It would be no exaggeration to say that most treatises written by Sufis are in fact treatises on "ethics" — provided ethics is understood not only as أَخْلَاق in the narrow sense, but also as the fruit of احسان, of spiritual virtue. The various spiritual stations (مَقَامَات) the Sufis describe often bear the names of particular virtues: patience (صَبْرٌ), gratitude (شُكْرٌ), contentment (رِضَاءٌ), generosity (كَرَم), and so on.

The thread running through all these stations is a transforming awareness of God — the principle of being and acting "as if you could see God," of carrying and composing oneself in the constant awareness of standing in the divine presence, for even if one cannot see Him, "He sees you." The call of احسان thus lies at the very root of a life of virtue in the spiritual world of Islam. In his sermons in the نَھْجُ الْبَلَاغَۃِ, Ali turns his hearers again and again to the "vision" of God, in whatever measure, as the true ground of a virtuous life, and warns again and again against its opposite — the all-absorbing vision of oneself — as the source of every vice. On the one hand the Imam says, "Know that your every action is dependent upon your prayer," that is, upon one's way of "gazing upon the Good," to put it in Platonic terms. On the other, he warns against vanity, conceit, and the lavish praise of oneself by others, for these are Satan's (شَیْطَان) surest openings for effacing the virtue, the احسان, of the virtuous (المُحسنین). Set against each other here are two visions. The true, objective vision of اَلْحَقّ, the Real, is reached by effacing oneself in prayer, for in the prostration (سَجْدَۃ) of prayer one effaces one's own ego, one's nafs (نَفْس). Opposed to it stands the distorted, self-centered vision of the ego, swollen by pride — which is exactly what Iblis (اِبْلِیس) desires. The first leads to justice, putting all things in their proper place, and, most fundamentally, the ego itself; the second leads to every injustice that flows from self-aggrandizement, fanaticism and bigotry among them. For Satan, the very embodiment of pride and disobedience, is described as "the leader of the fanatics and the forerunner of the proud" (اِمَامُ الْمُتَعَصِّبِینَ وَ سَلَفُ الْمُسْتَکْبِرِینَ, imam al-mutaassibin wa salaf al-mustakbirin). Bigotry is intolerance toward those who hold different opinions; and fanatical bigotry is an especially fierce form of injustice, for it carries that poisonous prejudice which bends the objective demands of justice to the supposed privileges of one's own self — whether that self is defined as the individual, the family, the clan, the tribe, the nation, or the religion.

The Imam's letter appointing Malik al-Ashtar as his governor of Egypt appears as letter 52 in most editions of the نَھْجُ الْبَلَاغَۃِ. Across the centuries it has served as a source of inspiration, read as an ideal constitution for Islamic government. In its fairly detailed account of the duties and rights of the ruler, of the various officers of the state, and of the main classes of society, it fills out the more general framework of principles set down in the famous "Constitution of Medina" that the Prophet dictated. Yet the counsel in the letter goes far beyond the bounds usually set by political or legal texts on government. Though it is addressed in the first place to the ruler or governor, much of its counsel in fact concerns ethical principles that hold everywhere, and so it speaks as much to the governed as to those who govern. The letter is as much a statement of ethics as of politics — not a worked-out "system" or theory of ethics, to be sure, but a moving expression of the spiritual ethos the Imam embodied and radiated. That ethos flows straight from the sources of the Islamic revelation, and so it lays bare the roots of virtue and ethics within a world governed by the all-embracing principle of revelation. The document can therefore be read as a commentary on that revelation as well — one that, in turn, draws its readers to look more deeply into the meanings held within the revealed texts. Malik al-Ashtar was a long-standing and devoted follower of the Imam, spoken of as one of his "right-hand" men. Muawiya, the Imam's chief opponent in the civil war that dominated his caliphate, was among those who used the phrase — notably on hearing that his plot to have Malik poisoned had succeeded before Malik could take up his post as governor of Egypt. Tabari reports Muawiya as saying: "Ali ibn Abi Talib had two right hands. One of them was cut off at Siffin, and the other today." The Imam, for his part, met the news of Malik's assassination with these words: "Malik, and what was Malik! By God, were he a mountain, he would be the solitary one soaring above all others, and were he a rock, he would have been impenetrably solid. No horse could ascend such a mountain, no bird could fly up to it."

The Imam's definition of justice stands at the center of what follows: "Justice puts everything in its right place." In everyday speech, justice means little more than fairness, equality, and fair play — a workable account, but a merely formal one. The idea at work here is of an altogether different order. It joins justice to the sacred, to القدس, the All-Holy, and finds the very roots of justice within the divine nature itself. What ties this conception of justice to the sacred is the demand that one first put one's relationship with God in its right place; everything else follows from that spiritual demand. The same order of priority is set out plainly in an important line of the Imam's letter to Malik: "Be just with God and be just with people [giving them what is their due] from yourself."

Being just with God means, among other things, conforming as far as one can to His own nature — which is not merely the source of justice but is justice. The weight falls on that word is. Justice is not one attribute among His many, ranged beside His mercy, His wrath, and His love; it is the very essence of His nature, and all His other qualities flow from it. His mercy is an expression of His justice, and so are His anger, His love, His creating, and His destroying. "I bear witness," says the Imam in one of his sermons, "that He is Justice (عَدْل) and He acts justly."

It might be objected that there is a deep gulf between justice as a divine reality and justice as a human act. In God, justice means that everything is in its right place; in man, justice means the effort to put everything in its right place — an unchanging metaphysical quality on the divine side, a living act of will on the human side. To this the reply is that man's moral effort to act justly, in a world where things are not in their right place, springs from an inborn spiritual leaning toward justice. Just action thus expresses the principle of justice, which, in man as in God, is that unchanging principle by which everything stands in its right place. As divine action in creation, judgment, and elsewhere expresses absolute justice, so in man just action expresses not only a moral, willed, and intellectual effort to bring justice about, but also a spiritual kinship with the ultimate nature of reality — with things as they truly are in God, and therefore as they ought to be here on earth.

Applied to state and society, one of the plainest consequences of this conception of justice is that rulers must be pious and upright. As the Imam says in another sermon: "The most momentous of the reciprocal rights that God has made obligatory is the right of the ruler over the subjects, and the right of the subjects over the ruler, a reciprocal obligation which God has ordained for them." He then makes a point wholly indispensable to any account of good governance in Islam: "The subjects will not be righteous except through the righteousness of the rulers, and the rulers will not be righteous except through the uprightness of the subjects. So if the subjects render to the ruler his rightful due and he renders them theirs, then will right be honored among them, the ways of religion will be upheld, and the banners of justice will be erected." This insistence on the ruler's piety is, as one would expect, central to the Imam's message to his governor; but it involves far more than a simple call to keep the formal rules of Islamic law.

The whole of the Imam's life can be seen as a quest to conform as perfectly as he could to the divine nature, and to this quality of justice above all. The principle has far too many sides — social, economic, political, legal — as they appear in his life and teachings to be treated in a short space. One defining moment in his caliphate shows the rigor with which he held to justice whatever it cost. The revolt of Muawiya, and the civil war into which the young Muslim empire was thereby plunged, can be said to have begun not at Siffin but in Medina. When Ali took up the caliphate, his cousin Ibn al-Abbas advised him to confirm all of 'Uthman's governors in their posts for the time being, and then, once the new order was secure, to remove them. The Imam's reply to this proposal was reported thus: "I do not doubt that this would be best for the sake of reconciliation in this world. But there is my obligation to the Truth (الَّذِي يَلْزَمُنِي مِنَ الْحَقِّ) and my knowledge of Uthman's governors, so, by God, I shall never appoint one of them."

And so the die was cast: the Imam dismissed Muawiya along with all of his predecessor's governors. The Imam saw clearly that Ibn al-Abbas' proposal had every chance of working; he knew that all Muawiya wanted was worldly power, and that Muawiya would have been content to govern Syria had the Imam allowed it. Commentators have taken this episode — which made the governors' revolt all but inevitable — as evidence of Ali 's want of political shrewdness and his stubbornness. It should rather be understood as an expression of his disdain for worldly ease when that ease can be bought only at the price of essential principles. As he says in one of his sayings: "People renounce no part of their religion for the sake of rectifying their world without God opening them up to something worse than it" — that is, worse than the very defect they were trying to set right.

Clearly, to keep in power governors whose corruption was beyond both doubt and remedy would have amounted, for the Imam, to "renouncing" a part of his religion — for religion without justice is not religion. To support unjust governors, even as a tactical move, was a compromise of religious principle simply unthinkable to him. This attitude ran through countless episodes of his life, all of which show that the principles set down in the letter to Malik al-Ashtar were far from merely theoretical.

What follows examines one particular dimension of justice — political on the surface, ethical in its outward expression, but spiritual in its depth: the justice of the political ruler, understood against its most clearly definable opposite, which is injustice, tyranny, oppression, and corruption. A deep taking-in of the Imam's spirituality can lend a real, existential power to the moral effort to live justly — a power that is missing when justice is conceived only in individual or horizontal terms, that is, as a secular moral philosophy cut off from religious or spiritual principles. The crucial place of devotion and worship, both formal and beyond-formal, belongs to this same inquiry. Contemplation of the divine Reality is the wellspring from which whole and undivided virtue flows freely and without end — a point brought into sharper relief by comparison with Plato's "gazing" upon the Sovereign Good as the source of all virtue.
