The inquiry that follows takes its bearings from a single document above all others: the letter of Ali to Malik al-Ashtar. It is there that a sacred conception of justice is set out most fully, and it is from its key passages that the present analysis draws.

Around that letter stand the Imam's other writings. His further letters, his sermons, and his sayings are gathered here as they bear on the same theme, extending and deepening what the letter to Malik sets down. Treated in this way, justice is not read out of one text in isolation but heard across the whole body of his counsel.

Beyond the words of Ali himself, the analysis turns to certain traditions of the Prophet and to verses of the Quran. These are brought forward not as ornament but as light, for they illuminate the very themes the letter raises and lay bare the ground on which its conception of justice rests.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> The chapter turns on *tawhid* — the "integrating oneness" that binds contemplation to action and
> makes one a *muwahhid*. A related teaching preserved in this tradition clarifies the word
> itself: it is built from a root meaning "to be one," and as a form-II noun it names not the mere
> counting of God as a single item but the deliberate *act* of declaring Him one — a oneness that
> admits no partner, division, or second. That grammatical shape is worth holding onto here,
> because it mirrors exactly what the chapter argues: unity is not something passively observed
> but something a person actively enacts, joining theory to practice until being and doing become
> one. Read this way, becoming a *muwahhid* is itself the work of justice the chapter describes —
> the willed alignment of the soul with the undivided nature of the divine.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 7. Justice and the Tyrant's Pride

The letter is addressed to one of the Imam's closest and most trusted companions. For that reason, its warnings about injustice and oppression carry a very precise force, over and above the universal theme they express. They apply to everyone who lives under Islam and its laws, and who begins with every right intention, yet remains vulnerable to the temptations of political power. They reckon fully with an unfortunate but proven truth, caught in the maxim that power corrupts — or, in the Imam's own words, 'he who rules, appropriates (مَنْ مَلَكَ اسْتَأْثَرَ).'

The Imam's understanding of justice plainly reaches beyond both its own historical moment and the formal boundaries of the Islamic faith. The letter can be read as speaking not only to those who wish to govern by just principles, but to all who, though not corrupt at the outset, are corruptible — a class that takes in the overwhelming majority of humanity.

A note of somber realism sounds from the very opening of the letter. Man's potential for evil, once he is left to his own devices and cut off from divine grace, dominates the mood of the preamble. Hence its emphasis on abiding by God's wajibat, the religious obligations binding on every Muslim. Malik is instructed to set obedience to God above all else, 'to abide by what He has commanded in His Book ... for no one prospers except through abiding by them, and no one is wretched except through repudiating and neglecting them'. The preamble is introductory, but by no means perfunctory. Obedience to the divine commands is to be understood not merely as legally binding but as spiritually liberating. Such obedience plays a crucial part in freeing a person from the tyranny of the 'soul which incites to evil' (النَّفْسُ الْأَمَّارَةُ بِالسُّوءِ) — to which the Imam turns at once after calling Malik to obedience: 'For truly, the soul incites to evil, unless God has mercy.' This sentence is almost identical to the words of the Prophet Joseph in the Quran (Yusuf: 53), and it matters greatly in setting the scene for the ethical and socio-political instructions that follow. The relationship that determines the spiritual substance of the soul — and all the more its moral conduct — is the relationship between the human soul and divine grace. Without grace the soul inclines toward evil; with God's help it is freed from its base inclinations and guided back to its true, original nature, its fitra, to which the Quran refers in the verse that follows:

So set thy purpose for religion with unswerving devotion: the nature of God (firat Allah), according to which He hath created man. There is no altering God's creation. That is the right religion (ٱلدِّينُ ٱلْقَيِّمُ), but most men know not. (Ar-Rum: 30)

The present state of the fallen soul, then, is called to give way to the ideal state of original human nature. That nature is both the origin and the end of the human condition, and at once the true substance of every human soul — always accessible in principle, even if for most people it stays clouded by fallen nature in practice. The quest to revive this original nature turns on the complementary work of the human intellect and divine revelation. In the first sermon of the Nahj, the Imam states that God sent mankind 'His messengers, dispatching prophets in succession, in order to claim from them [His creatures] the fulfillment of the covenant of His creation; to remind them of His forgotten graces; to remonstrate with them through communication [of His revelation]; to unearth for them the buried treasures of the intellects (دَفْعُ الْعُقُولِ).'

These 'buried treasures' can be identified with the original, God-given knowledge that belongs to the fitra. Out of this whole human nature, in complete harmony with the divine nature, all the essential virtues flow freely and unhindered. 'Let your most beloved treasure be the treasure of virtuous acts', the Imam writes. Virtue will indeed be the 'most beloved treasure' of one who reverses the natural inclinations of the self-centered soul and sets a supernatural orientation toward its own deepest nature — and by that very act, toward the divine Reality that furnishes the foundations in being of all authentic virtue. For اَلْحَقّ, the Real, is not only the deepest foundation of virtue; it is the true substance of every positive quality, and, in the end, of every single thing that exists. As one of the most celebrated supplications attributed to the Imam puts it:

By Thy tremendousness (بِعَظَمَتِكَ), which has filled all things ... by Thy Names which have filled the foundations of all things, by Thy knowledge which encompasses all things, by the light of Thy face, through which all things are illumined ...

From this vantage point, every virtue can be seen as the reflection, on the human plane, of a divine attribute — what is called a divine 'Name'. Hence a theme running through the whole ihsani tradition, the path of spiritual excellence (احسان) that the Prophet defined as worshipping God as though one could see Him, knowing that even if one does not see Him, He sees him. That theme finds expression in the saying: 'Assume the character traits of God (تَخَلَّقُوا بِأَخْلَاقِ اللهِ).'

The link between human virtue and divine reality is wholly indispensable in the Imam's perspective. Subjectively, the practice of virtue draws down the matching divine quality; objectively, the divine quality is the source and — in accordance with true تَوْحِيد — the actual substance of all human virtue. Every positive quality, both in itself and in its manifestation, belongs to God, the One who 'has no partner' theologically and 'has no second' ontologically. This mystical grasp of the principle of تَوْحِيد is strongly implied in the Quranic verse, 'And thou threw not when thou didst throw [a handful of dust], but God it was who threw' (Al-Anfal: 17). These divine qualities are not to be seen as purely transcendent, as though they were wholly one with the divine essence and utterly beyond the soul's reach. They are also to be grasped as exemplars, as patterns of perfection for the soul to imitate as fully as it can. The goal is to become as much 'like' God as possible; yet this growth can unfold only within the bounds of one's servitude to Him. A person is generous for the sake of God, because God is الکریم, the Generous; he is compassionate and merciful because He is الرحمن الرحیم — never out of any claim that 'I am generous' or 'I am merciful', for the aim is to imitate God's qualities, not to claim to embody Him in person. The individual must therefore be effaced before God in order to be truly virtuous, and must at the same time share in and embody the very qualities he wishes God to show him. He stays lowly, humble, and effaced throughout, certain that no measure of virtue or intelligence can ever lessen or match the qualities of God — there being no proportion between the Absolute and the relative, the Creator and the created. His effort to embody these qualities is strengthened to the degree that he apprehends the divine attributes existentially, through intuition, rather than merely through reason, in theory. It is like gazing into a mirror: what one sees is a reflection, but an inverted one. The aim is to catch, on the human plane, the inverted reflection of divine transcendence. For man is at once خَلِیفَۃُ اللَّہِ, God's vicegerent upon the earth, and عِبَادُ اللّٰہِ, His slave. The awareness of servitude keeps him, even while he represents God, from slipping into rivalry with Him in grandeur; the active commitment to representing God's attributes guards him from the spiritual paralysis of neglecting his duties toward the world. Such is the meaning of the verse, يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ أَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ إِلَى ٱللَّهِۖ وَٱللَّهُ هُوَ ٱلْغَنِىُّ ٱلْحَمِيدُ, 'O mankind, you are the destitute before God, and God is the Self-sufficient, the Praiseworthy' (Fatir: 15). There must be a synthesis between the active human virtues and the spiritual awareness of one's nothingness before the divine Reality. The bond between intelligence and humility deserves emphasis here: intellectual perception of the divine attributes produces humility, and the effacement of the soul in turn draws attention away from the ego and toward the transcendent reality as the source of all virtue and truth.

The notion of the 'soul which incites to evil', and the Quranic story of Joseph (يُوسُفُ) in which it arises, deserves closer attention. The Imam could assume that his readers knew this context well, and he clearly meant it to stay before the mind of Malik, and of anyone else reading this letter on governance.

Joseph gives this description of man's base inclinations after his innocence of every charge has been established before the Egyptian king. Even so, he says, 'I do not exonerate myself. Truly, the soul incites to evil, unless my Lord has mercy.' On the two occasions when he faced the seductive temptations of Zulaykha, the Quran makes clear that he resisted not through his own moral resources but through the grace of God.

This theme of human helplessness before gratification that is alluring but immoral is strongly implied in the Imam's repetition of the Quranic phrase, 'Truly the soul incites to evil, unless God has mercy.' Here, then, are the two poles that draw the ordinary soul, the soul not yet 'at peace': its own base inclinations toward the negative possibilities of the fleeting world of appearances, and the higher pull toward the grace of God, which draws the soul back toward the pure positivity of ultimate reality. The sheer sharpness of the contrast within this powerful Quranic sentence quickens an awareness of the soul's absolute and unconditional need of God in every circumstance. It is a most fitting way to open a letter of instruction to a man about to be given authority over a vast and wealthy country, where temptations lie in wait on every side. It stands as a solemn reminder of the soul's openness to allurement on every plane, gross and subtle alike — an openness the soul cannot neutralize by itself, but only through the mercy and grace of God. And yet, to return to Joseph's words, the 'vision of the evidence' of God implies active discernment and, as its moral consequence, firm self-control. Human effort to avoid sin therefore does not contradict the need for grace in the attainment of virtue; rather, it expresses a grace already given, already present within the conscientious soul. The very capacity of the human will to strive for virtue is itself a grace already there, set within the spiritual constitution, or fitra — though a grace that must be made present, or actualized, through the sincere effort to draw upon this God-given power.

How this grace enters the soul as a shaping force of moral and spiritual life becomes clearer later in the Imam's letter. For now, the negative side of the question calls for more elaboration, since the ruler's ever-present temptation to abuse his power and commit injustice and tyranny deserves more than a passing mention. One might argue that how to refrain from injustice is a far harder and subtler question than its positive counterpart, how to act with justice. The Imam sets out the positive rules of political governance directly and plainly; what is far less plain is whether any holder of office can actually stay faithful to those rules when the temptations of power press upon him. Laws can be framed to punish corruption, but what no law can supply is the will, or the capacity, to stay incorruptible before the blandishments of political authority. This helps explain why the Imam's exhortations take on so markedly negative a cast in the early part of the letter — the command to 'break the soul in the face of passionate desires', to 'dominate your inclination', to 'withhold yourself from that which is not lawful for you'. It also explains why the moral demand of justice must be set firmly within a lived spirituality. Without that grounding, commands to be fair, just, and honest lack the existential quality that turns them from abstract propositions into unquestionable realities, and that turns formal obedience to rules imposed from outside into a spiritual kinship with the source of those rules.

Living by just precepts then stops being a matter of compliance with something imposed from outside and becomes instead the moral companion of one's inner being. In other words, a whole approach to justice leads to an identification with what is right, with اَلْحَقّ itself, rather than merely to the doing of what is right. There is a shift of consciousness from doing to being, from action to contemplation — yet without any weakening or undermining of action. On the contrary, action comes to reflect, on its own plane, the divine object of contemplation, and so becomes more effective, not less. An orientation toward the Real lends a deeper degree of reality both to what one does and to one's own mode of being. One does what is right insofar as one is real; the Islamic spiritual tradition names this process of realization تَحْقِيق, 'making real'. One's ethical conduct is 'made real' through spirituality, and spirituality, in turn, is expressed by, and made more concrete through, the practice of virtue.

The Imam then issues a stern warning: وَلاَ تَنْصِبَنَّ نَفْسَكَ لِحَرْبِ اللهِ, 'Do not set your soul up for war with God.' One may well ask what this means, and what kinds of acts or attitudes place the soul at war with God. The phrase seems to reach deeper than plain disobedience, deeper than the many breaches of religious obligation named at the letter's outset. That is the most obvious sense of 'fighting' against God, but it does not rule out subtler forms of opposition to divine reality — attitudes that may go along with outward acts of obedience yet contradict their very spirit. One example is the cardinal vice of pride (takabbur, or kibr), against which the Imam warns again and again: the inner state in which a person feels superior and treats others highhandedly, its signs so plain in his conduct that they cannot be hidden. A ruler's pride is a theft of a divine attribute, a seizing of a greatness that belongs to God alone, who declares: الْكِبْرِيَاءُ رِدَائِي، وَالْعَظَمَةُ إِزَارِي، فَمَنْ نَازَعَنِي وَاحِدًا مِنْهُمَا، قَذَفْتُهُ فِي النَّارِ, 'Pride is My cloak and greatness is My robe; whoever contends with Me over either of them, I shall cast him into the Fire.' God alone has the right to takabbur, and God alone may be called, without offense, al-Mutakabbir, 'the Proud'. To vaunt oneself before others is, in effect, to defy God's authority inwardly, and so to deny in an unspoken way His unique and incomparable majesty. This denial of something inseparable from divine sovereignty can itself be understood as a kind of 'war' with God, for a subject who openly defies his king is, in effect, waging war against him. In short, enmity toward transcendent reality is the invisible substance that pervades all pride, and every act of arrogance or oppression a ruler commits against those beneath him.

The paradox here can be grasped only through a sense of spiritual values: an attitude of superiority toward those 'below' is in fact an act of rebellion against that which is 'above'. An injustice toward creatures does two things at once — it draws down upon its perpetrator the justice of the Creator, and it is, in itself, outright opposition to the Creator. What looks like a horizontal vice — 'horizontal' in the sense that it belongs to this world — is in reality a spiritual disorder. The outward immorality of corruption is only the symptom of an inner spiritual malaise: an unspoken denial that the source of all power and authority is absolute, and hence unique, and a forgetting of the truth expressed in the Quranic verse, لِّمَنِ ٱلْمُلْكُ ٱلْيَوْم لِلَّهِ ٱلْوَحِدِ ٱلْقَهَّارِ, 'To whom belongeth the dominion this day? To God, the One, the All-Conquering' (Ghafir: 16). This truth should sound constantly in the ears of every ruler, 'this day' understood as every day, not only the Day of Judgment. In the Imam's sermon known as al-Qasia (القاسیۃ), 'the Abasement', so named because it humbles the proud, Iblis (اِبْلِیس) is described as the first of those who 'competed with God' in grandeur, and thus as the very embodiment of pride and arrogance. When he was commanded to prostrate before Adam, Iblis refused, as the Quran recounts:

إِذْ قَالَ رَبُّكَ لِلْمَلَٓئِكَةِ إِنِّى خَلِقٌۢ بَشَرًۭا مِّن طِينٍۢ ۝ فَإِذَا سَوَّيْتُهُۥ وَنَفَخْتُ فِيهِ مِن رُّوحِى فَقَعُوا۟ لَهُ سَجِدِينَ ۝ فَسَجَدَ ٱلْمَلَٓئِكَةُ كُلُّهُمْ أَجْمَعُونَ ۝ إِلَّآ إِبْلِيسَ ٱسْتَكْبَرَ وَكَانَ مِنَ ٱلْكَفِرِينَ, 'When your Lord said to the angels: Truly I am going to create man from clay; and when I have fashioned him and breathed into him of My spirit, then fall down before him prostrate. So the angels prostrated themselves, all of them together; save Iblis: he was proud and was one of the disbelievers' (Sad: 71-74).

He refused because 'vehement passion' overcame him. He judged himself superior to Adam on the grounds that he was made of fire while Adam was made of clay, خَلَقْتَنِی مِنْ نَارٍ وَ خَلَقْتَہُ مِنْ طِینٍ. In taking pride in this presumed superiority, and thereby rebelling against God's command, Iblis becomes not only the first 'enemy of God' but also 'the leader of the bigots and the forerunner of the proud' (imam al-muta assibin wa salaf al-mustakbirin, اِمَامُ الْمُتَعَصِّبِینَ وَ سَلَفُ الْمُسْتَکْبِرِینَ). The Imam warns his listeners: 'Beware lest he infect you with his disease' through his insinuations, whisperings, and machinations. He urges them: 'Be resolute in placing self-abasement over your heads, casting self-glorification beneath your feet, and removing pride from your necks. And take up humility as the fortified watchtower between you and your enemy.'

Humility is the chief weapon in the war against Iblis, whose own aim is the reverse: to instil pride. For pride is not only the worst of vices but the poison that infects and destroys every virtue. A virtue is turned into a vice the moment pride accompanies it. 'The sin that grieves you is better, in the sight of God, than the virtue that makes you proud.' Bound up with this is the point made earlier about the dangers of excessive praise and the conceit it breeds, which are among 'Satan's most reliable opportunities to efface the virtue of one who is virtuous'.

Taken together, these statements form a commentary on the Quranic account of Iblis' disgrace. They grasp a simple act of disobedience at its spiritual root, and in doing so bring home the ever-present danger of falling prey to that 'hidden' form of شِرْك (polytheism) to which the Prophet refers in these words: الشِّرْكُ فِي هَذِهِ الْأُمَّةِ akhfa مِنْ دَبِيبِ النَّمْلِ عَلَی حَجَرِ الْأَسْوَدِ فِی لِیلَۃِ الظُّلْمَا — 'The creeping of شِرْك in my community is more hidden (أَخْفَى) than the creeping of a black ant over a hard rock on a dark night.' شِرْك is commonly taken to mean the worship of material idols, شِرْکُ الْجَلِیِّ, its open and manifest form; but it must be understood more subtly as شِرْکُ الْخَفِیِّ, the hidden form — false gods concealed within one's intentions, attitudes, and orientations, all the elements of hidden pride, vanity, ostentation, and pretentiousness. As the Imam says, 'Know that the slightest pretension (رِيَاء — ریا) is polytheism.' To act for the sake of earning others' praise is to act for others rather than for God, and so to attribute partners to God without saying so: 'Whoso acts for other than God, God entrusts him to those for whose sake he acted.'

In this light the meaning of Iblis' disobedience can be understood more deeply. It is the outward moral consequence of an inner spiritual malaise, rooted in that pride which is tantamount to 'hidden polytheism' and which brings a disgraceful humiliation in its train. Iblis is disgraced because he disobeyed God, and he disobeyed God on account of pride. Pride is therefore, in and of itself, a disgrace — a state of 'dis-grace', a perversion of the human condition that carries within it the seed of its own inexorable inversion. 'Do you not see how God humiliated him through his pride?' the Imam asks. To be proud is already, in reality, a form of humiliation, for to make oneself great is tantamount to self-destruction.

Returning to the pride of those in power, the Imam sets out concisely the train of thought that starts up in the mind of a ruler as he steps onto the slippery slope toward oppression: وَلاَ تَقُولَنَّ: إِنِّي مُؤَمَّرٌ! آمُرُ! فَأُطَاعُ! إِنَّ ذلِكَ إِدْغَالٌ فِي الْقَلْبِ، وَمَنْهَكَةٌ لِلدِّينِ — 'Do not say, "I have been given authority, I order and am obeyed", for this leads to corruption in the heart and the erosion of religion.' This self-glorification is seen as producing first an inner, spiritual corruption, that of the 'heart', and only then the destruction of religion. The order of priority makes a point: the outward prescriptions of religion require the right inward attitude if they are to be applied with integrity and maintained with stability. The Imam's thought might be restated thus: when power goes to the head, faith departs from the heart; and conversely, for faith to be properly manifested in the world, it must flow from a heart suffused with humility. This is another aspect of 'putting each thing in its proper place', وضع کل شئی حق محلہ.

The Imam continues: وَإِذَا أَحْدَثَ لَكَ مَا أَنْتَ فِيهِ مِنْ سُلْطَانِكَ أُبَّهَةً أَوْ مَخِيلَةً، فَانْظُرْ إِلَى عِظَمِ مُلْكِ اللهِ فَوْقَكَ، فَإِنَّ ذلِكَ يُطَامِنُ إِلَيْكَ مِنْ طِمَاحِكَ، وَيَكُفُّ عَنْكَ مِنْ غَرْبِكَ،يَفِيءُ إِلَيْكَ بِمَا عَزَبَ عَنْكَ مِنْ عَقْلِكَ — 'If the authority of your position engenders vanity and arrogance, then look at the grandeur of God's dominion above you … this will calm your ambition, restrain you from your own vehemence, and restore to you what had strayed from your intellect.' The bond between intellect and humility deserves close attention here. The part of the intellect that 'strays' when vanity and arrogance take hold is precisely the part that is conscious of God's absolute reality and of the relative, derivative, and ultimately illusory character of everything else. It is telling that the word for 'arrogance', makhila (مَخِيلَةً), comes from the same root as the word for imagination, khayal (خیال), and is thus intimately connected with the realm of the imaginary, the fanciful, the illusory, and the unreal: when imagination fastens upon the unreal, it becomes infused with pride and turns into مخیلۃ, arrogance. Arrogant pride is therefore an intense form of self-delusion rooted in a defect of the intellect. It manifests as a vice, but its cause runs deeper than the moral level, for it involves not merely an overestimation of oneself but, more fundamentally, an underestimation of the Absolute. Seen this way, the overt egotism of the tyrant is an intensification of the congenital egocentricity that clings to anyone who has not realized the truth of God's sole reality and the ultimately illusory nature of all else. The sage alone fully assimilates the truth expressed in such Quranic verses as: كُلُّ شَىْءٍ هَالِكٌ إِلَّا وَجْهَهُ — 'Everything is dying away except His countenance' (Al-Qasas: 88); كُلُّ مَنْ عَلَيْهَا فَانٍۢ ۝ وَيَبْقَىٰ وَجْهُ رَبِّكَ ذُو ٱلْجَلَلِ وَٱلْإِكْرَامِ — 'Everything that is thereon is perishing, and there subsisteth but the Face of thy Lord, possessor of Glory and Bounty' (Ar-Rahman: 26-27); and وَقُلْ جَآءَ ٱلْحَقُّ وَزَهَقَ ٱلْبَطِلُ ۚ إِنَّ ٱلْبَطِلَ كَانَ زَهُوقًۭا — 'The Truth hath come and falsehood hath passed away; indeed, falsehood is ever passing away' (Al-Isra: 81). Only the sage draws from this truth the humility that dissolves all individualism and egocentricity — the roots of pride, vanity, and arrogance, which are in turn the lifeblood of tyranny and oppression.

The Imam's warning can now be better appreciated: إِيَّاكَ وَمُسَامَةَ اللهِ فِي عَظَمَتِهِ، وَالتَّشَبُّهَ بِهِ فِي جَبَرُوتِهِ، فَإِنَّ اللهَ يُذِلُّ كُلَّ جَبَّار، وَيُهِينُ كُلَّ مُخْتَال — 'Beware of comparing [yourself] with God in greatness and likening [yourself] to Him in might, for God abases every tyrant and disgraces every braggart.' A ruler who does not act with justice toward God and His creatures — for the Imam commands, أَنْصِفِ اللهَ وَأَنْصِفِ النَّاسَ مِنْ نَفْسِكَ، وَمِنْ خَاصَّةِ أَهْلِكَ، وَمَنْ لَكَ فِيهِ هَوىً مِنْ رَعِيَّتِكَ، فَإِنَّكَ إِلاَّ تَفْعَلْ تَظْلِمْ — will find that not only His creatures but God Himself becomes his adversary: وَمَنْ ظَلَمَ عِبَادَ اللهِ كَانَ اللهُ خَصْمَهُ دُونَ عِبَادِهِ، وَمَنْ خَاصَمَهُ اللهُ أَدْحَضَ حُجَّتَهُ، وَكَانَ للهِ حَرْباً حَتَّى يَنْزِعَ وَيَتُوبَ — 'He remains at war with God until he desists and repents.' The tyrant will in the end be compelled to repent, even if only at the moment of death and imminent judgment. And even if he seems to have escaped the consequences of his tyranny in this life, he cannot escape them in the Hereafter.

As for the nature of the tyrant's war against God in this world — that is, before repentance or judgment overtakes him — it consists in his implicit opposition to ultimate reality, his violation of the deepest nature of things, of the true nature of his own being, his فطرۃ. This war takes various forms.

One form is the very persistence of tyranny itself. The longer it continues, the more wretched the tyrant's soul becomes, and the more firmly the seeds of misery are planted in his being. This is what is implied by the Quranic doctrine of istidraj (اِسْتِدْرَاج), the drawing out of punishment by degrees. The word derives from درج, meaning stages — as even in Urdu one says درجہ بدرجہ, step by step. God uses it in Surat al-Araf: ‏وَمِمَّنْ خَلَقْنَآ أُمَّةٌۭ يَهْدُونَ بِٱلْحَقِّ وَبِهِ يَعْدِلُونَ ‎۱۸۱‏وَٱلَّذِينَ كَذَّبُوا۟ بِـَٔايَتِنَا سَنَسْتَدْرِجُهُم مِّنْ حَيْثُ لَا يَعْلَمُونَ ‎۱۸۲ — 'Of those We have created are people who guide with truth and dispense justice therewith. But those who reject Our signs, We will lead them on by degrees [to punishment] from whence they know not' (Al-Araf: 181-2). This punishment meted out by degrees can take the form of apparent worldly success, as the verse makes clear: ‏وَلَا تُعْجِبْكَ أَمْوَلُهُمْ وَأَوْلَدُهُمْ ۚ إِنَّمَا يُرِيدُ ٱللَّهُ أَن يُعَذِّبَهُم بِهَا فِى ٱلدُّنْيَا وَتَزْهَقَ أَنفُسُهُمْ وَهُمْ كَفِرُونَ ‎۸۵ — 'Let not their wealth nor their children please thee. God desireth only to punish them thereby in the world, that their souls shall pass away while they are disbelievers' (At-Tawbah: 85). A tyrant or oppressor can be a Muslim in the formal sense and yet at the same time be guilty of a mode of disbelief — taking the word kafir in its strictly etymological sense, as one who 'covers over' the truth and its moral concomitants, both in his very being and in his actions. This connects directly with the contrast drawn in the verses just quoted (Al-Araf: 181-2): between those who dispense justice in accordance with the truth and 'those who reject Our signs' — the oppressors and tyrants, precisely.

## 8. Conscience, Compassion, and the Ordering of Society

One form this enmity with God takes is the inner war against one's own conscience — what Urdu calls the ⟪ar:ضمیر⟫. The voice of conscience is the whole substance of secular ethics, and it can be understood here within its fuller context, not merely as the overgrown foundation of individual morality that secular ethical thought has made of it. That fuller context is the source — beyond the individual, beyond the physical — from which individual conscience springs: the divine infusion of spiritual discernment into the soul, the divine inspiration that lies beneath every distinction between right and wrong. At the level of the morally responsible soul, then, the divine quality of justice becomes the voice of inner conscience:

> وَنَفْسٍۢ وَمَا سَوَّىٰهَا ۷ فَأَلْهَمَهَا فُجُورَهَا وَتَقْوَىٰهَا ۸
>
> 'And the soul and that which perfected it, inspiring it with [consciousness of] its wickedness and its righteousness' (Ash-Shams: 7-8).

The war against one's own conscience, even when the oppressor scarcely acknowledges it, is in truth a war against God, and all the more so.

At a deeper level, then, this war against God is revealed as a war against the very reality that makes the soul a soul. The soul contains not only a moral conscience — a built-in, inseparable awareness of the difference between good and evil, placed in it by God — but also an inborn consciousness of God's lordship and of its own answering servitude as a creature. Spiritual consciousness is therefore the source of moral conscience. The Quran describes how this spiritual consciousness dwells within each soul in terms that stress its nature before and beyond existence, calling up a 'moment' at the very dawn of creation — a moment, or degree of being, that comes before the human condition itself yet enters into the very shaping of the human soul:

> وَإِذْ أَخَذَ رَبُّكَ مِنۢ بَنِىٓ آدَمَ مِن ظُهُورِهِمْ ذُرِّيَّتَهُمْ وَأَشْهَدَهُمْ عَلَىٰٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ أَلَسْتُ بِرَبِّكُمْ ۖ قَالُوا۟ بَلَىٰ ۛ شَهِدْنَآ ۛ أَن تَقُولُوا۟ يَوْمَ ٱلْقِيَمَةِ إِنَّا كُنَّا عَنْ هَذَا غَفِلِينَ ۱۷۲

'And when thy Lord brought forth from the children of Adam, from their loins, their seed, and made them testify against their souls [saying], "Am I not your Lord?" They said, "Yes, verily, we testify." [This was] lest ye say on the Day of Resurrection, "Truly, of this we were unaware" (Al-Araf: 172). The moment He created them He made them witness His glory and testify to it, so that they could plead no ignorance on the Day of Judgment.

What comes to light here is that the source of morality rises above the human level even while forming part of the very definition of what it is to be human. Seen this way, what makes the human soul fully human is precisely that element of transcendence which infinitely surpasses the soul and yet, mysteriously, supplies its own deepest being. In this light, tyranny and oppression take on a subtler meaning: these vices are drawn much closer to the lived reality of every soul, rather than confined to those who hold and abuse political power. The tyrant is, before anything else, one who violates his own soul, one who lets it be ruled by 'the soul which commands evil', النَّفْس al-ammara (⟪ar:النَّفْسُ الْاَمَّارَۃُ بِالسُّوءِ⟫). And so the saying the Imam cites as summing up the attitude of one on the way to corrupting his own soul and becoming an oppressor — وَلاَ تَقُولَنَّ: إِنِّي مُؤَمَّرٌ آمُرُ فَأُطَاعُ, 'I order and am obeyed' — is to be understood not only in social terms but also within the small world of the soul. It becomes the utterance of the evil element within the soul: the nafs al-ammara, which literally 'orders' (آمُرُ) and is obeyed by every other faculty of the soul — intelligence, imagination, will, sentiment, sensibility, and the rest.

The struggle against one's own soul (mujahadat النَّفْس, ⟪ar:مُجَاھَدَۃُ النَّفْسِ⟫) is a theme the Imam returns to again and again. The need to wrestle with and master one's own faults is an essential part of justice in the sense defined earlier: to put each thing in its proper place begins with setting oneself right before presuming to reform or govern others; it means restoring the divine nature within oneself. As the Imam says, 'If your aspiration is to reform the people, begin with yourself, for your pursuit of the reform of others while your own soul is corrupt is the greatest of faults.' In his letter appointing Muhammad b. Abi Bakr (⟪ar:مُحَمَّدُ بْنُ اَبِی بَکْرٍ⟫), the governor of Egypt before Malik (⟪ar:مَالِکُ اَشْتَرُ⟫), the Imam draws a firm link between the granting of power and the duty to resist one's own soul: 'And be aware, O Muhammad b. Abi Bakr, that I have appointed you as governor over the most immense of the forces at my disposal, the people of Egypt, so you are obliged to oppose your soul and protect your religion.' The tie between the size of the power placed in one's hands and the spiritual responsibility that comes with it is worth close attention. A ruler must be on guard, first against his own soul and its desires, and second in regard to the religion. The greater the power at hand, the greater the duty to control and restrain oneself; for if the soul gives way to its desires and satisfies them through that power, the outcome is the corruption not only of the ruler himself but of the very religion he is charged to uphold. Here again priorities are set by a principle that is essentially spiritual: the outward form and practice of religion depends for its integrity on the inward state of the Muslim's soul. This is one more expression of the principle of putting each thing in its proper place. The commanding soul that claims for itself the right to independence and mastery is described with terse precision in the Quranic verse:

> كَلَّآ إِنَّ ٱلْإِنْسَنَ لَيَطْغَىٰٓ ۶ أَنْ رَّآهُ ٱسْتَغْنَىٰٓ ۷
>
> 'Truly man is rebellious, in that he deemeth himself independent' (Al-Alaq: 6-7).

Insofar as the soul pretends to be self-sufficient and tries to cut itself off from its total dependence on God, it sets itself up as a god in its own right — the person coming to believe that he himself is God. Herein lies the true portrait of the tyrant, the one who, as the Quran puts it, 'taketh his own desire as his god' (Al-Jathiyah: 23): أَفَرَءَيْتَ مَنِ ٱتَّخَذَ إِلَهَهُۥ هَوَىٰهُ. To know one's own nothingness before God, and therefore one's absolute need of Him, is the chief means of loosening the grip of the soul that commands to evil. For only the soul that already commands itself to evil will command others to evil; and only the soul that is lowly before God will be humble, compassionate, and just in its dealings with people.

This principle emerges clearly from these words of the Imam: 'Let the most beloved of affairs to you be those most centered upon the right, the most comprehensive in justice, and the most inclusive of popular approval, for the disapproval of the common folk undermines the approval of the elite ...' This stress on the importance of the common people is not only sound practical counsel for fair and effective government; it can also be read as an echo of the spiritual duty to recognize man's real lowliness before God, and so as a way of pressing the ruler to identify with the poor rather than the rich, in keeping with the principle:

> يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ أَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ إِلَى ٱللَّهِۖ وَٱللَّهُ هُوَ ٱلْغَنِىُّ ٱلْحَمِيدُ
>
> 'O mankind, ye are the poor before God, and God, He is the Rich, the absolutely Praiseworthy' (Fatir: 15).

The Imam's own poverty and severe austerity were proverbial. Yet this was far from a simple turning away from the world for its own sake, as his exchange with an ascetic, 'Asim b. Ziyad, makes clear. Asim's family had complained to the Imam that he denied himself too much, and the Imam told him to think of his family and not to cut himself off from the good things (الطَّيِّبَاتُ) that God has permitted. Asim retorted, O Commander of the Faithful, and yet here you are, in your rough clothes and your coarse food!' The Imam replied, 'Woe to you, I am not like you. God, the Exalted, has made it incumbent on true leaders (أَئِمَّةُ الْحَقِّ) that they proportion themselves to the weakest among the people (يُقَدِّرُ أَنْفُسَهُمْ بِضَعَفَةِ النَّاسِ), so that the poverty of the poor will not engender covetousness.' Here is a rigorous statement of the duty of the most powerful to identify themselves, in material terms, with the most powerless. It is hard to imagine a more effective way of lessening worldliness and strengthening spirituality in a society than this deliberate self-lowering, in purely material terms, to the level of the weakest people one rules. He who could have the most chooses to have the least. The Imam implies that if a leader is to stand among the أَئِمَّةُ الْحَقِّ, he must show his attachment to اَلْحَقّ — the ultimate spiritual reality, in whose light material wealth counts for nothing. So even though the most powerful and the most powerless may both be poor in material terms, both alike have access to the treasures of the spiritual realm, in proportion to how far they grasp the truth expressed in the verse already cited: 'O mankind, ye are the poor before God, and God, He is the Rich, the absolutely Praiseworthy'. On the level of social psychology, too, the Imam's insistence that rulers live like the poor, borne out in his own life, is an effective way of ensuring that the unavoidable inequalities of a society will not breed envy among the poor.

The compassion of justice

Identification with the poor also deepens a ruler's awareness of his own unceasing need of God, helping him to live more fully in reliance on divine mercy, in hope and trust; and the natural companion of such a life is the giving of mercy and compassion to one's fellow human beings. The capacity to act with compassion in no way conflicts with the demands of justice; it is, rather, an intrinsic part of justice understood ontologically — that is, in accordance with the fundamental nature of reality. Acting with compassion agrees with justice not only in the sense of putting things in their right place, since compassion is to be extended wherever and whenever it is called for; it also agrees with justice by being at one with the true nature of things, with the intrinsic character of the Real. This is why, at the head of every chapter of the Quran but one, the name 'Allah' is followed by 'the infinitely Compassionate, the ever-Merciful' (al-Rahman, al-Rahim). To put it another way, the capacity for compassion springs not so much from personal feeling as from an inborn kinship with the very nature of divine reality — that higher reality in which, as the Quran tells us, 'My mercy encompasseth all things' (Al-Araf: 156); a reality in which 'thy Lord has prescribed for Himself mercy' (Al-Anam: 54); a reality whose essential character is fixed by the principle, 'My mercy takes precedence over My wrath'. Among the many sayings of Ali (ع) on the need to show mercy are these: 'The dispensing of mercy brings down [divine] mercy.' 'As you grant mercy, so will you be granted mercy.' 'I am astounded by the person who hopes for mercy from one above him, while he is not merciful to those beneath him.'

Neither the severe aspect of the nature of things nor the 'wrathful' side of God is denied here, but both are clearly placed beneath the higher ontological principle of mercy. There is, in other words, no common measure between the merely relative things that are subject to wrath and punishment and the realities — blessed in their very nature — to which divine mercy and compassion open the way. A soul is therefore more 'real' the more it reflects this predominance of mercy, both in its spiritual orientation and in its moral conduct. And within human society, it lies in the very nature of justice, understood in this sacred way, to lean toward compassion wherever possible, even though there must also be a place for the strict application of corrective penalty where that cannot be avoided. Indeed, in his letter the Imam goes on to instruct Malik to inflict corporal punishment on any executive officer found guilty of misappropriating public funds. The Imam's counsels and letters are full of stern warnings of swift and severe retribution to his governors, the severity of these warnings matching his unflinching fidelity to the demands of fairness and honesty in handling public revenues. For example, he writes to Ziyad b. Abihi, deputy to the governor of Basra — Ali 's cousin Abd Allah b. al-Abbas:

I swear by God an oath in all sincerity: if news reaches me that you have misappropriated the revenue of the Muslims, whether a small or large amount, I shall inflict a severe punishment upon you, one which will lighten your wealth, burden your back, and degrade your affair.

The Imam was just as severe toward his own relatives at the least hint of a request for favors that would require the abuse of public funds. In one sermon he recalls an incident in which his blind brother, الْعَاقِل b. Abi Talib, asked for funds from the treasury beyond what was his due. He thought I would sell my religion for him, the Imam said. His response was to bring a red-hot piece of iron close to his brother's body, telling him that by making such a request he was urging Ali himself to enter a far fiercer fire. In the same way, when told of the misappropriation of funds by his cousin and long-standing ally 'Abd Allah b. al-Abbas, the Imam answered with a thundering rebuke. He ordered his cousin: Fear God and return their property to these people. If you do not, and God gives me authority over you, I excuse myself before God in regard to you [and your wrongdoing], and I shall indeed strike you with my sword, with which whomever I have struck enters the fire [of Hell]. By God, even if Hasan and Husayn had done the like of what you have done, they would not have been granted any leniency by me.'

The mention of the Prophet's grandsons is telling. However close the tie to a wrongdoer, however exalted his standing, the principle of justice demands complete impartiality: there are to be no favors for one's favorites. This stance is not only the exact opposite of the nepotism that had undermined the rule of the Imam's predecessor; it is also precisely what the Quran commands: 'O ye who believe, be staunch in justice, witnesses of God, even though it be against yourselves, your parents or your near of kin ...' (An-Nisa: 135).

On the theme of mercy, the passage that follows places the necessity of compassion in a universal context. It stands as one of the Imam's most important statements on the unity of the human race and the equality of all human beings, and as a corrective to every form of prejudice, particularism, and sectarianism that would confine justice or compassion to members of one's own group, however it is defined:

Infuse your heart with mercy for the subjects, love for them and kindness toward them. Be not like a ravenous beast of prey above them, seeking to devour them. For they are of two types: either your brother in religion or your like in creation. Mistakes slip from them, defects emerge from them, deliberately or accidentally. So bestow upon them your forgiveness and your pardon, just as you would have God bestow upon you His forgiveness and pardon; for you are above them, and the one who has authority over you is above you, and God is above him who appointed you ... and through them He tests you.

Here the universal reach of compassion is joined to a reminder of God's absolute sovereignty. No man — neither a governor nor the ruler who appointed him — is anything more than a slave of God, wholly dependent upon His mercy. Anyone who stands in some position of superiority over others must therefore keep his own lowness before the Absolute always in mind. That awareness works in two directions at once: it moves him to compassion toward those below him, and it draws down upon him the compassion of God above him.

One incident shows well how the Imam put justice into practice with compassion, and gives vivid form to the principle already noted, that all people are 'your like in creation'. The Imam came upon an old, blind beggar and asked about him. He was told that the man was a Christian. He said to those around him, 'You have employed him until he is old and infirm, and now you refuse to help him. Give him maintenance from the public funds (بَيْتُ الْمَالِ).' The words state in brief the Islamic principle of social welfare (مَصْلَحَة), which rests on redistributive justice and on a strict refusal to discriminate between Muslims and others. Social justice and religious equality flow from a compassion understood not as mere sentiment but as an intrinsic dimension of the Real; and so they become a fundamental religious duty binding on every Muslim, ruler and citizen alike.

The last sentence of the passage quoted earlier — which warns the governor to regard his power over his subjects not as a privilege but as a trial from God — expresses the Imam's own attitude to rulership. In the famous sermon al-Shiqshiqiyya he says that he accepted power only as an unavoidable duty:

Had God not taken from the learned [a promise] that they would not acquiesce in the rapacity of the tyrant nor in the hunger of the oppressed ... I would truly have flung its reins [that of the caliphate] back upon its withers ... and you would indeed have discovered that this world of yours is as insignificant to me as that which drips from the nose of a goat.

This is a striking embodiment of Plato's ideal attitude toward power — the attitude of the true 'philosopher', who rules solely for the sake of the people, 'not regarding it as a distinction, but as an unavoidable task ... caring only for the right and the honors to be gained from that, and above all, for justice as the one indispensable thing in whose service and maintenance they will reorganize their own state.'

The principle behind this approach to authority and governance is plainly that of noblesse oblige. To be granted the grace, the blessing, or the privilege of knowledge, together with a clear sense of justice, is to be placed under a correspondingly greater obligation: one's duty to God grows in proportion to the graces bestowed. The Imam gives the principle memorable form in one of his sermons, spoken after one of his supporters had praised him: 'The blessings of God upon a person do not become immense without the right of God over him becoming immense.'

'A good opinion of God'

A subtle point in the letter is easily overlooked, both because it is put so tersely and because its positive counterpart is never stated outright. It comes as the Imam instructs Malik about the kind of advisers he should keep near him, telling him to avoid misers and cowards:

> وَلاَ تُدْخِلَنَّ فِي مَشُورَتِكَ بَخِيلاً يَعْدِلُ بِكَ عَنِ الْفَضْلِ، وَيَعِدُكَ الْفَقْرَ، وَلاَ جَبَاناً يُضعِّفُكَ عَنِ الاْمُورِ، وَلاَ حَرِيصاً يُزَيِّنُ لَكَ الشَّرَهَ بِالْجَوْرِ، فَإِنَّ الْبُخْلَ وَالْجُبْنَ وَالْحِرْصَ غَرَائِزُ شَتَّى يَجْمَعُهَا سُوءُ الظَّنِّ بِاللهِ.

Do not include among those you consult a miser who would hold you back from generosity and warn you of destitution; nor a coward who would make you feel too weak for your affairs; nor a greedy person who would make the amassing of wealth by evil means seem attractive to you. For miserliness, cowardice and avarice are so many diverse inclinations, all united by a bad opinion of God.

The phrase 'a bad opinion' (سُوءُ الظَّنِّ بِاللهِ, su al-zann) points to a basic misunderstanding of the divine nature, and from that misunderstanding a whole series of false ideas about the qualities of God follows. Each vice grows from it. The miser holds the bad opinion that God may fail to provide in time to come; the coward, that God may fail to protect him; the greedy person, that God has given him less than he needs. Seen this way, every human vice traces back to a failure of the intellect, which is itself the fruit of weak faith, or even of outright atheism — for to disbelieve in God is to deny His reality, and so to hold the worst 'opinion' of Him. The converse follows just as plainly. A strong and deep faith produces a 'good opinion' (حُسْنُ الظَّنّ, حَسَنَ al-zann) of God: a true perception of the divine Reality, both intellectual and contemplative. And this good opinion in turn lets the soul be genuinely, and not merely superficially, marked by the divine qualities that Reality contains. Those qualities are to be understood as transcendent — absolutely one with the divine Essence, and utterly beyond the reach of the human soul — but also as models, exemplars, patterns, and perfections that the soul is to imitate so far as it can.

This is the very opposite of 'comparing oneself with God in greatness'. One must become as 'like' God as possible, yet this growth unfolds entirely within the unchanging fact of one's servitude to Him: it is the imitation of God's qualities, never the personification of God. One must be generous because God is al-Karim (اَلْکَرِیم), the Generous, and compassionate and merciful because He is al-Rahman (الرحمن الرحیم), the Compassionate — not because one is generous or merciful in oneself. Yet one must be equally lowly, humble, and self-effacing, for no measure of virtue or intelligence can lessen the gulf between the Absolute and the relative, between the Creator and the created. Humility and self-effacement, and virtues like them, are so many ways of expressing the human being's utter dependence upon God, or of manifesting the transcendence of the Absolute. Between the human and the divine, then, there is analogy of two kinds, positive and inverse. By the first, 'He who shows not mercy will not have mercy shown to him'; by the second, as quoted above:

> يَٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّاسُ أَنتُمُ ٱلْفُقَرَآءُ إِلَى ٱللَّهِۖ وَٱللَّهُ هُوَ ٱلْغَنِىُّ ٱلْحَمِيدُ
>
> 'O mankind, ye are the poor before God, and God, He is the Rich, the absolutely Praiseworthy' (Fatir: 15).

The two must therefore be brought together: the active human virtues, which are positive reflections of the divine qualities, and the spiritual awareness of one's own nothingness before the divine Reality, which is, on the human plane, the inverted reflection of divine transcendence. The image is that of a mirror. What one sees in a mirror is oneself, but reversed; and to look into the divine mirror is to see, on the human plane, that same inverted reflection of God's transcendence. A human being is at once the slave and the representative of God — 'abduLlah (عِبَادُ اللّٰہِ) and khalifatuLlah (خَلِیفَۃُ اللَّہِ). Awareness of one's servitude keeps one from sliding, even without knowing it, from representing God to rivalling Him in grandeur. A living commitment to representing God keeps that awareness of servitude from sinking into forgetfulness of the dignity and the responsibilities of the human state — a forgetfulness that ends in spiritual paralysis and the neglect of one's duty toward the world. The balance between these two sides of human existence is itself a further aspect of sacred justice, of setting things in their right order, and it tunes the quality of moral action to the harmony of pure being.

State and society

The Imam then describes in the letter the different classes of people within state and society — the army, the scribes, the judiciary, executive officers, tax-collectors, peasants, merchants, artisans, and last of all the destitute — each with its own rights and duties. The instructions in this part of the letter may be regarded as justice at its sharpest edge in practice, taking full account of the demands of social and political life. The governor is to treat every class under his authority as holding both rights and obligations; yet the essential character of his relationship to society as a whole is once again fixed by his primary duty to God. For it is God who has assigned each class its share, and who has laid down, as a binding covenant from Him, the limits and duties of each, according to His Book or the Sunna of the Prophet:

> وَكُلٌّ قَدْ سَمَّى اللهُ سَهْمَهُ، وَوَضَعَ عَلَى حَدِّهِ وَفَرِيضَتِهِ فِي كِتَابِهِ أَوْ سُنَّةِ نَبِيِّهِ (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَآلِهِ) عَهْداً مِنْهُ عِنْدَنَا مَحْفُوظاً

God has ordained the share of every one of them and laid down His covenant about the limits of each in His Book and the Sunna of His Prophet by way of a settlement which is preserved with us.

The governor's duty to society is therefore part of the universal covenant between man and God — the 'ahd (عہد) that recurs so often in the Quran. By calling on this Quranic term to reinforce the duty of acting justly toward every class of society, the Imam knew that his words would gain immeasurably in force from their resonance with verses such as these:

> أَفَمَن يَعْلَمُ أَنَّمَآ أُنزِلَ إِلَيْكَ مِن رَّبِّكَ ٱلْحَقُّ كَمَنْ هُوَ أَعْمَىٰٓ ۚ إِنَّمَا يَتَذَكَّرُ أُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْأَلْبَبِ ۱۹ ٱلَّذِينَ يُوفُونَ بِعَهْدِ ٱللَّهِ وَلَا يَنقُضُونَ ٱلْمِيثَقَ ۲۰

'Is one who knoweth that what is revealed to thee from thy Lord is the truth like one who is blind? But only men of substance take heed, those who abide by the covenant (عہد) of God, and break not the tryst (ٱلْمِيثَقَ) (Ar-Ra'd: 19-20); and:

> وَلَا تَقْرَبُوا۟ مَالَ ٱلْيَتِيمِ إِلَّا بِٱلَّتِى هِىَ أَحْسَنُ حَتَّىٰ يَبْلُغَ أَشُدَّهُۥ ۚ وَأَوْفُوا۟ بِٱلْعَهْدِ ۖ إِنَّ ٱلْعَهْدَ كَانَ مَسْـُٔولًۭا ۳۴
>
> 'Approach not the wealth of the orphan except with that which is more right, until he attains maturity; and abide by the covenant, truly, the covenant will be asked about' (Al-Isra: 34).

Significantly, just before the instructions quoted above, the Imam advises Malik to study long with the 'scholars' (الَعُلَمَاءِ) and to converse long with the 'sages' (الْحُكَمَاءِ):

> وَأَكْثِرْ مُدَارَسَةَ الَعُلَمَاءِ، وَمُنَافَثَةَ الْحُكَمَاءِ، فِي تَثْبِيتِ مَا صَلَحَ عَلَيْهِ أَمْرُ بِلاَدِكَ، وَإِقَامَةِ مَا اسْتَقَامَ بِهِ النَّاسُ قَبْلَكَ

The purpose is to 'consolidate that which brings well-being to your lands, and to further entrench that which has already been established by your predecessors.' The distinction between scholars and sages matters here. The scholars (عُلَمَاء) know the formal rules as set down in the Quran and the Sunna — the letter of the law, the Sharia (شریعۃ). The sages (حکما) know how to apply that knowledge; theirs is the spirit of wisdom, for wisdom is knowledge put into practice. The distinction is between the letter of the law and the spirit behind it — the spirit without which the rules cannot be applied with care and sensitivity.

There follows a passage that describes, with admirable concision, how each class or segment of society depends upon the others. It begins with the army as the foundation of the state and ends with the governor himself as the center around which the different elements turn. The image is that of a wheel. The governor is the hub, working on the horizontal plane, among created things; the various classes — the army, the scribes, the executive officers, the dispensers of justice, the tax-collectors, the merchants, the artisans, and, lowest of all, the needy and destitute — are the spokes that radiate from him to the rim. Yet the hub is nothing without the axle that turns it. This horizontal center is nothing without the vertical axis that joins it, and through it the whole of society, to the Real. As a hub is useless without its link to the axle, so all the governor's efforts are useless without his link to the Absolute, on which he must wholly depend if society is truly to flourish. The soldiers are called the 'fortresses' of the subjects; and at the close of the passage the governor is told that he cannot accomplish what God requires of him without resolute determination and recourse to Him (al-ihtimam and al-istiana) — a point driven home later in the letter, where the needs of the poor are addressed. Of the instructions concerning the several classes of state and society, a few salient points are enough. For the commander of the troops, Malik is told to choose the man who is 'the most sincere in relation to God, the Prophet and your Imam, the purest of heart, the one most excellent in forbearance (حِلْم), who is slow to anger, happy to pardon, kind to the weak, severe with the strong; one who is neither stirred by violence nor held back by weakness.'

This portrait of the ideal commander reflects the Imam's own character. What stands out is its emphasis on kindness, gentleness, and forbearance — qualities not usually associated with fighting men. Here is a spiritually refined approach to an activity that, left to itself, easily tramples the norms of decency and propriety. Once again the Imam's description of the perfect warrior resembles Plato's description of the 'guardian', the protector of the ideal state. Plato states the central difficulty in raising a force to protect and defend the city as this: how are men whose nature is 'spirited' and courageous 'to be kept from behaving pugnaciously to one another and to the rest of their countrymen'? For 'they must be gentle to their own people and dangerous only to their enemies.' This echoes the Quranic description of those who are with the Prophet as 'severe against the disbelievers, compassionate among themselves' (Al-Fath: 29) — hence the Imam's insistence on the gentle qualities a warrior needs as the essential complement to the martial ones.

For the next class, the judges, the Imam names the virtues that must be present in those who dispense legal justice: they must not be confused by complexities or provoked by litigants; they must be unflinching before the truth, free from greed, unsatisfied by shallow solutions, rigorous in argument, steadfast in seeking out the truth of every matter, and proof against praise and temptation. 'Such people,' the Imam adds, 'are indeed rare.' He also recommends that judges be paid generously, 'so that any deficiency will be removed, thus diminishing the need for help from people' — a condition for keeping any judiciary incorruptible.

For the administrators, Malik is told to test their honesty before employing them and to take scrupulous care that no partiality or favouritism sways his choices. He is to keep watching and investigating their conduct; and, as noted earlier, severe punishments are to be imposed wherever there is evidence of abuse of privilege or misuse of public funds.

For those who till the soil, from whom the land tax is collected, the Imam offers this wise maxim: 'Let your concern with the cultivation of the land outweigh your concern with the collection of the tax, for no tax will be collected if there be no cultivation. And whoever exacts the tax without cultivating the land ruins the land and destroys the people.' His further urgings to attend to the needs of the tillers and to deal fairly with all who seek redress of wrongs close with this warning:

The devastation of the land only comes about through the destitution of its inhabitants; and the destitution of its inhabitants only comes about when the desire to amass wealth rules the souls of the governors, when they have doubts about what endures, and when they profit little from exemplary teachings.

As for the phrase 'having doubts about what endures' (سُوءُ ظَنِّهِمْ بِالْبَقَاءِ), the commentators suggest two meanings. It may be that the governors think they will last forever, forgetting that they will die and be judged in the Hereafter; or it may be that they doubt their position will last long, and so seize whatever opportunities they can while they can. The first reading seems the more likely, so closely does it echo the following Quranic verses — which may themselves be counted among the 'exemplary teachings' (عِبْرَة) the Imam names at the very end of the sentence: 'Woe be to every slandering backbiter, who amasseth wealth and counteth it. He thinketh that his wealth will render him immortal. Nay, but he will be flung to the all-consuming Fire' (Al-Humazah: 1-4).

The Imam uses for the amassing of wealth the same word, jamaa, that appears in this verse. The lesson is plain: greed is a symptom of spiritual short-sightedness. Forgetfulness of what truly endures breeds greed in one degree or another — greed being, here, the craving for a false material richness in place of a true spiritual plenitude — and greed in turn breeds exploitation and oppression. Once more the Imam's vision shows that the vice of injustice does not merely produce corruption; it is itself the product of a chain of causes rooted in a fundamental inversion of spiritual values, a failure to see the true nature of things.

Conscience, Compassion, and the Ordering of Society

The same vision that traces injustice to a disorder within the soul governs the more practical counsel that follows, where the ordering of society is shown to rest upon the conscience of those who hold authority. Even the advice on appointing scribes is sealed with a spiritual message. Malik is told to resist the temptation to appoint only those who have shown him their best side. The governor is to examine each candidate's past record along with the impression he has left upon ordinary people, and then to appoint only those well known for trustworthiness. To do all this is called 'proof of your sincerity toward God.' The choice of officials is thus no mere matter of administration; it is a measure of the ruler's own truthfulness before his Lord, and the care he takes in it answers to God, not to the men who court his favor.

Merchants and craftsmen the Imam then describes as those who bring many benefits to society and must therefore be treated well. On the whole they are a peaceable class, and should be left in peace to go about their work. Yet the vices they are prone to must be held in check: hoarding, miserliness, and cheating. Here again the outward regulation of trade rests upon an inward discernment — one that tells apart the honest labor that enriches a community from the greed that preys upon it.

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> A note on the two words this chapter turns on. In the wider tradition, *nafs* derives from a
> root meaning "to breathe" — the nafs is the breathing self, and in spiritual usage it names the
> lower self or ego whose cravings the seeker must master; this is exactly the "commanding soul,"
> al-nafs al-ammara, the chapter warns against. The covenant is worth pausing on too. The root of
> *ahd* is the language of solemn promise — to bind oneself by a pledge and be charged with
> keeping it. In this tradition it reaches in two directions at once: the primordial compact
> between God and humanity ("Am I not your Lord?") and the charge of office a ruler receives — the
> very pledge Ali lays on his governor Malik al-Ashtar. The chapter draws on both, and the single
> word shows why they are one duty: the pledge made and the trust to be kept.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 9. Justice for the Poor and the Sincerity of Intention

The Imam now turns Malik toward those who most need justice, opening the passage with the exclamation "Allah, Allah" to mark its urgency. The instruction concerns those with no resources whatsoever: the destitute, the crippled, orphans, the elderly, those most in need of justice from a governor, who must be treated in such a way that "God may excuse you on the day you meet Him." The governor is to provide for all of these, and for others who are in need but too proud to beg. He ought to appoint an officer charged specifically with uncovering the needs of the most destitute and attending to them. The Imam then adds words that reach the heart of the matter: "This is onerous for the governors, and [the fulfillment of] all rights is onerous, but God makes it light for those who aspire to the Hereafter, who restrain their souls in patience, and trust in the truth of that which is promised them by God."

It is the spiritual dimension that makes an otherwise crushing ideal workable. God's help is assured to those whose attention is not confined to this world alone, but whose aspirations reach beyond it to the Hereafter. Only when this world is seen through to the next does a fully just attitude toward this world become possible. Those whose talab, their aspiration, does not reach beyond this world are far more likely to take the easy path and neglect the destitute; for in the purely horizontal reckoning of politics, the cost to a ruler of a policy of charity toward the poor may well outweigh any benefit he can see for himself. Here an ethics bound to a purely this-worldly assessment — to pragmatic politics or to personal interest — shows its inherent flaws. But when the sense of what is owed to the poor is charged with firm faith in God's unfailing justice and boundless compassion, and filled with longing for the Hereafter, it generates an unflinching fidelity to the needs of the destitute and renews that fidelity again and again.

Such an attitude goes far beyond conventional notions of charity or generosity. A person who has realized it to perfection cannot be swayed by any contrary suggestion rooted in self-serving pragmatism or individualism, still less in indifference or wilfulness. With this attitude firmly in place, the next quality the Imam names — patience — follows naturally. Given a concrete rather than merely notional foretaste of the absolute values at stake, patience can be sustained without limit; it no longer rests on personal will alone, but is upheld by the empowering grace that flows from heartfelt faith. The Imam completes the thought by noting that those with the right aspirations come, in consequence, to trust in the truth of God's promise. If that promise — of His reward, His unfailing justice, His boundless compassion — is true, then the motive behind the governor's effort to show the poor mercy and justice is immeasurably deepened. The task the Imam calls onerous — the duty to help the weak and helpless, from whom no political benefit can be gained — is transformed into an inescapable duty that follows directly from spiritual conviction. That conviction rests on the certainty that such generosity and compassion are not merely good, right, and proper, but that these virtues accord with the true and inalienable substance of ultimate reality. The moral implications of spiritual reality, and equally the spiritual consequences of virtue, are hidden from worldly rulers — by their covert prejudices at best, and their overt vices at worst. Such rulers are blind, too, to the divine promise of ultimate felicity, and equally blind to the warnings of retribution for the oppressors, al-zalimun. The zalim may be understood as one who defies the divine commandments concerning justice and, more fundamentally, violates the compassion that lies at the very heart of the divine nature.

These considerations open onto the crucial question of intention. Even when the just person's effort to live according to justice is thwarted by the uncertainties of worldly life, two things remain: the right intentions that move him, and his awareness of the immutable divine qualities that serve as the models and principles guiding his endeavor. Together these produce a real, inward "taste" of the grace that furnishes the spiritual foundation of his moral life. If God's promise is true, and one is absolutely certain of that truth, then its effect on the soul of the sincere believer transforms a personal ethic into an unconditional imperative, and a desirable value into an undeniable reality. Morality is thus deepened by spirituality, and into it: a total way of being, one that embraces the whole of existence, within which the burden of individual duty is not only immeasurably lightened but becomes a companion to one's taste of spiritual reality. One then acts justly out of gratitude for what one has already received — the joy of spiritual certainty — and not only out of hope for something not yet attained.

The Quran speaks of the reward of Paradise as something already granted to the true believer. For one whose certainty of Paradise is absolute, the future has already arrived: "We have given thee [the paradisal fountain of] al-Kawthar; so pray to thy Lord and sacrifice ..." (Al-Kawthar: 1-2). To worship God out of certainty of the blessedness to come — a blessedness that has, in one respect, already come — is well expressed in the following saying of the Imam:

Indeed there is a group who worship God out of desire [for something not yet attained]; and this is the worship of the merchants. And there is a group who worship God out of fear, and this is the worship of the slaves. And there is a group who worship God out of gratitude, and this is the worship of the free.

The just person is further freed from the material consequences of his actions, in proportion to the uprightness and sincerity of his intentions, since in the Islamic view actions are judged not by their consequences but by their intentions. The intention is to be just — not for the sake of any earthly reward or tangible worldly result, but purely for the sake of justice itself. And this essence or principle of justice is in turn inseparable from the divine nature. For justice is one with God not simply because whatever God commands is just; rather, God commands just acts precisely because they are just, and because justice is one with His own nature: "I bear witness that He is Justice and He acts justly." Justice is not one attribute among others, something merely present in the divine nature; it is that nature. Were it only in His nature, it would stand alongside His other qualities; but because it is His very essence, all His other qualities essentially flow from it, so that His mercy no less than His wrath, His creation no less than His destruction, all proceed from His justice. If God acts justly by virtue of His very nature, which is pure justice, then the spiritual person will act justly out of a desire to conform to God's nature, and not simply out of obedience to God's commands, and never out of desire for any earthly reward.

This can be seen in any single virtue. If one speaks the truth without grasping the reason for it — that truth, الحق, is the very nature of the Creator, and that the goal in this world is to take on the qualities of that Creator — then truthfulness soon becomes situational and self-serving, open to compromise whenever circumstances seem to warrant it: I speak the truth when it benefits me and lie when lying benefits me. But once truth is seen as the nature of the divine, it becomes everlasting and eternal, and the consequences of speaking it cease to matter; one speaks it simply in emulation of الحق, the absolute truth. Virtue practiced on an ad hoc basis is no virtue at all, merely a series of pragmatic adaptations to shifting circumstances. This is why the life of Ali (ع), when studied, reflects an unflinching adherence to the divine virtues. His was a life governed not by his نفس but entirely by the divine nature of Allah, and his every celebrated action was the fruit of an irresistible attraction toward the nature of الحق. His gaze was never fixed on the action itself but on the source of the principles that generated it; by fixing his attention on the beauty of the divine nature and seeking to take it on, good actions emerged from him naturally and inevitably. As the Quran says:

The most pious will be far removed from it [the punishment of Hell], he who giveth of his wealth to purify himself. And nobody possesseth, in the sight of such a person, any favor that might be bestowed on him as reward [for his generosity]. [His action is performed] seeking only the Countenance of his transcendent Lord, and he will be content (Al-Layl: 17-21).

The same principle appears in the description of the righteous, the abrar. Commentators on the Quran agree that the occasion of revelation, sabab al-nuzul, for the following verses was a time when Ali and his family went three days running without food. At the end of each day, just as they were about to break their fast, someone in greater need came and asked for what they had. The Quran relates this as follows: "They feed, out of love for God, the needy, the orphan and the prisoner, saying, 'We feed you only for the sake of God; we desire from you neither reward nor thanks'" (Al-Insan: 8-9).

In the very measure that a good action is performed for the sake of that absolute goodness which God is, the one performing it rests wholly content, untouched by the blame or praise of others, neither deflated when outward adversity thwarts his good actions nor inflated when they succeed. This understanding of the relation between action and intention illuminates the meaning of the Imam's saying that "their hearts are in the Gardens [of Paradise] while their bodies are at work." Here again a difference emerges between a secular ethic of justice, one devoid of all spiritual or transcendental points of reference, and a spiritual conception of justice. A secular ethic stands or falls by how far it succeeds outwardly in realizing its ideal of justice; a spiritual conception already sees its ideal fulfilled in the sincere intention to be just. Even so, the fulfillment of a spiritual ideal of justice requires an unyielding struggle to achieve it, for without that struggle the intention proves to lack sincerity. Without matching action, aspirations and intentions remain shallow and purely theoretical. To hold an intention with total sincerity requires diligent, ceaseless striving toward its realization. Thus, however formidable the obstacles, however compelling the arguments of political pragmatism, however thoroughly his strenuous efforts are undone by the unavoidable pressures of outward life, the just person never despairs. He never abandons the effort to achieve justice, precisely because achieving justice in this world is not the whole of his aim. He knows that the intention is already, in an invisible but spiritually palpable way, its own fulfillment; and he knows, with sober realism, that the outward realization of any intention depends on circumstances beyond his control and, ultimately, on the grace of God.

On the surface this may seem to excuse the failure to achieve justice in society, and so to offer a subterfuge by which actual injustice is legitimated through an appeal to good but thwarted intentions. The very opposite is in fact the case. Far from betraying any lack of resolve in one moved by a sacred conception of justice, such a person's efforts are, on the contrary, as ceaseless and unyielding as the eternal and unfailing reward in which he fervently believes. Those efforts are sustained, too, by an awareness of the subtle continuity between just intentions and the divine quality of justice, and through it the divine nature itself. An inalienable sense of the sacred sustains the just person in his pursuit of justice. The argument is not that all secular or atheistic conceptions of justice are without value; only that, set beside a sacred conception, they lack those immeasurable and inexhaustible spiritual resources that sincere faith nourishes. The theories that dominate the present age bear this out. Relativism holds that knowledge, truth, and morality are not absolute but exist only in relation to culture, society, or historical context. Nihilism rejects all religious and moral principle and holds that life is essentially meaningless. Utilitarianism counts an action right only insofar as it is useful or benefits the majority, reducing good and bad to a reckoning of gain and loss with no fundamental principle beneath them. And opportunism takes whatever advantage presents itself regardless of principle, in the manner of the manipulator who flatters, performs, and pretends, saying or doing whatever it takes to get what he wants. These are human constructions, and the world arrived at them by severing practice from principle. Whatever value may emerge from them is not rooted in the divine, so that even where they coincide with the values of faith they do so only by coincidence and remain, for all their machinery, without spirit — dead corpses. "With men this is impossible, but with God all things are possible," as Jesus says in the Gospel. Applied to the present context, that principle might be stated thus: when justice is conceived in isolation from spiritual principles, personal idealism lies acutely exposed to outward contingencies and is all too likely to disintegrate under the pressure of difficulty and disappointment; in a sacred conception of justice, by contrast, divine grace holds the incorruptible ideal of justice high above the frailties and upheavals to which both the human soul and human society are subject. The more one's ideals are rooted in spiritual knowledge, the less one is prone to disappointment and despair in the face of difficulty.

The importance of this ideal of justice is clearly visible in the Imam's own experience as caliph. An unbroken succession of bloody conflicts against foes who professed Islam prevented him, outwardly, from enacting many of the principles and policies that flowed from his conception of truth and justice. Yet none of this produced dejection, despair, or cynicism, nor did it lessen in the slightest his efforts to achieve justice wherever and whenever possible. The quality of his own administration shows as much, as does the steady stream of letters and counsels to his officers and governors — the letter to Malik among them. Such despair is almost inconceivable for one whose inner life is shaped by the conviction that "No act is negligible if it is accompanied by piety." For such a person, despair is excluded by piety in the deepest sense of the word — the permanent awareness of divine Reality; for this degree of piety is the makhraj, the "way out," from the darkness of all earthly injustice. As the Imam says, "He who has pious awe toward God, for him God establishes a way out of tribulation, and a light out of darkness (اعْلَمُوا أَنَّهُ مَنْ يَتَّقِ اللَّهَ يَجْعَلْ لَهُ مَخْرَجًا مِنَ الْفِتَنِ، وَنُورًا مِنَ الظُّلَمِ)."

## 10. Prayer, Remembrance, and Gazing on the Good

The lynchpin of the entire epistle now comes into view: prayer, the most important means by which the ruler takes into himself the sacred substance of all the virtues. At the opening of the letter the Imam began with a counsel to observe the obligatory prayers.
