These modes transcend the formal rules governing the fixed canonical prayers, which prescribe specific words, movements, and conditions. The dhikr, by contrast, is to be performed at all times, in all places, and in all postures; it is something to be woven into the texture of everyday life rather than imposed upon life as an external, formalistic practice. According to Imam Jafar al-Sadiq, invocation is the one practice that should be carried out in all circumstances and at all times, with no limit placed upon it: 'Everything has a limit, at which it terminates, except the dhikr Allah. God has made obligatory the religious duties (الْفَرَائِضُ), so whoever fulfills them, that fulfillment constitutes their limit. For the month of Ramadan, whoever fasts therein, that constitutes its limit ...' He goes on to mention other rites, then cites the Quranic verse: 'O ye who believe, invoke God with much invocation', and adds that his father, Imam Muhammad al-Baqir, never ceased invoking, even while eating and walking.

In this light it becomes easier to understand those whom the Quran describes as 'men whom neither trade nor merchandise diverts from the remembrance of God'. The verse also illuminates why believers are described as those who are 'perpetually at prayer (عَلَىٰ صَلَاتِهِمْ دَآئِمُونَ) (Al-Maarij: 23). Such people are not merely regular in performing the canonical prayer at its appointed times; for the men of substance, there is no moment when they are not at prayer', if one understands 'prayer' as the remembrance of God. The Quran instructs mankind to 'establish the prayer for the sake of My remembrance' (Ta-Ha: 14). The very purpose and goal of prayer, its spiritual value and substance, is thus the remembrance of God.

A saying of Ali bears directly on this: 'Continuous invocation is the food of the spirit and the key to prayer (مِفْتَاحُ الصَّلَاةِ).' This sheds further light on why the Quran says that 'prayer keepeth [one] away from lewdness and iniquity, and the remembrance of God is greater' (Al-Ankabut: 45). The formal, canonical prayer is described here in negative or constraining terms: it is a preventative. Its formality is a ritual necessity, but it is also, unavoidably, an existential limitation, performed and operative at one time and not another, expressed in certain forms and not others. The remembrance of God is greater in that it is the positive, enlivening substance of prayer; it is what liberates consciousness from the verbal, mental, and existential limits by which all formal prayer is defined. Formal prayer is inescapably bounded by such limits, and so its baraka, its blessedness, can all too easily become confined, in one's own experience, within those same limits — so that one feels close to God only during formal prayer, and not in everyday life outside the prayer-times. It is dhikr, in the sense of recollectedness and constant awareness of God, that allows prayer, or the consciousness shaped by prayer, to open outward into its intended essence: an awareness of the unconditional presence of God. Dhikr thus transforms a preventative measure against moral transgression into a positive apprehension of spiritual reality. One might say that the dhikruLlah is 'greater' than the ritual or canonical prayer in the measure that spiritual awareness transcends moral conscience, or in the measure that essence transcends form. The 'lesser' is not negated by the 'greater' but is rather contained within it, enriched by it, and brought to a fullness and scope it could never attain on its own. In this light one can better understand at least some of the reasons why the Imam, following the Prophet and the Quran, presents dhikruLlah as the quintessence of worship and devotion.

What remains are the spiritual fruits of the remembrance of God, and in particular 'realization', تَحْقِيق: the process by which اَلْحَقّ, the Real, is 'made real' within oneself. This is closely related to the process the Imam earlier described as tawahhud, self-integration, the spiritual assimilation of oneness within oneself, as distinct from the simple theological affirmation of oneness, تَوْحِيد, in its conventional sense. In keeping with the Imam's characteristic use of paradox, the fruit of remembrance can be approached first by examining its very opposite: غَفْلَةٌ, heedlessness, or sahw, forgetfulness and distraction. Here again the Imam's sayings function implicitly as a commentary on Quranic verses, while opening onto a rich terrain of mystical speculation and spiritual contemplation concerning the ultimate consequences of the invocation and remembrance of God. The Quran is severe in its condemnation of those who pray ostentatiously and hypocritically, those who 'give the lie to religion': 'Woe to those who pray, those who are heedless of their prayer, those who make a show, and who withhold simple assistance' (Al-Maun: 4-7). The Imam likewise warns against remembering God heedlessly or distractedly, and in describing its opposite, perfect or complete dhikr, he articulates a paradoxical return to a kind of 'forgetting' that goes to the very heart of the Sufi principle of 'extinction in remembrance' (اَلْفَنَاءُ فِي الذِّكْرِ):

Do not remember God absent-mindedly (سَاهِيًا), nor forget Him in distraction; rather, remember Him with perfect remembrance (ذِكْرًا كَامِلًا), a remembrance in which your heart and tongue are in harmony, and what you conceal conforms with what you reveal. But you will not remember Him according to the true reality of the remembrance (حَقِيقَةُ الذِّكْرِ) until you forget your own soul in your remembrance.

This saying offers a summary of the entire mystical path. What is implied is the whole sequence of spiritual 'stations' (مَقَامَات, sing. maqam) through which total sincerity is attained. Sufism is itself often described simply as 'sincerity', that is, sidq or إِخْلَاص. To be totally sincere in this sense is to orient oneself and one's desires purely toward God, without any taint of impurity, where impurity means the presence, in one's devotion, action, or intention, of any desire for something other than God. On one hand, the dhikr itself helps to realize sincerity by 'polishing' the heart; on the other, it is sincerity that confers 'perfection' or 'completion' upon the spiritual efficacy of the dhikr. What matters at this stage of 'perfect' dhikr is that the outward verbal form of the invocation must harmonize with heartfelt intention. There must be no contradiction between what the tongue articulates and what motivates the heart. Bringing heart and tongue into harmony means that the divine Reality invoked by the tongue reverberates in the heart, the very source of the soul's fundamental dispositions, its virtues.

The divine qualities contained within the Name of the Absolute must, to some degree at least, be reflected in the heart and soul of the invoker as virtues — virtues that are summed up in the sincerity or 'purity' of soul to which إِخْلَاص refers. When the soul is completely pure, sincerely and utterly at one with the highest ideals proclaimed by the tongue and the intellect, it becomes transparent to the spiritual qualities embedded within its own substance: the fitra, or primordial nature. These are the qualities 'brought to light', and brought to life, by the dhikr; they are the qualities that shine forth from the heart once it has been polished by the dhikr. In their absence, however, the purely technical act of invocation is 'heedless' and without any effect. One who makes no effort to attain sincerity, who does not take up the 'greater' jihad, is heedless of God, neglecting the moral qualities that accompany authentic remembrance and ignoring the moral conditions its efficacy requires.

Yet what is most remarkable in this saying of the Imam is his presentation of the ultimate stage of dhikr in terms of its apparent opposite. The 'reality' of dhikr, its haqiqa, opens outward into the supreme haqiqa, divine Reality as such. This degree of dhikr requires the 'forgetting' of the individual: the veil of the self must be cast aside if the spiritual vision of divine Reality is to be attained. There can be no marifa without self-effacement at the outset, and 'extinction' (فَنَاء) in the end. The reality of 'remembering' calls for the most radical 'forgetting'.

In other words, a clear distinction — indeed a radical break — separates 'perfect' dhikr from the 'reality' of dhikr. To move from 'perfection' to 'reality', from human perfection to divine Reality, is to make an ontological leap of consciousness. At the first level, the aim is to eliminate hypocrisy and to attain sincerity, integrity, and wholeness — in short, perfection on the plane of the human soul. Here the individual, as the agent of the dhikr, keeps his identity. The harmonious reverberation between hidden intent and outward act is what, at this stage, is called 'perfect' dhikr. Yet it remains a dhikr belonging to the individual as agent. At the higher level, by contrast, the dhikr can no longer be defined in relation to the individual, for the individual has been 'forgotten' — forgotten by himself. This can only mean that the center of consciousness has shifted from the invoker, al-dhakir, to the Invoked, الْمَذْكُورُ. The haqiqa of the dhikr is thus one with the object of invocation, the Real itself, اَلْحَقّ. Esoterically, then, the true agent of the invocation is God Himself. Man's apparent invocation is no more than an outward appearance, an illusion that the very act of invocation extinguishes.

The question of agency returns here. A hadith qudsi bears upon it: "My slave never ceases to draw near to Me through supererogatory acts (نَوَافِلُ) until I love him." In his commentary on the Quranic verses about those whom the world does not distract from the remembrance of God, the Imam made clear that these نَوَافِلُ center upon the practice of invocation. Invocation, then, can be understood as disclosing the reality of God's ontological agency within all acts: God is the hearing and the seeing of the individual whom He loves as a result of his devotions. This account of the Divine penetrating the faculties of soul and body describes a universal reality, the true state of affairs — not any change of state, whether in God or in the individual. God does not "change" in order to enter into the acts and perceptions of one person rather than another.

His "becoming" one with the acts of perception must be understood as a reality "becoming clear" to the soul once it is unveiled, so that the soul sees through the illusory appearance of its own agency. Through the invocation, the soul is enabled to see the deeper meaning of two Quranic verses:

> وَٱللَّهُ خَلَقَكُمْ وَمَا تَعْمَلُونَ

"God hath created you and what ye do" (As-Saffat: 96), and:

> وَمَا رَمَيْتَ إِذْ رَمَيْتَ وَلَكِنَّ ٱللَّهَ رَمَىٰ
>
> "thou didst not throw when thou threwest [the stones], but God threw" (Al-Anfal: 17).

The kind of "vision" the dhikr makes possible is illuminated by the very title of one of Ibn al-Arabi's treatises, Kitab al-fana fil-mushahada (The Book of Extinction in Contemplation). The title seems to point directly at the Imam's paradox of forgetting at the very heart of remembrance, for to be extinguished in contemplation is akin to being forgotten in invocation. At the opening of this treatise, Ibn al-Arabi writes that the divine Reality is too exalted to be witnessed by the creaturely eye. The vision of God is impossible so long as any "trace of the condition of the creature in the eye of the witness" remains. Only toward the end of the treatise is this seemingly insuperable barrier removed. Ibn al-Arabi resolves the problem by means of the Prophet's definition of احسان: "It is to worship God as though you see Him, and if you see Him not, yet truly He sees you." The Arabic wording allows a pause to be placed in the middle of the phrase "if you see Him not (in lam takun: tarahu)," and that pause transforms the meaning entirely into: "if you are not, you see Him."

This vision of God cannot, then, belong to the individual — "forgotten" in the Imam's words, or non-existent according to Ibn al-Arabi. The vision of God can only be by God, not by man. The metaphysical underpinnings of this "ontological transference," in relation to the divine Names, are taken up further on. The message of "extinction in contemplation" is reinforced by a dialogue Ibn al-Arabi narrates between himself and Moses in the sixth heaven, set within his account of his own visionary ascent through the heavens to the divine presence within his own soul.

He addresses Moses: "You requested the vision (of God), while the Messenger of God (مُحَمَّد) said that 'not one of you will see his Lord until he dies.'"

Moses replies: "And it was just like that: when I asked Him for the vision, He answered me, so that I 'fell down stunned' (Al-Araf: 143). Then I saw Him in my (state of) being stunned."

Ibn al-Arabi asks: "While (you were) dead?"

Moses: "While (I was) dead. I did not see God until I had died."

Here is a supra-personal vision entirely congruent with Ali 's unselfconscious remembrance. What both forms of spiritual assimilation imply is the "ontological transference" of agency. The true vision of God cannot take place so long as anything creaturely remains in the "eye" of the beholder. The spiritual principle of this dialogue — and even some of its language — is rendered in condensed form in a supplication attributed to the Imam. He pleads with God to make him "one of those whom You have called and he has replied to You, upon whom You have glanced and who falls unconscious before Your majesty." The word saiqa ("to fall unconscious," "to be stunned") in this supplication is the same word used in the Quranic verse (Al-Araf: 143) for Moses' state when God revealed Himself to the mountain. A single "glance" from the Real suffices to precipitate this undoing of self-awareness — the shadow cast, upon the plane of individual consciousness, by the blazing light of God's self-disclosure, His tajalli.

This divine "glance" (لَحْظَة) returns the discussion to Ibn al-Arabi's evocative phrase "extinction in contemplation." In his Tarjuman al-ashwaq, he refers to divine wisdom as a peacock that "kills with her glances," and adds in his own commentary that this refers to "the station" of al-fana fil-mushahada (extinction in contemplation). Earlier in the same commentary, he refers to the "murderous glances" of this divine wisdom "which accrues to a man in his hours of solitude, and which assaults him with such violence that he is unable to behold his personality, and which exercises dominion over him." His commentary on the following lines is also instructive, underlining the relationship between dhikr and فَنَاء: "Wild is she, none can make her his friend; she has gotten in her solitary chamber (بَيْتُ خَلْوَتِهَا), a mausoleum for remembrance (ذِکْر)." The commentary adds: "because contemplation of the Essence is a passing away (فَنَاء). Her solitude is her looking on herself, for God says, 'Neither My earth nor My heaven contains Me, but I am contained by the heart of My believing servant,' and since the heart which contains this essential wisdom of the race of Jesus is bare and empty of all attributes, it is like a desert and she is like a wild animal."

Realization through remembrance, then, strictly implies the extinction of individual consciousness, the perishing of egoistic self-awareness before the Absolute. This principle is far from merely speculative. Quite apart from the wealth of Sufi literature on the states of consciousness (ahwal, sing. hal) through which the mystic passes, there is the evidence of the Imam's own immersion in such states. Abul-Darda, an eminent companion of the Prophet, reported that he once found Ali lying in one of the palm orchards of Medina, his body seemingly lifeless, as stiff as wood. He went to condole with Ali 's family, but was told by his wife Fatima, "My cousin has not died. Rather, in fear of God he has fainted. This condition overcomes him often." The "fear" in question is to be understood as "awe." It relates to the heavenly aspect of khawf — heavenly because Paradise is said to be the fruit of realizing this kind of fear to perfection, as the Quran indicates: "But whoso feareth (خَافَ) the station of his Lord and restraineth his soul from caprice, verily, the Garden will be his abode" (An-Naziat: 40-41). "Fear of God," in the spiritual rather than the moralistic sense, does not so much terrify as beatify. It produces not merely quaking awe before the transcendent Absolute, but also an intimate absorption within the immanent divine presence. As is often quoted in Sufism: when one fears creatures one flees from them, but when one fears God one flees to Him:

> لَّا مَلْجَأَ مِنَ ٱللَّهِ إِلَّآ إِلَيْهِ
>
> "There is no refuge from God except [fleeing] to Him" (At-Tawbah: 118).

This report also shows that the following saying of the Imam is not mere extravagant rhetoric. It appears, rather, to allude to an authentic state of spiritual intoxication — a total re-absorption of consciousness within its divine source, and a description of the conditions leading up to the state that "overcomes him often," as Fatima put it:

Truly, God has a drink for His friends (أَوْلِيَائِهِ). When they drink it, they are intoxicated (سَكَرَ); and when they are intoxicated, they are enraptured (عَرِبَ); and when they are enraptured, they are blessed (طَابَ); and when they are blessed, they dissolve (ذَابَ); and when they dissolve, they are free (خَلَصَ); and when they are free, they devote themselves purely (أَخْلَصَ); and when they devote themselves purely, they seek (طَلَبَ); and when they seek, they find (وَجَدَ); and when they find, they arrive (وَصَلَ); and when they arrive, they are at one (اِتَّصَلَ); there is no difference between them and their Beloved.

This saying of the Imam echoes that of the Prophet, who said he had states or "moments" (وَقْت) that no "angel brought close" nor "prophet sent forth" could encompass — the implication being that neither angel nor prophet can subsist as such in the ineffable "moments" of absolute تَوْحِيد. This is not "union"; it is, rather, the realization of oneness. Union implies two entities becoming one, whereas God is, as the Imam put it, "that which has no second." Even to call Him "one" falls short, since that word implies that a second might stand in sequence to Him. He is more truly named unique — a reality that admits no otherness. This ultimate realization of تَوْحِيد can be seen as the fruit of the invocation — the most potent of all intoxicating "drinks," if prayer may be called a drink. And it leads to a careful consideration of the true agent of the invocation, the true "drinker." What appears to be man's invocation of God is transformed into the divine invocation of man. At the outset it seems that man "drinks" God through His Name; but the ultimate result of the invocation, unitive realization, reveals that it is in fact God who "drinks into Himself" the essence of human consciousness — an essence from which the impurity of self-consciousness has been extracted. The true nature of the dhikr, masked by the outward form of man's invocation of God, is alluded to in another saying of the Imam: "The invocation is not a formality of speech nor a way of thinking; rather, it comes forth firstly from the Invoked, and secondly from the invoker (awwal min الْمَذْكُورُ wa thanin min al-dhakir)."

It is thus God Himself who performs the invocation, first and foremost; man's invocation is but a shadow, a reflection, a consequence of this divine invocation. The many-sidedness of the Imam's brief sentence opens onto a number of fruitful meditations upon the meaning, the origin, the end, and the very substance of the dhikr. Among these stands the Quranic verse cited earlier: "Remember Me, I will remember you."

This perception of the reality of the invocation reveals its spiritual efficacy more clearly. The reality denoted by the term dhikr is no longer confined within the boundaries of the individual, still less definable in terms of his verbal or mental activity. In its primary, metaphysical significance, dhikr opens out into the realm of engendering — it implies the function of bringing into existence, the effusion of Being. The creative Word uttered by God — "Be! (كُنْ)" — which brings the possibilities of being into existence, can be seen in this light as a form of dhikr: God utters the "name" of the possible thing, "calling" it from nonexistence into existence. This passage from nothingness to being is what renders the possible thing "remembered," madhkur. Here again, the Imam's sentence can be read both as a commentary on a Quranic verse and as an anticipation of the highly elaborate cosmogony associated with Ibn al-Arabi.

The Quran poses the following question:

> هَلْ أَتَىٰ عَلَى ٱلْإِنسَنِ حِينٌۭ مِّنَ ٱلدَّهْرِ لَمْ يَكُن شَيْـًۭٔا مَّذْكُورًا
>
> "Hath there come upon man any moment in time when he was not a thing remembered?" (Al-Insan: 1).

Man's being a "thing remembered" is here tantamount to his coming into being, and his nothingness is equivalent to his being "not remembered" — that is, not yet called forth from the abyss of pre-existential hiddenness into the light of manifest existence. In this pre-existential state, the human being is, according to Ibn al-Arabi, "known," malum, but only by God. He refers to this verse in the following lines of al-Futuhat al-makkiyya (The Meccan Illuminations):

God said of Himself that He is al-Badi, the Originator, that is, He created that which had no like or exemplar in all the degrees of being, for He knows, by His all-encompassing nature, every single thing that enters into the degrees of being. Therefore He says, as regards the creation of man, that he "was not a thing remembered," for His is the remembrance, so a thing has being in the remembrance of the one who remembers it.

God's "remembrance" of man thus precedes man's remembrance of God — ontologically, not merely temporally. This yields another interpretation of the verse cited earlier: "and the remembrance of God is greater" — infinitely greater, that is, than man's remembrance of Him. The true being of man resides in the "remembrance" God has of him. While man's devotion to God is articulated through the utterance of the divine Name, God's creation of man is articulated through His utterance of the creative Word. In Ibn al-Arabi's cosmogony, the utterance of this Word is not simply an event in time; that Word never ceases to reverberate, furnishing each human soul with its divine substrate, support, or foundation — its mustanad ilahi. This Word "breathes" existence upon the immutable entity, al-ayn al-thabita, which, though inexistent in itself, determines the contours, form, and configuration of the being that will be manifested through it — just as a prism refracts the light passing through it, or as water takes on the color of its cup, an image used by Junayd and often quoted by Ibn al-Arabi.

The hadith al-haqiqa, attributed to Ali, contains the essence of the Akbari doctrine of the tajalli of the unique Light of God upon the differentiated "receptacles," the loci of manifestation for that Light. The Imam's disciple Kumayl questions him on the meaning of al-haqiqa. The response includes the following affirmations:

Al-haqiqa is the unveiling of the splendors of Majesty without any allusion; the effacement of what is falsely imagined (الْمَوْهُومُ) together with the clarity of what is truly known (الْمَعْلُومُ); the drawing of absolute unicity toward the quality of تَوْحِيد; a light dawning from the morning of eternity, its traces shimmering upon the temples of تَوْحِيد.

Al-Kashani comments on this last image: "That is, the manifestation of the Light of the one Essence upon the loci of manifestation of the qualities of the Real, the Essence, these being the loci of the entities of existents (أَعْيَانُ الْمَوْجُودَاتِ), which the Imam calls 'temples of تَوْحِيد,' that is, the forms of the divine Names in the station of تَوْحِيد." This mustanad ilahi, or divine support of each individual soul, is therefore its particular Lord, the divine Name that engenders and sustains it. The soul is but the locus of manifestation, the mazhar, where God discloses Himself in a particular, unique mode.

The very first words of the khutba with which Ibn al-Arabi opens the Futuhat al-makkiyya are these: "Praise be to Him who engendered things from nothing, then negated it; and established their being through the orientation toward them of His Words." When the divine Word turns toward a possible thing—this turning is تَوَجُّه—the Word's properties become manifest according to what that possible thing can receive. The possibility is then "breathed" into existence and becomes like a "body," whose "heart" is the divine Name that brings it into being: "every slave has a Name which is its Lord; so it becomes the 'body' of that Name, which is its 'heart.'"

This comparison recalls the Imam's image of the dhikr as a "polish" for hearts. The Name revealed from "above" is mirrored by a Name that resounds from "within." This mutual reflection appears once the rust clouding the surface of the heart is removed by the polish of the dhikr. What is evoked here is a kind of union between two divine realities, and it brings to mind once more the phrase "light upon light."

Earlier the Imam had spoken of the Names of God "which have filled the foundations of all things," a phrase that anticipates Ibn al-Arabi's teaching that the divine Names are the supports and underlying ground of all things. A question arises here, and its answers bring one nearer to grasping the powerful and mysterious efficacy of the dhikr. What is the difference between the divine Names understood as the metaphysical roots of all things and the particular Names of God revealed in the Quran? On one side, the whole of creation is the "Book of God," and every phenomenon is one of God's "words." On the other, the revealed text holds specific words and Names, and it is these that are to be recited and invoked. Why?

One clear answer is given in the following verse:

> قُلِ ٱدْعُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ أَوِ ٱدْعُوا۟ ٱلرَّحْمَنَ ۖ أَيًّۭا مَّا تَدْعُوا۟ فَلَهُ ٱلْأَسْمَآءُ ٱلْحُسْنَىٰ ۚ وَلَا تَجْهَرْ بِصَلَاتِكَ وَلَا تُخَافِتْ بِهَا وَٱبْتَغِ بَيْنَ ذَلِكَ سَبِيلًۭا
>
> "Call upon Allah or call upon al-Rahman. By whatever Name you call upon Him, His are the most beautiful Names" (Al-Isra: 110).

The Quran itself commands believers to invoke these specific, revealed Names, and not others. These Names are therefore utterly unlike the names of the idolaters' gods:

> إِنْ هِىَ إِلَّآ أَسْمَآءٌۭ سَمَّيْتُمُوهَآ أَنتُمْ وَآبَآؤُكُم مَّآ أَنزَلَ ٱللَّهُ بِهَا مِن سُلْطَنٍ ۚ إِن يَتَّبِعُونَ إِلَّا ٱلظَّنَّ وَمَا تَهْوَى ٱلْأَنفُسُ ۖ وَلَقَدْ جَآءَهُم مِّن رَّبِّهِمُ ٱلْهُدَىٰٓ
>
> "They are but names that ye have named, ye and your fathers, for which God hath revealed no authority" (An-Najm: 23).

The names of idols are like the ordinary names given to things: arbitrary labels or signs with no real bond to the things themselves, and still less to the principles beneath those things. The Names of God, revealed by God, belong to another order. They are steeped in the very substance of the reality they name. For this reason they carry "authority" (سُلْطَان), and by virtue of that authority they have the power to transform and not merely to inform. They are, in other words, charged with a theurgic power, the capacity to render present to mind and heart the indwelling reality of God.

God's presence cannot be taken away, since nothing that exists can lie "outside" His all-encompassing presence; yet that presence can still become veiled, at least from the side of the one who perceives. The heart can be veiled from God by the "rust" of forgetfulness that settles over it, so that the divine Reality comes to be hidden by the very phenomena that manifest it. The Imam raises the question of how it is that the Creator both manifests Himself "by things" and is hidden from sight "by things."

This being so, two things are needed to open oneself to the divine presence within and beyond all things: a polishing of one's own "within" through the dhikr, and an unveiling or revelation from that "beyond." This second disclosure can be understood as a direct revelation of God within the indirect revelation of Him that is the whole created cosmos—a cosmos that reveals and veils the divine Reality at once. From one point of view the two revelations, cosmic and scriptural, are both manifestations of God; from another, the scriptural revelation is charged with a spiritual power that makes it incomparably more revealing than the created cosmos. As a spiritual method, the cosmos as manifested divine "Word" and Allah as a revealed Name of God share no common measure. The one is differentiated manifestation; the other, a unique revelation. The divine Name may also be called the quintessential manifestation, for it both sums up the entire realm of manifestation and transcends it.

In one respect the difference between cosmic manifestation and scriptural revelation is like the difference between shadow and light. Revelation is like a projection of the pure Light of God, and is therefore of one substance with the source from which it emanates; manifestation is like the shadow cast by that light. Both are projections of one and the same reality, yet this does not lessen how far apart light and darkness stand. In the words of Ibn al-Arabi, "The very Being of the Real is a verified Light, the very existence of creation is a shadow following after."

There is a remarkable supplication attributed to Ali, the Dua al-Mashlul, the "supplication of the lame man." It runs to one hundred and twenty-two verses, filled with a great many divine Names, attributes, and titles. Near its end comes a verse that may be taken as the key to the entire text: "I ask Thee by every Name with which Thou hast named Thyself." It is because God "names Himself" that His Names hold their theurgic power, their sacramental substance, their mystical efficacy. The revealed substance of the Names is what accounts for these distinctive properties and makes them unlike any other names. This is why, as Sufi texts so commonly affirm, the Name is the Named (اَلِاسْمُ هُوَ الْمُسَمَّى).

These reflections on how the Names of God stand in relation to being do illuminate certain sides of the dhikr as a mystical practice, yet they fall short of explaining that leap of consciousness in the order of being, that extinction of self-awareness, to which the Imam points in the words, "you will not remember Him according to the true reality of the remembrance until you forget your own soul in your remembrance." A question then follows: why and how does the dhikr bring about this extinction of consciousness, this effacement of individual being, especially when the very work of the dhikr seems to be the opposite—to awaken and bring on a heightened mode of consciousness and to deepen one's quality of being?

<!-- editorial:begin -->
> **Editorial note (tradition-grounded).** 
> The "spiritual amnesia" this chapter names, ghafla, is worth pausing over, since the very
> remembrance it prescribes is defined against it. The Arabic root غ-ف-ل describes an untended
> emptiness — land with no landmark, a mind with no attention — and ghafla is its inward form: a
> heedless forgetfulness of God, the exact opposite of the wakeful remembrance that dhikr demands.
> That opposition is why the chapter can treat distraction from the Name as distraction from God
> Himself, and why the invocation must be performed abundantly rather than merely contemplated. To
> invoke much, in this light, is to keep the field of the heart tended against the very emptiness
> the word describes.
<!-- editorial:end -->

## 13. Extinction and the Name: The Death of the Saint

The first response to that question begins with a limit. A person cannot, by his own effort, cross from remembrance into forgetfulness. Someone who is conscious, and who is concentrating on the act of invocation, cannot make himself unconscious of himself in the course of that invocation. The Imam's phrasing is exact: the soul is to be forgotten 'in' the remembrance. Any effort to will oneself into unconsciousness comes from a conscious will, and so it deepens self-consciousness rather than erasing it. The point, then, is not to force oneself to forget oneself while invoking God. It is rather to concentrate on the Name so completely that no room is left in one's awareness — no 'space,' whether mental or existential — for anything but the Named, the reality being invoked. A first degree of self-effacement can therefore be understood as a natural by-product of that total concentration.

The full completion of self-effacement in فَنَاء, however, comes as a divine grace. It exceeds all individual effort, even though the act of invocation is understood to draw this grace down, or to clear from the heart the 'rust' that hides from a person his own nothingness before the sole reality of God, 'that which has no second'. This part of the response can be gathered up in an esoteric reading of the Quranic words, ... thou wilt not forget, unless God willeth it so (Al-Ala: 6-7). Al-Kalabadhi, who wrote one of the most important early manuals of Sufism, opens his chapter on dhikr with words that echo the Imam's: 'The haqiqa of the dhikr is this: that you forget everything other than the madhkur (the Invoked) in the dhikr. For He says: "So remember thy Lord when thou hast forgotten" (Al-Kahf: 24). That is, when you have forgotten what is not God, then you have remembered God.'

The second response can be framed through the principle of inverse analogy. Human invocation is analogous to divine invocation: it is a kind of reflection of it. And like any reflected image, human invocation both shares in what it reflects and turns it upside down. In the first respect, the act of invocation brings the invoker a fuller consciousness and a fuller share in being. In the second respect, human invocation reaches its completion in what looks like the very opposite of this: the effacement of the individual in the object he invokes. Yet the opposition is only apparent. In reality this effacement is the fulfillment of that same growth in consciousness and being, though its final stage is a sudden leap — the negation of the very limits that make a person a separate individual. The individual is limited, and a limit is itself a kind of negation; so to negate this negation amounts to pure affirmation: the affirmation of unconditional being that lies beyond the individual. On the plane of the self, this negation of a negation can be seen as the reflection of the negation of nothingness that Ibn al-Arabi expresses in his line, 'Praise be to Him who engendered things from nothing, then negated it.'

Here the Imam's enigmatic saying returns: 'I am the death of the dead.' It recalls the words Rumi places in the Imam's mouth in these lines of his Mathnawi:

He said, 'I am wielding the sword for God's sake, I am the servant of God, I am not under the command of the body.

I am the Lion of God, I am not the lion of my passion: my deed bears witness to my religion.

In war I am (manifesting the truth of) thou didst not throw when thou threwest: I am (but) as the sword, and the wielder is the (divine) Sun.

I have removed the baggage of self out of the way, I have deemed (what is) other than God to be non-existence.

I am a shadow, the Sun is my lord; I am the chamberlain, I am not the curtain (which prevents approach) to Him.

I am filled with the pearls of union, like a (jewelled) sword: in battle I make (men) living, not slain.

The cosmic unfolding of creation moves from God to man, and it requires the negation of a relative nothingness that is pregnant with the possibilities of being. Man's realization of God moves the other way, and it requires the negation of that same 'nothingness' once breathed into being by God. Ibn al-Arabi's treatise Extinction in Contemplation names what is at stake: 'the extinction of that which never was ... and the subsistence of that which never ceased to be.' The following Quranic verses are clearly implied: 'Everything thereon perisheth (فَانٍۢ), and there subsisteth (يَبْقَىٰ) only the Face of thy Lord, possessor of glory and bounty' (Ar-Rahman: 26-27).

Yet everything that exists has two aspects, or two 'faces': a divine face, which is the thing's true being, and a created face, which is 'nothing'. The 'Face' of the Lord that alone subsists is therefore not only the divine Reality above and beyond all things, but that same reality within all things. This is the meaning of al-Ghazali's esoteric commentary on the Quranic verse, 'Everything perisheth except His Face' (Al-Qasas: 88). Al-Ghazali comments:

[it is] not that it perisheth at some particular moment, but rather it is forever perishing, since it cannot be conceived except as perishing. For each thing other than God is, when considered in and by itself, pure nothingness; and if considered from the face (وَجْهَهُ) to which existence flows to it from the Prime Reality, it is viewed as existing, but not in itself, solely from the face which accompanies Him Who gives it existence. Therefore, the divine face is the sole thing in reality.

He then ties the mystical realization of this oneness to the invocation of God and to the forgetting of the self within that invocation, and so he gives a valuable commentary on what this total dissolution of self-consciousness means. The Sufis who verify this oneness through mystical 'taste' (ذَوْقٌ), he writes, are 'drowned in oneness', so that they become 'dumbfounded':

No capacity remained within them save to recall Allah; yea, not so much as the capacity to recall their own selves ... when this state prevails, it is called in relation to him who experiences it Extinction, nay Extinction of Extinction, for the soul has become extinct to itself, extinct to its own extinction; for it becomes unconscious of itself and unconscious of its own unconsciousness, since were it conscious of its own unconsciousness, it would be conscious of itself.

Relevant here too is a saying that al-Qushayri attributes to Abu 'Uthman, which throws light on another side of the unconsciousness' reached through invocation: 'Whoever has not tasted the loneliness of unconsciousness will not find the food of the intimacy of remembrance.' Here losing consciousness of oneself is called 'loneliness', while becoming fully conscious of God through invocation yields 'intimacy'. One cannot find true intimacy with the divine Reality except by stripping the self of its false 'intimacy' — that self-satisfied, habitual self-centeredness which is the shadow cast by self-awareness. It is this awareness of self that, in the Imam's statement, must be forgotten. Such higher forgetfulness can arise only on the ground of the remembrance of God; for only the source of Being, unconditional Consciousness, and eternal Life can extinguish, eclipse, or efface the relative existence, partial consciousness, and apparent life of the individual. This, the haqiqa of the dhikr, is put with perfect economy in the Imam's own words: ... forget your own soul in your remembrance.

The state of فَنَاء, the highest degree of this 'forgetting' of oneself, is therefore a grace. The individual cannot attain it, however hard he strives. Yet this does not mean the invoker gives up effort. On the contrary, as the Sufi tradition attests again and again, فَنَاء is closely bound to the practice of dhikr, and is in fact its very climax. After this state, or hal, of extinction comes the station, or maqam, of subsistence, baqa: a return of consciousness to the self — a self now transfigured by the knowledge of reality that was impressed upon the soul in the state of فَنَاء. For it is only in the state of فَنَاء that the final 'vision' of absolute oneness is reached, a vision that leaves no room for the duality of being which clings inescapably to individuality. To assert the 'individual' is to assert its correlate, the Lord, and so to affirm duality.

Al-Kashani makes this point in his commentary on the Quranic verse, 'Remember Him as He hath guided you' (Al-Baqarah: 198), where he traces the guided ascent through the degrees of the dhikr. The first is the dhikr of the tongue, which corresponds to the soul (النَّفْس). Then comes the dhikr of the heart (الْقَلْب), which corresponds to the divine acts, those that make His graces manifest. Then the dhikr of the 'secret' (اَلسِّرُّ), which corresponds to the vision of the divine acts together with 'the unveiling of the sciences of the manifestation of the divine qualities'. Then the dhikr of the spirit (الرُّوح), corresponding to 'the contemplation of the lights of the manifestations of the divine qualities, with an apprehension of the light of the Essence'. Then comes the dhikr of 'the hidden' (الْخَفِيُّ), which is 'the contemplation of the beauty of the Essence along with the subsistence of duality'. And finally the dhikr of the Essence, 'which is essential vision, attained through the removal of duality.'

The same point emerges from the counsel of Ibn 'Ataillah, which shows how the dhikr opens a person to the divine presence, and to the grace by which alone the individual can truly forget' himself — that is, become 'unconscious of his own unconsciousness', as al-Ghazali put it:

Do not abandon the invocation because you do not feel the Presence of God therein. For your forgetfulness of the invocation of Him is worse than your forgetfulness in the invocation of Him. Perhaps He will take you from an invocation with forgetfulness (غَفْلَةٌ) to one with vigilance (يَقَظَة), and from one with vigilance to one with the Presence of God (حُضُور), and from one with the Presence of God to one wherein everything but the Invoked (الْمَذْكُورُ) is absent. 'And that is not difficult for God.'

In his commentary on the Hikam, Ibn 'Ajiba adds: So, in the measure that one is extinguished in the Name, one is extinguished in the Essence.' This follows from the principle that the Name is the Named. In another work, a commentary on a poem by Shushtari on the divine Name Allah, he sets out this central tenet of the invocatory discipline in Sufism more plainly. The first line of the poem reads, speaking of the letters that make up the word Allah: 'The alif before two lams, then a ha': coolness of the eyes. Ibn 'Ajiba comments: The "coolness of the eyes", this is joy, for tears of joy, as one knows by experience, are cool. The author means this: this Name is the joy of my heart, its happiness, its beatitude.' He then quotes some lines from al-Hallaj on the letters of the Name Allah, adds a verse of his own, and follows with this comment: 'The Name is not other than the Named, for joy cannot come from anything but the essence of a thing, not from the name as such.'

This sums up the doctrine that the Name and the Named are identical — the rationale for the Sufi practice of dhikruLlah. Yet a problem arises here. However closely the Imam's statements on the dhikr may agree with these later Sufi expressions of the same underlying principles, this particular emphasis on the identity of the Name and the Named appears, on the surface, to conflict with many sayings of the Shii Imams that deny the Name is the Named. Imam Jafar al-Sadiq, for instance, is reported to have said:

The Name of Allah is other than Him. For everything to which the name of a thing is applicable is a creation, hence other than God. For whatever the tongue expresses, or is worked on by the hand, is a creation ... everything described is created, and the Creator of things cannot be described within the limits of a named object.

Allah has ninety-nine Names. If the Name were the Named, then every one of the Names would be a god. But Allah is the one meaning, or spiritual reality, mana, indicated by the Names, and all of them are other than Him.

This apparent contradiction can be resolved in two ways. First, one must weigh carefully what the word 'Name' means in each of the two contexts: the context of doctrinal distinction, where the Absolute is held strictly apart from the relative, and the Essence from all forms; and the context of methodic concentration, where the Absolute is understood to be the reality of the very form through which it reveals itself. One can then turn to one of the most esoteric aspects of how the Shii Imams understood themselves: their references to their own selves as the 'most beautiful Names' (ٱلْأَسْمَآءُ ٱلْحُسْنَىٰ).

As for the meaning of 'Name', Ibn al-Arabi offers a precise definition: [T]he Names have two connotations; the first connotation is God Himself who is what is named, the second that by which one Name is distinguished from another ... As being essentially the other, the Name is the Reality, while as being not the other, it is the imagined Reality. On the one hand, each Name is identical with every other Name, because each is essentially identical with the Named — the one Essence that is the source of all the Names. On the other hand, each Name has its own particular meaning and quality: it is the relationship through which the one Reality engages the created cosmos. In this second respect each Name is distinct from the others, and distinction implies limitation, and so relativity, and in the end a kind of illusion before the one and only Reality. It is in this sense that the Name takes on the character of 'imagined Reality'. For Ibn al-Arabi, the Names have no separate existence of their own; they all return to the one and only Reality, that of the divine Essence. This dissolves the problem raised in the saying of Imam Jafar — the problem that different Names would imply different 'gods'.

A lexicon of Sufi terms, the Lataif al-ilam fi isharat ahl al-ilham (Subtle Disclosures in the Allusions of the Folk of Inspiration), attributed to al-Kashani, gives a definition of the divine Names that also helps to distinguish these two connotations. Under the term 'Names of the Divinity', the text reads:

These are, in the terminology of the Sufis, expressive of the manifest aspect of being, in respect of its determination within a specific meaning. For every divine Name is the manifest aspect of being, which, in turn, is identical to the Essence, but not as the Essence is in itself; rather, in respect of its specification and determination within a particular meaning, or what one might call a 'quality'. For example, the Name al-Hayy ('The Living') is a Name of manifest, determined being, but in respect of its specification and determination as a particular meaning, it is Life. So in regard to being itself, al-Hayy is identical to the Essence, while in regard to its determination as a particular meaning, it is other than the Essence. If you have understood what we have said, you will know what is meant by the saying: the Name is not identical to the Essence, the Named, neither is it other than It.

The Name and the Named differ in name and in thought, and in this respect the Name is not the Named. Yet the two are also spiritually assimilated, and in this respect the Name is the Named. A further distinction must then be drawn. The spoken form of the Name is one thing; the Name as an attribute of the Essence is another. Taken as an attribute, the Name is nothing but the divine quality it designates, and that quality is in turn identical to the Essence. Taken as the 'name' of this Name, it is merely a spoken form. Qaysari, another leading commentator on Ibn al-Arabi, brings this distinction to light in the introduction to his commentary on the Fusus al-hikam.

The Essence receives a Name when it is considered together with some determined quality and in relation to one of Its theophanies. 'The Compassionate' has an essence, which is compassion; 'The All-Conquering' has an essence, which is all-conquering power. Such spoken Names are Names of Names, and this is how the saying is to be understood: the Name is identical to the Named.

In his monumental commentary on Qaysari's introduction, Jalal al-Din Ashtiyani, a contemporary authority on Shii 'Irfan, adds an important observation after quoting this passage. It returns the discussion to two earlier threads: extinction through the invocation of the Name, and the point that the Imams themselves are the most beautiful Names'. The saints, Ashtiyani writes, often speak of the Names as one with the Named because they have in mind the referent (مِصْدَاقٌ), which is the الصِّفَة, the divine attribute or quality: 'The الصِّفَات are in one respect the specifications of the Essence, while in the station of unicity they are annihilated in the Essence, and are thus identical to the Essence.'

This metaphysical account of how the Name, the الصِّفَة, and the Essence stand to one another mirrors, on the objective and universal plane, the path the soul travels inwardly and mystically when it is extinguished in and by the invocation of the Name. This extinction is what فَنَاء means: the perishing of egoistic consciousness before the Absolute. The الصِّفَات are 'annihilated' in the Essence and are thereby absolutely and unconditionally one with it; in the same way, the soul extinguished by the Name reaches at a single stroke both its total nullification and its ultimate realization — no longer as an individual soul. This 'extinction' on the plane of the individual soul amounts to 'realization' on the plane of transcendence only by virtue of the divine theophany, the particular divine Name, the 'heart' that forms the soul's true being, its mustanad ilahi. Here the deepest reality of تَوْحِيد comes to light. The individual no longer affirms that 'there is no god but God', for the individual has been extinguished by the reality that 'there is no reality but the one and only Reality'. God is 'that which has no second'. Just as 'No one has truly said Allah but Allah', so too no one realizes God but God. God alone comes to 'know' or 'be' Himself, and this is one aspect of the purpose of creation, as the hadith qudsi indicates: 'I was a hidden treasure and I loved to be known.'

Seen in this light, the saints (اَلْأَوْلِيَاءُ) are perfectly self-realized theophanies, wholly effaced in that of which they are the theophanic manifestations; and their very effacement in the divine Reality is the ultimate source of their knowledge of it. This is all the more true of the Prophet himself, the prototype of all the saints and the 'perfect man', al-insan al-kamil. On this point Sabziwari, in his commentary on the divine Names, cites the saying of Ali (ع), 'We are the most beautiful Names': 'There is no doubt that they are His most exalted indicators and His greatest signs (عَلَائِمِهِ الْعُظْمَى وَآيَاتِهِ الْكُبْرَى), as the Prophet said, God bless him and his family, "Whoso sees me has seen the Truth (اَلْحَقّ)." He goes on to equate the spiritual station, or inner reality, of the Imams with that of the divine Names and attributes, while stressing that the divine Essence transcends all Names and attributes and remains forever ineffable, known only to itself. The Prophet, the Imam, the saint, the perfect man', thus manifest those aspects of the divine nature that can be perceived on the human plane. The more the divine Essence is exalted in transcendence above all Names and attributes, the more brilliantly those Names and attributes are reflected in the mirror that the 'perfect man' constitutes. The unknowable transcendence of the hidden 'treasure' is, in some measure, offset by the faithful reflection of its communicable aspects in the 'friend of God'.

Identifying the Imam with the divine Name or attribute has implications worth weighing, for in one respect such an identification would appear to be شِرْك — the 'association' of a creature, however exalted, with the transcendent Reality. The same charge appears to apply to the ecstatic utterances (شَطْحِيَّاتٌ) of many of the most celebrated Sufis, such as the anal-Haqq ('I am the Real') of al-Hallaj and the subhani ('Glory to me') of Bayazid. Yet from a viewpoint shaped by the Imam's own conception of the divine attributes, a very different picture emerges — one that, far from being tainted by شِرْك, in fact reaches the most profound application of تَوْحِيد. The first sermon of the Nahj al-balagha contains these remarkable and highly important statements about the attributes of God:

The perfection of purification (أَخْلَاص) is to divest Him of all attributes (الصِّفَات) because of the testimony of every attribute that it is other than the object of attribution, and because of the testimony of every such object that it is other than the attribute. So whoever ascribes an attribute to God, glorified be He, has conjoined Him with something else; and whoever so conjoins Him has made Him twofold; and whoever makes Him twofold has fragmented Him; and whoever thus fragments Him is ignorant of Him.

When the Imam says that pure devotion to God requires stripping God of all attributes, what is negated is not the divine attributes themselves. What is negated is any notion of them as separate, self-standing, or added-on realities — as things substantially 'other than' the Essence of God, or set apart from it in their very being. The reality of God's attributes is plainly affirmed in an earlier phrase of the same sermon: 'He whose attribute has no binding limitation (الَّذِي لَيْسَ لِصِفَتِهِ حَدٌّ مَحْدُودٌ).' What the sermon denies is that the reality of any divine attribute can be confined within the bounds of human thought and language. No divine attribute can stand apart from the divine nature; that nature is infinite; and so every attribute is of infinite scope as well, beyond all the limits that formal thought and verbal definition presuppose.

The attribute or Name, then, is to be affirmed in one respect and negated in another: affirmed as not other than the Essence, negated insofar as it is regarded in itself, on its own account. This same metaphysical view of how the attribute or Name stands to the Essence can be applied, mystically, to how the individual soul stands to the divine Reality. On its own account the individual is nothing; but in respect of the divine ground beneath it — the divine Name, the mustanad ilahi that forms its essential reality — it is not other than the unique Real. As Ibn al-Arabi puts it, 'The final end and ultimate return of the gnostics is that the Real is identical with them, while they do not exist.' This captures perfectly the twofold aspect of the realized sage's soul: insofar as he is aware that 'he does not exist', he knows that the Real is his true identity. However paradoxical it seems, the mystic's realization of absolute Reality goes hand in hand with his realization of his own non-existence. Ibn al-Arabi expresses these two aspects of one and the same realization in compressed form. He describes the summit of his spiritual ascent, his miraj, recounting that God removed from him his 'contingent dimension (إِمْكَانٌ), resulting in the attainment of the inner realities of all the Names'. He continues:

Thus I attained in this nocturnal journey the inner realities (مَعَانِي) of all the Names, and I saw them returning to One Subject (مُسَمَّى) and One Entity (عَيْنٌ): that Subject was what I witnessed and that Entity was my Being. For my voyage was only in myself and pointed to myself, and through this I came to know that I was a pure servant without a trace of lordship in me at all.

This passage helps to explain so many of the ecstatic utterances of the Sufis and the seemingly self-deifying statements of the Imams. Their personal servitude to God is not denied. On the contrary, it is fully realized through the knowledge of their true identity, for that identity as the Named is reached only when their individuality is effaced. Like the divine attributes, they are mustahlak, annihilated in the divine Essence, so that no individual appropriation of the glory of God is possible. When the Imam appears to speak of his own greatness, this is nothing but the greatness of God expressed through him as a mazhar, a locus for the theophanic manifestation of the divine — and expressed precisely in the measure of his own effacement in that greatness.

The same point is reinforced by what 'Attar recounts about Imam Jafar al-Sadiq in his Tadhkiratul-awliya'. Someone accused him of being proud, and the Imam replied: 'I am not proud, for pride pertains to the divine greatness. Since I have gone beyond the pride of self, His pride has come and taken the place of my own pride. With pride of self present, there can be no divine greatness, but through the divine greatness, pride will be present.' The divine qualities shine with greater brilliance, not less, through the prism of a soul made transparent to the divine Reality — a transparency that comes from the dissolving of all the clouds of egoism and passion.

A holy utterance bears on this: 'My slave draws near to Me through nothing I love more than that which I have made obligatory for him. My slave never ceases to draw near to Me through supererogatory acts (نَوَافِلُ) until I love him. And when I love him, I am his hearing by which he hears, his sight by which he sees, his hand by which he grasps, and his foot by which he walks.'

In the light of all this, the paradox this divine utterance contains can now be resolved. On the one hand, the thing most beloved to God appears to be the obligatory rites. On the other, it is the supererogatory rites that carry the slave near to God until He loves him — and that love seems the greater, since it issues in the divine penetration of the human faculties, the unitive realization proper to تَوْحِيد. The paradox dissolves once it is seen that God's 'love' for the obligatory rites is a love directed toward something apart from Himself, whereas His love for the 'slave' — the pure slave effaced in his Lord — is infinitely greater, since it shares in God's love for His own Self-manifestation, and so for Himself. In the beginning God 'loved to be known', and so He created the world. In 'knowing' Himself through the effaced slave, the seeming 'other', His love to be known is realized through the saint whose greatest glory is precisely his own effacement, poverty, and emptiness of self.

It is in this sense that the following statement of the Imam is to be understood: 'Down from me torrents do pour; up to me no bird can soar.' Torrents pour down from him because — and in the measure that — he is extinguished in the source of the torrent. The image recalls the Quranic description of the 'slaves of God' who, unlike the 'righteous', drink directly from the celestial spring of Kafur, identified in the end with the divine Essence: 'Truly, the righteous shall drink from a cup flavoured with Kafur, a spring wherefrom the slaves of God drink, making it gush forth abundantly' (Al-Insan: 5-6). These slaves make the spring flow more abundantly because, in al-Kashani's words, 'they are themselves the sources of this spring; there is no duality or otherness. Were it otherwise, it would not be the fountain of Kafur, because of the darkness of the veil of egoity (ana iyya) and duality.'

The final degree of the dhikr, as al-Kashani defined it, is the dhikr of the Essence, 'which is essential vision, attained through the removal of duality'. The dhikr 'polishes' away not only the impurities of the heart and mind but, in the end, through the grace of the madhkur, every taint of duality — and above all that root supposition of duality, the individual as such. It removes all subtle or hidden شِرْك, the 'partners' in being that are non-existent before the One 'that has no second', and it establishes the spiritual principle of تَوْحِيد, so that the heart, transfigured by the knowledge of the sole Reality, comes to see the light of that Reality reflected in all the mirrors of creation. To realize this one and only Reality — to 'make it real' in the fullest sense — is, by the same stroke, to realize the unreality, or merely apparent reality, of everything else.

At this level of realization a measure of perplexity inevitably attends the revelation of the divine Reality on every side. The more the divine Essence is exalted beyond all comprehension, the more unavoidable it becomes that creaturely consciousness must be utterly annihilated if that Essence is to be 'realized', if gnosis of it is to be reached. On the one hand, one 'knows' God by knowing that His Essence is unknowable, utterly other, utterly transcendent — the essence of the Creator lying beyond the veil that creation itself forms. On the other hand, the very distinction between creature and Creator is effaced once the creature has 'forgotten' itself, not merely as a notion but existentially, in a state of genuine contemplative extinction. It is then no longer a matter of one form of self-consciousness replacing another, but of the 'self' being taken out of the reckoning altogether, leaving only pure consciousness as such — which cannot 'belong' to any entity without being reduced to the relativity proper to that entity. Consciousness of the Absolute, that is, 'being conscious of the Absolute', is thus transmuted into absolute consciousness. The Real is 'realized' not by another but through another, which is the source of the title al-arif biLlah, literally 'the knower through God' rather than simply 'the knower of God'. The Imam says, 'Know God through God.' Knowledge of God at this deepest level is the Real knowing itself through the creature; and to say 'through' implies an unobstructed passage, the creature being marked by perfect faqr — poverty, self-effacement, and, in the end, extinction.

Realization through remembrance, however, means not only "forgetting" everything in existence except God, but also discovering that in reality there is nothing in being but God. In the Imam's image of the "polish" worked by remembrance, the heart — once blind and deaf — is made able to "see" and to "hear." It sees the Face of God wherever it turns, and it hears the cosmic hymn of remembrance and praise that the whole of creation sings, for every creature proclaims the boundless creativity of its Creator. As the Quran says:

> وَلِلَّهِ ٱلْمَشْرِقُ وَٱلْمَغْرِبُ ۚ فَأَيْنَمَا تُوَلُّوا۟ فَثَمَّ وَجْهُ ٱللَّهِ ۚ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ وَسِعٌ عَلِيمٌۭ
>
> "Wherever ye turn, there is the Face of God" (Al-Baqarah: 115).

> أَلَمْ تَرَ أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُسَبِّحُ لَهُۥ مَن فِى ٱلسَّمَوَتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱلطَّيْرُ صَٓفَّتٍۢ ۖ كُلٌّۭ قَدْ عَلِمَ صَلَاتَهُۥ وَتَسْبِيحَهُۥ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ عَلِيمٌۢ بِمَا يَفْعَلُونَ
>
> "Do you not see that God is the one who is glorified by everything in the heavens and the earth, and the birds with wings outstretched; each one indeed knows its prayer and its glorification, and God is All-Aware of what they do" (An-Nur: 41).

A final reflection turns to a passage from the Imam's famous and much-disputed Khutbat al-bayan, "The Sermon of Clarifying Expression":

I am the Sign of the All-Powerful. I am the gnosis of the mysteries. I am the Threshold of Thresholds. I am the companion of the radiance of the divine Majesty. I am the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden. I am the Face of God. I am the mirror of God, the supreme Pen, the Tabula secreta. I am he who in the Gospel is called Elijah. I am he who is in possession of the secret of God's Messenger.

This remarkable affirmation of the Imam's inmost reality may seem shocking to the exoteric mentality, and is all too easily misread as a blasphemous self-deification of the kind associated with Hallaj. One key to understanding these declarations of "his" true nature correctly is the statement: "I am the gnosis of the mysteries." Once this is grasped, every other statement falls naturally into place. For here the Imam is not identifying himself with God, but with the knowledge of God — and not with his own knowledge of God, since that would still be a relative knowledge, belonging to him as a relative being. Only knowledge itself — the knowledge God has of Himself, not the knowledge a human being has of God — can be fully identified with its object. The distinctions between knowledge, knower, and known dissolve only at this transcendent level, just as the distinction between knowledge and being ceases to operate there. Thus the knower is the known, when the knowledge in question is that consciousness which the divine Reality has of itself. Individual consciousness reaches this knowledge only on one condition: the utter extinction of every trace of individual, creaturely selfhood. To "remember" God, one must have "forgotten" oneself. To "be" the knowledge of God is, by a kind of metaphysical reverberation, to "be" everything that God is; for the consciousness that is wholly at one with the Absolute is nothing other than "the radiance of the divine Majesty." Insofar as this consciousness is one with "the gnosis of the mysteries" — and these mysteries (أَسْرَارٌ) are all rooted in the ultimate mystery (اَلسِّرُّ), the one and only Essence of God — it is necessarily one with all the divine attributes. It is in this sense that the Imam can proclaim: "I am the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden."

The same reasoning applies to the claim to be "the Face of God" (wajh Allah). It becomes intelligible only once one grasps that the Imam describes himself as the "mirror" of God. A mirror loses its identity as a mirror when attention rests entirely on the object reflected in it: the mirror's own surface becomes, as it were, invisible, and only the object is seen. Conversely, when the mirror itself becomes the object of attention, the face reflected in it can no longer be seen. The mirror is thus identified with what it reflects — which in a certain sense is "in" the mirror — yet the mirror does not cease to be a mirror, and the Face does not cease to be wholly independent of it. The "veil" here is the mirror itself. To attend to the mirror as a mirror prevents one from seeing the object it reflects; but once attention rests wholly on the image the mirror holds, the mirror ceases to be a veil and instead reveals the object itself. Within this symbolism, forgetting the mirror answers to the effacement of the self, and fixing on the image within it answers to the remembrance of God.

Yet this leads to another paradox: the deepest effacement of individuality goes hand in hand with the most perfect expression of humanity. The "friends of God," who know themselves to be nothing before the Absolute, are precisely those who most faithfully reflect the divine Names and qualities — qualities that belong not to them but to God. This is why the great saints and sages are also models of virtue, virtue whose true foundations are the "most beautiful Names" and attributes of God. Those divine attributes shine through them most clearly because the mirrors of their hearts have been polished by the remembrance of God, and the most stubborn stains — those left by egotism — have been removed. The saint's creativity is raised to its highest pitch precisely because the divine qualities reflected by the heart are no longer blocked — on the inner side, by the individual coagulations that egotism generates, nor, on the outer side, by the cosmic coagulations of this lower world. For such saints are those whose "hearts are in the Gardens [of Paradise] while their bodies are at work." Their hearts, bound and attached to that divine realm — the abodes and stations of paradise reaching all the way to the divine presence — are already "within" that transcendent realm; they already live more in that spiritual world than in this earthly one. And so, though they labor in this world with their bodies, their outward actions veil their inward transcendence of higher realities: they act, but they are not bound by the fruits of their action.

Such souls have not merely understood this principle; they have been seized by it, in their very existence, at the root of their being. It is the principle voiced in the Quranic verse:

> وَمَا هَذِهِ ٱلْحَيَوٰةُ ٱلدُّنْيَآ إِلَّا لَهْوٌۭ وَلَعِبٌۭ ۚ وَإِنَّ ٱلدَّارَ ٱلْآخِرَةَ لَهِىَ ٱلْحَيَوَانُ ۚ لَوْ كَانُوا۟ يَعْلَمُونَ
>
> "And the life of this world is naught but sport and play; and truly, the abode of the Hereafter, that is true life" (Al-Ankabut: 64).

Their instincts are therefore always attuned to that higher reality. When Ali was struck by the poisoned sword of Ibn Muljam, the cry that broke from him was not one of pain but of triumph: "I have triumphed, by the Lord of the Kaba!" At the very moment of death there was in him nothing but the remembrance of God, and the Imam passed away, it is said, with his tongue still repeating the words la ilaha illaLlah. His death was thus completed exactly as his life had been lived, in accord with the wisdom of the Prophet, who, when asked which of all acts is the most meritorious, replied: "That you die while your tongue is moistened with the invocation of God."

All the rites and practices bound up with the chivalric orders of Islam were traced, without exception, back to the Prophet's son-in-law, Ali ibn Abi Talib, who was thereby established as the supreme source of the virtues of futuwwa, as Muhammad Jafar Mahjub observes in his "Chivalry and Early Persian Sufism." S. H. Nasr writes to similar effect that Ali, having received the "truth and power" of futuwwa from the Prophet, "became the supreme source of futuwwa in Islam for both Sunnis and Shiites... The personality of Ali, at once sage and knight, contemplative and protector of laborers and craftsmen, has continued to dominate through the centuries over the horizon of futuwwa as it has over much of Sufism." Nasr also draws attention to the organic link between futuwwa and the guilds, the asnaf, singular sinf, of craftsmen and artists established throughout the length and breadth of the Muslim world.

It is often said that the Naqshabandi tariqa is the one exception to this rule, tracing its line of descent from the Prophet through the first caliph, Abu Bakr, rather than through Ali. But this affiliation with Abu Bakr belongs to only one of its three principal silsilas. 'Abd al-Majid b. Muhammad al-Khani, himself a Naqshabandi-Khalidi shaykh, calls the first chain (السِّلْسِلَةُ الْأُولَى) the "golden chain," which begins with Ali and runs through the Shii Imams down to Ali b. Musa, then to Maruf al-Karkhi and the other masters. The second chain (السِّلْسِلَةُ الثَّانِيَةُ) likewise begins with Ali, running through Hasan al-Basri. Only the third chain, called al-silsilat al-siddiqiyya, runs from the Prophet to Abu Bakr (الصِّدِّيقُ).

## 14. The First Sermon of the Nahj al-Balagha

The opening sermon of ⟪ar:نھج البلاغہ⟫ — delivered by Maulana Ali (ع) as a ⟪ar:خطبۃ⟫ — proclaims the oneness of God: a oneness that stands outside the category of numbers altogether. The sermon is built on paradox. The loftier the truth Maulana Ali means to reveal, the deeper the paradox he forms to reveal it. By negating the reach of human words and concepts, he affirms the Reality that no word and no concept can contain. That is the manner of his sermons. The eye cannot see Him, yet the mind cannot deny Him. He is farther than the farthest flight of imagination, yet nearer than the jugular vein. Nowhere is this plainer than in these opening lines.

The sermon opens: Praise be to God, الحَمْدُ للهِ الَّذَي لاَ يَبْلُغُ مِدْحَتَهُ القَائِلُونَ — whose laudation those who speak cannot deliver. The paradox is immediate. Speech exists to carry a concept from one mind to another; here it is turned to demonstrate that God is so lofty and sublime that words can carry nothing of Him. Whose graces those who count cannot number, وَلاِ يُحْصِي نَعْمَاءَهُ العَادُّونَ — for counting belongs to things that can be enumerated, and His bounties surpass all enumeration. Whose rightful due those who strive cannot render, ولاَ يُؤَدِّي حَقَّهُ الُمجْتَهِدُونَ — for however hard they strive, the praise and gratitude He is owed remains forever beyond their reach. He cannot be reached by far-reaching aspirations, الَّذِي لاَ يُدْركُهُ بُعْدُ الهِمَمِ, nor fathomed by profound intuitions, وَلاَ يَنَالُهُ غَوْصُ الفِطَنِ.

He whose attribute admits no binding limitation, no settled description, no appointed time, no extended term — الَّذِي لَيْسَ لِصِفَتِهِ حَدٌّ مَحْدُودٌ، وَلاَ نَعْتٌ مَوْجُودٌ، وَلا وَقْتٌ مَعْدُودٌ — so that His attribute cannot be confined within a definition and yet is affirmed as real, described in the very words of revelation even though no description can capture Him. Through His power He brought all created things into being; through His mercy He sent the comforting winds abroad; and with rocky pillars He entrenched His swaying earth.

Foremost in religion is knowledge of Him. The perfection of that knowledge is belief in Him; the perfection of belief is affirming His oneness; and the perfection of that affirmation is to purify one's devotion to Him — the perfection of sincerity, أخلاص — which means rendering both one's devotion and one's very conception of God entirely pure. The perfection of that purification is to divest Him of all attributes (نَفْیُ الصِّفَۃِ), because every attribute bears witness that it is other than the thing it is attributed to, and every such thing bears witness that it is other than the attribute.

So whoever ascribes an attribute to God, glorified be He, has conjoined Him with something else. Whoever conjoins Him has made Him twofold. Whoever makes Him twofold has fragmented Him. And whoever fragments Him is ignorant of Him. Whoever points to Him confines Him. Whoever confines Him counts Him. Whoever asks "in what?" encloses Him. And whoever asks "upon what?" isolates Him.

The very attributes by which He is named in revelation — that He is the All-Compassionate (رَحْمَٰن), the All-Merciful (رحیم), the Almighty (عزیز) — are affirmed as real and yet cannot be defined, bounded, or described. He is merciful, but not as any mercy the human mind can picture; powerful, but not as any storm or flood or force that creation has known. Reason itself "de-fines," drawing a boundary, a ⟪ar:حد⟫, around whatever it grasps. In breaking that boundary, the sermon opens an awareness beyond the reach of speculative thought: by negating the limits of human words, it affirms the Absolute that has no limit. This is the very movement of the testimony of faith — لا الہ, "no deity," which is the negation (نفی), and الا اللہ, "except God," which is the affirmation (اِثْبَات). Each attribute is negated only insofar as the limited mind conceives it, and is thereby affirmed as nothing but God Himself: La Rahmana illaLlah, there is no All-Compassionate but God; La Raheema illaLlah, there is no All-Merciful but God. Thus is God glorified above all that the mind would attribute to Him: "‏سُبْحَنَ ٱللَّهِ عَمَّا يَصِفُونَ," "Glorified is God above all that they attribute to Him!" (Surat al-Muminun, 23:91).

Being, but not by way of any becoming; existing, but not from having been non-existent; with every thing, but not through association; other than every thing, but not through separation; acting, but not through movements and instruments; seeing, even when nothing of His creation was to be seen; solitary, even when there was none whose intimacy might be sought or whose absence might be missed.

He brought forth creation, originating it from the very beginning, without pondering over any deliberation or benefiting from any previous experience, without initiating any movement or engaging in any unsettling exertion. He assigned to all things their span of time and harmonized their differences. He instilled within them their natural dispositions, impressing upon them their respective forms, knowing them before their genesis, encompassing their boundaries and their finalities, and aware of their configurations and their contours.

Then He, glorified be He, created a fissure in the atmospheres, cleaving apart directions and the uppermost levels of air, and caused water to flow therein — a current of surging waves swelling and swirling in overflowing abundance. He mounted it upon a tempestuous wind, a thunderous gale, then made that wind prevent the return of the water, enabling it to dominate the violence of its flow and aligning it with the boundaries of the water. The air beneath the wind spread wide open, while the water above flowed copiously.

Then He raised up another wind, making it sterile, fixing its course, intensifying its current, and distancing it from its source. He ordered it to agitate the swelling waters and to stir up oceanic waves. The wind churned up the waters as one churns milk in a milkbag, imparting to the water its own violence in the atmosphere, turning its foremost back upon its hindmost and its stillness into motion, so that its torrents surged and its swelling waves threw up foam. He then elevated that foam within a cleft air and a vast open atmosphere, and formed from it the seven heavens, making the lowest a suspended wave and the highest a protected shelter and an elevated ceiling, without any pillars to support them or any clinch to transfix them.

Then He adorned the heavens with the ornaments of the stars and the effulgence of the firmament, and made a radiant lamp — the sun — and an illuminating moon to follow an orbit in a revolving sphere, beneath a roof in motion and a firmament in flux.

Then He split up the highest heavens and filled them with angels of different dispositions:

- some prostrating and not bowing; others bowing and not standing upright;
- some arrayed in ranks, not separating from one another;
- others glorifying, never wearying, not overcome by sleepiness in the eyes, distraction in the mind, fatigue of the body, or the negligence of forgetfulness.

Among them are custodians of His Revelation and conveyors of His Words to His Messengers, and those who come and go with His Decree and His Command. Among them are the guardians of His slaves and the wardens of the gates of His Gardens. And among them are those whose feet stand firmly on the lowest worlds, whose necks surpass the highest heaven, whose sides exceed all bounds, and whose shoulders are proportioned to the pillars of the Throne — their eyes cast down before it, enwrapping themselves beneath it in their wings. Between them and all others there is a barrier: veils of Glory and screens of Might. They do not imagine their Lord through formal representation, nor ascribe to Him created attributes. They do not confine Him within places, nor allude to Him through similitudes.

Then He, glorified be He, gathered up a quantity of dust — from some hard earth and some soft, some sweet and some salty — and poured water onto it until it was pure, then kneaded into it moisture until it held together. From this He fashioned a form with curves and joints, limbs and parts, compacting it until it held fast, solidifying it until it became as sounding clay, and assigning to it a measured duration and a known end.
